### Benchmark completion oracle measures processor entries, not durable terminal transitions

Completed elixir/guides/benchmarks.md:1–417. Provided scripts increment an in-memory counter inside the processor before its :ok return (108–111,166–169,223–227). The wait stops once counter>=job_count, without distinct-job IDs or checking datastore completed count; duplicate attempts can inflate it and terminal mutations may remain pending. Elapsed time excludes enqueueing and includes startup/polling granularity. Thus published numbers are a processor-entry benchmark intention, not proof of exactly-once durable job throughput or downstream business effects. The loops have no aggregate timeout and cleanup issues KEYS/DEL; no script ran or resource was removed.

The claimed nearly-linear10worker scaling at40is qualified by its own table:10workers gives≈4x single-worker throughput and40%efficiency, rather than10x. Single-worker500row differs across tables; environments list M2Pro/Redis7.4/Elixir1.15+/OTP26+ at5–10 and broaderMacBook/Redis7.x/Elixir1.18/OTP27 at311–318, without immutable run receipts, repetitions or distribution/error bars here. No-op or10ms sleep jobs do not model CPU work, realistic external API limits, secret retrieval, detection/challenge cost or failure recovery. Default-optimal and throughput multipliers cannot be generalized to this Windows host or fleet. Transaction all-or-none prose retains the already traced partial-result/per-batch qualifications.

### Loader context is shared parser state, not a browser context or tenant boundary

Completed loaders.rst:1–464 and reread the complete Scrapy loader wrapper. It subclasses external itemloaders.ItemLoader, adds response-derived selector construction, and passes item/selector/response plus kwargs into the base. The wrapper does not implement the external processor/context/nested accumulation semantics; documentation of those features is not code-review credit for the external package. The documented context is an arbitrary dict shared among processors of a loader. It is neither a browser cookie container nor a permission boundary; do not reuse account-specific mutable context across unrelated tenants without explicit ownership. Input normalization is not proof of typed, authorized or complete output. Nested examples use Item() while adding undeclared social/email fields; with the separately reviewed base Item assignment check these need declared fields or another suitable item type rather than being accepted as verified runnable examples. No parser package or example was executed.

### Export format is not an output confinement or durability boundary

### Media storage completion is not account isolation or all-or-nothing delivery

Fully read pinned Scrapy docs/topics/media-pipeline.rst:1–1060. The pipeline uses downloader machinery, but the item waiting for media is not a distributed ownership lease. Default media requests do not inherit the originating response account/session automatically. URL-derived filenames are location keys, not content authenticity; customized basename or short digest schemes may collide across URLs or accounts. Cache/dedup identity must be scoped deliberately for authenticated media (the already-reviewed default request fingerprint excludes cookies and authorization). Freshness checks and expiry settings concern download reuse, not deletion, publisher trust, or secret retention. Default completion retains successful results and returns the item even when some media fail; an explicit rejecting policy is needed when all media are mandatory. Redirect settings, store configuration, bucket ACL defaults, and examples disabling SSL verification are configuration facts, not proof of secure deployment.

Rechecked images.py:127–237,239–279: full image persistence is awaited before thumbnail generation continues. A later thumbnail/storage failure has no rollback here, so earlier persisted output can remain (source-established failure path, not a newly executed storage test). Default conversion can replace received bytes with JPEG/white transparency flattening. The same-format/no-resize fast path returns the original response buffer at230–232, including when format preservation selects it; preserve-format alone is therefore neither a universal transformation claim nor a universal byte-for-byte archive guarantee across thumbnails and custom processing. Minimum dimensions are not a maximum decoded-pixel/resource budget. The checksum covers the first yielded stored buffer and uses the selected store algorithm; it does not authenticate the source. No media request, renderer, cloud store or upstream test was run.

## Local tooling names do not create a read-only execution boundary

Closed Scrapy commands977/shell345 and revisited cmdline48–100/185–246, response helper101–152. CLI loads project settings and command modules/entry points before selecting/printing commands; entry_point.load and constructors can execute installed code. Thus invoking help/list/settings/completion is not equivalent to this review reading source text without imports. COMMANDS_MODULE can override built-ins, and shell is a full Python interpreter with crawler/request/settings access. BOT_NAME/project aliases identify labels/modules, not enrolled operator/accounts. Core discovery uses configured project paths, not a hostile-package sandbox. No CLI or completion script was invoked.

Fetch/parse/shell/view use project/spider settings and downloader paths; their names do not guarantee no outgoing requests or no writes. Copied cURL can contain account cookies/auth headers/body and generated code/clipboard can expose them. Shell unprefixed index.html is treated as a hostname, so a supposed local file can produce network lookup. no-redirect on shell initial fetch does not set a global subsequent fetch policy. parse can explicitly enable pipelines/output; contracts and parse have different side-effect scopes. Settings inspection without selected spider may not reflect actual crawler settings, and reactor choice occurs before per-spider settings. Version output proves an environment string, not dependency integrity.

view/open_in_browser creates a persistent temporary file containing response bytes and inserts a base URL for HTML before opening the browser. Rendering it can fetch external assets or execute page content, so it is not an inert offline screenshot or the same session/device identity as the crawler. The browser uses its own profile/cookies/network context; no automatic account continuity follows. Historical shell transcript fields/cookies are sample output, not current site behavior or credentials used by this work. inspect_response blocks engine progress and cannot use fetch while blocked, changing timing. No browser, renderer, clipboard, temp response file, shell, profile or external URL touched.

## Transactional-jobs proposal: bounded database claim, not implemented exactly-once fleet

Closed Elixir transactional-jobs-design1–112. It labels itself Proposal/feasibility and names required future backend/worker/API changes. Therefore its exactly-once/no-idempotence language is not an implemented capability receipt. The intended coupling covers writes using the same PostgreSQL transaction/connection plus job completion; it cannot atomically undo an external HTTP call, sent message, separate database commit, filesystem write or other non-enlisted effect. Ecto adoption, failure persistence, next-job claim and connection starvation remain explicitly open design concerns.

Revisited shared migration0002_functions.sql328–400: move_to_completed obtains parent advisory and job row locks, raises BM001 for missing/nonactive/mismatched-token and unresolved dependencies, then clears lock metadata on completion. It selects state/token but not locked_until_ms in this guard. Lease time having elapsed does not itself establish token mismatch before a reclaimer changes state/token; the proposal claim that a worker whose lease expired can never commit is stronger than this code. The special token0 also bypasses token equality, so any future transactional API must constrain that path. This does not prove a current transactional feature is exploitable: none was established or run.

The proposal claim that combining claim+processing necessarily permits two workers because uncommitted active state is invisible is not a general PostgreSQL theorem: retained row locks/SKIP LOCKED selection and isolation matter (prior claim SQL trace remains authoritative). Likewise app transaction lifetime, error rollback and ambiguous commit recovery need explicit oracles before promising never-double-write. Preserve the useful atomic shared-database design possibility while recording exactly which effects/guards and implementation are actually present. No Elixir package, database transaction, row lock, claim or side effect executed; no new repository added.

Missing-lock terminal errors are fencing symptoms, not proof external work never occurred. No queue/metrics reset or runtime test.

## Diagnostic authority and benchmark scope

Closed Scrapy debug265/benchmarking87/leaks284; revisited bench command79, private bench server57 and trackref91. Debugging tools can inspect or modify the crawler process, and heap/reference access can expose request URLs, responses, cookies/account metadata and referenced objects. Telnet/MCP authority remains the previously traced runtime-control boundary. TLS session key logging deliberately creates decryption material; capture files and key files are sensitive data, not ordinary anonymized performance evidence. Proxy interception changes connection topology and can change behavior, as the guide itself explains; a proxy-assisted result does not establish direct-handler fingerprint/timing behavior. No capture, key logging or proxy was enabled.

Benchmark docs show historical2016/Scrapy1.2.2 sample output. Its roughly3000pages/min is that illustrative no-op link-following workload, not our measured capacity, authenticated/browser workload, whole fleet scaling or detection accuracy. Actual bench command launches a subprocess, waits on its stdout without a startup deadline, then kills/waits it on normal context exit. Server listenTCP(0,Site(root)) specifies random port but no explicit loopback interface; caller localhost URL does not itself prove loopback-only binding. Thus even a local benchmark label is insufficient for the isolated-lab boundary without inspecting binding and process cleanup. It was not run.

trackref uses process-global weak-key maps for opted-in object classes, not a census of all Python/native allocations or per-tenant memory. Large queued references can be legitimate retained work, and observed RSS is not identical to live tracked-object bytes. Passing responses through request metadata extends their lifetime but is not proof of an uncollectable leak. Multiple crawlers share memory/resource impact; disk queues alter retention/durability tradeoffs rather than impose a hard RSS cap or automatically clean session secrets. Third-party profiling packages were neither obtained nor imported.

## Observability denominators and missing-data states

Closed Scrapy logging411/stats795. Stats reference explicitly counts cached requests/responses and redirects at different middleware/engine stages; request/response bytes are reconstructed and exclude wire overhead, while compressed/decompressed counters have different denominators. They are not independent packet counts, distinct identities, bot detections, blocked attackers, or directly comparable provider throughput. Retries, cache recovery, pipeline drop/error, exported entries and storage successes need separate stage labels. Neither INFO/ERROR severity nor finish_reason finished proves all items were valid or all feeds stored.

Stats guide138–142 calls missing stats equivalent to0, but only makes sense for known enabled/reached collection paths; disabled middleware, DummyStatsCollector, custom collectors, lost process or missing export must stay unknown/not collected for evaluation. StatsCollector35–181 confirms mutable in-memory dictionary, get_stats returns its actual object, callers can replace/reset it, MemoryStatsCollector retains last run by spider name in the instance, Dummy mutations no-op. No fleet durability, signed audit, per-account authority or cross-process aggregation is provided by this API. DownloaderStats36–79 counts at middleware hooks and can be NotConfigured, matching that limitation.

Logging uses shared named/root handlers, with optional file overwrite and no automatic rotation; setting LOG_ENABLED false controls Scrapy-installed output, not a proof every existing application handler is silent. Later changes to a named logger can affect other crawlers sharing that process. Spider name/reference helps attribution but is not an authenticated worker identity. ContentFilter example333–337 only returns false on regex match and falls through to None otherwise, so it has no true accept path; record.message also is not reliably preformatted at filter time. This is a documentation example defect, not a locally executed logging test. A redaction/filter policy needs a positive non-sensitive-record oracle and checks for credentials in URLs/item repr/headers, rather than assuming less visible output means safe retained evidence. No logs were shipped or configured.

## Output policy needs enforcement before every side effect

Closed Scrapy item-pipeline419/feed-exports968. Pipeline process_item order is sequential per item; open/close hooks are concurrent (ItemPipelineManager57–159). A later DropItem cannot undo an earlier database/file write. The illustrative JSON writer uses write mode and shared fixed filename; multiple workers/crawlers need explicit output ownership. In-memory ids_seen is per instance, unbounded and lost on restart, not distributed item deduplication or tenant scope. The synchronous Mongo example can block the event loop and supplies no exactly-once/transaction guarantee. Screenshot example passes item URL to a local rendering service: escaping it into a query is not a destination authorization policy inside that service, and rendering credentials/session/egress require separate ownership. No renderer or database was contacted.

Scraper521–585 emits item_scraped after successful pipeline processing, but catches/logs drop/error cases separately; that signal does not establish remote feed durability. Feed export965–1017 filters, reshapes and exports under a local lock. Per-feed item processors can expand/drop entries after original pipeline validation; if used for security-sensitive transformation, validate resulting entries too. Class filters default allow-all and fields are formatting, not tenant access control. Item count can include skipped entries for batch alignment, and close-slot909–926 increments item_count before storage attempt; success/failed/conflict counters must remain distinct. A feed_slot_closed signal follows caught store failure as well as success.

Documented create semantics vary: local exclusive file creation differs from FTP existence-check/write best effort; S3/GCS depend on their actual conditional APIs. Delayed delivery uses temporary local files, not zero-local-retention streaming. Batches can accumulate asynchronous pending stores; item-count batch size is not byte/RSS/upload concurrency limit. Resume with JOBDIR does not automatically append/recover an existing feed, and create-conflict intentionally loses that file output while logging/counting it. JSON append can invalidate a single-array format even when bytes append successfully. FTP is cleartext; FTPS certificate verification is a backend-specific claim, not inherited by all handler/storage schemes. URI parameters from spider attributes and errors with full item repr can expose sensitive context; separate vault references/permissions/redaction are application policy. Test oracles revisited: test_feedexport_reporting.py47–83 awaits crawl completion even while a mocked store raises, then expects failed_count1; test_feedexport_mode.py230–257 expects later-batch conflict to preserve old bytes, successful surrounding batches, conflict1/failed1/success2. These assertions directly separate crawl return from all-output success, but remain unexecuted here. Existing storage/main-source walkthrough preserved, no files exported or cloud configuration changed.

## Coroutine configuration does not unify every request or cancellation path

Closed Scrapy coroutine323/asyncio362/exception index28. The exception page is generated API references, not a newly reviewed exception implementation; actual class/handler paths remain in the preserved main walkthrough. Coroutine support is callable-specific: start and spider output require async generators, only selected signals await results, and downloader/scheduler/dupefilter/feed integrations have distinct adapters. versionadded VERSION placeholders are unreleased-source documentation, not a reliable released-version claim.

The coroutine examples include direct treq/aiohttp client calls outside the Scrapy request engine and inline engine.download_async calls; neither should be presented as equivalent to scheduler admission. Revisited engine649–716/downloader218–332: inline calls refuse drained/closing state and retain downloader middleware/per-slot queueing, while skipping scheduler duplicate admission and its total-concurrency backout check. This corroborates the earlier direct-path walkthrough, not a new runtime bypass demonstration. Direct third-party clients require their own shared rate/identity/timeout/cleanup policy. gather creates concurrent work; it is not a fleet-wide semaphore.

The reactor is process-global and module imports can install it before configuration. Reactorless mode is explicitly experimental, changes HTTP/FTP handlers and disables Telnet by default; behavior/setting parity must be checked for the selected handler, not assumed from an unchanged spider. Windows Selector/Proactor constraints differ by mode, so no host loop was changed to accommodate packages. inthread delegates through run_in_thread to asyncio.to_thread or Twisted deferToThread (decorators82–94; previously reviewed asyncio361–378); cancellation of an await is not proof the blocking function or external effect stopped. No upstream import, thread, event loop or request was started.

## CI configuration: selected gates, not executed assurance

Closed BullMQ OSV PR149/scheduled24/config3, CodeQL71/advanced103, Glide66, .NET130, Rust127 and Elixir183. OSV PR scans old/new code with continue-on-error, then reporter requests fail-on-vuln; this is not proof scanner failure is ignored overall, because reporter/output upload can fail. The initial checkout config is preserved for both comparisons, so submitted config changes can affect the comparison policy. Current pinned config contains comments only. External scanner/reporter action implementations and advisory database state are not reviewed here. Scheduled reusable workflow uses a version tag and fail-on-vuln only for schedule, not push; branch protection/required-check policy and actual runs were not queried. Artifact retention is5days, not an immutable research receipt.

Two CodeQL workflows coexist: one JavaScript-only, the other actions/JavaScript-TypeScript/Python with build-mode none. Neither configured matrix by itself covers all Lua, SQL, Rust, Elixir, PHP and .NET implementations. Some action references are commit-pinned and others tag/branch based; Ubuntu latest, Node lts and Rust stable are moving inputs, not our pinned runtime. No security result, measured sensitivity or false-positive rate is inferred from configuration.

Runtime test jobs have input-path gates and selected environments: Glide separately installs a2.5.0 adapter; .NET uses .NET8/Redis7/Postgres18; Elixir1.17/OTP27 uses Redis7 and a separate Postgres16 job; Rust stable uses Redis8 and a three-master cluster without replicas, not failover resilience. Rust input filter lacks shared SQL/migration paths present in .NET/Elixir filters; this is a configured reachability difference, not evidence a specific Rust SQL defect exists. A green skip is not a test execution. Build/test commands install dependencies and open services; none were run on this PC. As Rowan relayed from Linden, even broad upstream Go tests can execute HTTP examples, so offline assumptions require exact harness/import/example review, not a test command label.

## Job selection, payload and observation boundaries

Closed seven jobs guides: delayed47/FIFO45/LIFO17/prioritized82/getters107/data154/README10. FIFO is a collection-selection policy, not a guarantee of completion or external-effect order under concurrent workers, asynchronous scheduler acquisition, retries or mixed queue types. LIFO and priority can defer older work; without an aging/fairness policy there is no bounded wait guarantee. Priority0/no-priority is selected before the positive-priority set; a producer-controlled priority label needs application authorization and quotas. No priority policy proves tenant fairness or account isolation.

Revisited fetchNextJob.lua30–95 and promoteDelayedJobs.lua1–45: delayed promotion selects at most1000 at a time, then rate/pause/concurrency checks still gate acquisition; ordinary wait list is tried before prioritized fallback. Promotion is eligibility, not processor execution, and no exact wall-clock SLA follows. changeDelay-4.lua1–55 requires job existence and delayed-set membership and recomputes the score from current timestamp; this is a state mutation, not a lease extension or hard execution deadline. The delayed example non-ISO date string is ambiguous across environments and does not establish its narrated July3 timezone.

Getters describe bounded inclusive index pages; returned counts/pages are time-varying observations, not an atomic whole-fleet snapshot, proof all effects are done, or a revocation oracle. Repeat metadata is not the same thing as an ordinary job, as the historical/scheduler review distinguishes. updateData replaces a JSON value and does not refresh already-loaded copies in every worker, validate tenant authority, or perform compare-and-swap. JSON serialization loses prototypes/functions and may throw on unsupported values; re-instantiating a class is not input validation. The sample token is ordinary persisted payload, not a vault reference or authenticated workload credential. These guides add no automatic per-account secret/session isolation to shared workers. No upstream test or endpoint was run.

## Versioned control-plane migration is separate from workload identity

Closed migration v5-to-v6 124, general61, Bull-to-BullMQ19, historical repeatable257 and QueueScheduler33. The latter two explicitly fence their examples as historical: do not recommend removed legacy APIs or a separate pre-v2 scheduler as requirements for the pinned v6 Worker. Migration advice to enumerate/recreate/remove old definitions while still on v5 changes persistent data; this review did none of it. Recreating an immediate scheduler before retiring the old one can overlap effects unless application fencing/idempotency and rollout sequencing handle it. Similar repeat fields alone do not establish identical first-run/offset behavior. Scheduler/queue IDs identify scheduling records, not authenticated operators.

The general pause/upgrade/unpause prose says wait for all current queued jobs to process after global pause. Pause prevents new acquisition; the actionable wait can concern already active jobs, while waiting/delayed jobs remain for post-upgrade compatibility. An empty active set is not an empty queue or proof prior effects succeeded. Patch/minor SemVer advice is an author compatibility expectation, not a measured guarantee for a mixed-version fleet. New queue names/prefixes separate stored jobs but do not create Redis ACL or service-account isolation.

Crosschecked Queue.addJob 370–395 and addBulk407–455, Job validation1370–1410, JobScheduler legacy-key classifier26–97 and earlier Worker977–1042 metadata probe. Removal of TypeScript options and explicit legacy metadata errors must be distinguished from universal runtime validation of arbitrary JavaScript payloads. These entry points show explicit debounce rejection; the shown validation does not by itself prove every removed option is rejected on all paths. Legacy key-shape detection alone is deliberately insufficient because scheduler IDs can resemble old keys; the ic metadata probe disambiguates. Versioned reader guidance must retain this distinction, and no live migration, deployment, destructive cleanup or compatibility test was executed.

## Bulk insertion: do not infer all-or-none from batching

Closed batches guide 1–165 and adding-bulks 1–61. Batches correctly distinguishes independent bulk jobs, flow trees, one payload with shared retry, and proprietary multi-job callbacks. Its one-payload loop sends each invoice before updateProgress; retry after an intermediate failure can repeat prior sends. Progress is observation, not durable per-item idempotency or a resume cursor. No invoice was sent.

The adding-bulks guide 3/56 promises all jobs or none. Job.createBulk 292–328 first serializes/validates all entries, then calls backend.addJobs; this protects against some client validation errors before submission. RedisQueueBackend.addJobs 703–734, however, selects client.pipeline(), submits separate scripts, executes the pipeline, then scans [error,id] tuples. It throws on a tuple error after submission without rollback and does not map negative numeric script results in that scan. addJobToTransaction 640–691 has the negative-code conversion for a direct awaited result, but a pipeline runCommand returns the chain object when enqueued (ioredis-client 357–362), before the later script result exists. addStandardJob-9.lua 74–75 can return -5 for an absent parent. Thus a mixed successful insert plus missing-parent result is a precise untested candidate for partial insertion and an invalid returned ID rather than the advertised universal all-or-none behavior. Client validation is not server-state prevalidation.

Adapter distinction matters: ioredis-client 86–92 preserves pipeline versus multi; Bun pipeline explicitly transactional=false (753–755), and Bun exec 1403–1419 independently sends all commands and reports settled results. The Bun comment that writing a burst prevents interleaving is not a Redis server transaction guarantee. node-redis pipeline instead delegates to multi (508–512), so do not assert identical isolation across adapters. Existing bulk.test.ts 56–142 exercises successful jobs and existing-parent insertion, not the missing-parent/partial-error oracle. This is source analysis, not a reproduced runtime defect. Flow insertion separately uses multi and needs its own error semantics; no broad rollback claim follows from the word atomic.

## Event receipt is not durable application acknowledgement

Closed event guide 1–156, custom-event guide 1–45, returned-data guide 1–37; traced QueueEvents.consumeEvents 371–418, QueueEventsProducer 49–65, Redis backend 2763–2777/2860–2925 and QueueBase.emit 154–165. QueueEvents starts with supplied lastEventId or dollar (new events), then advances an in-memory cursor before decoding and synchronous emission. There is no consumer-group acknowledgement, persisted application cursor, or await of asynchronous listener success on this path. A new process without a persisted cursor does not replay its predecessor backlog. Approximate stream trimming and sufficiently long disconnections bound replay; docs 54 delivery guarantee must not be read as indefinite lossless storage. Explicit trimEvents also uses approximate=true, so the sample comment leaves 10 events is not an exact count guarantee.

Custom publication defaults maxEvents=1000 on the same queue event stream, differing from the general lifecycle-stream approximate 10000 default in the guide. Producer constructs event from eventName followed by the remaining payload fields, with no runtime reserved-name or shape validation here. A payload event field can override the constructed name. Consumers parse completed returnvalue and progress data as JSON before emission; trusted-writer permission and schema validation matter, including to prevent custom writers from presenting lifecycle-looking messages. This is an application boundary observation, not an externally tested exploit.

The custom-event test (events.redis.test.ts 627–669) deliberately starts with 0-0 and autorun=false, publishes before run, and checks one asynchronous listener result. It does not test restart acknowledgement, trimming loss, malformed records, reserved-name rejection, or adversarial publishers. The return-data guide correctly warns storing only in a completed listener can fail after the job completes. A results queue still requires atomic outbox/idempotency and recovery for the processor-to-results publication gap; prose alone does not establish exactly-once database effects. No upstream execution or external delivery.

## Cleanup guide crosscheck against the preserved enforcement trace

Closed queue-removal guide 1–336, individual removal 1–78, and pause guide 1–27. Queue-removal opening says waiting or delayed, but Queue.drain defaults delayed=false (queue.ts 764), with explicit true required; even true preserves scheduler slot IDs by design. Revisited drain-5.lua 1–42, list/zset/filter/remove helpers, and job_scheduler.test.ts 2641–2666: the scheduler survives the test while an ordinary delayed job is removed. This does not resolve the previously recorded mixed-list filtering/LTRIM concern; the fixture has only the scheduler in waiting, so it cannot distinguish that ordering case.

The pause guide correctly says active jobs continue and pause(true) only skips waiting for their completion. Queue.obliterate 844–872 first pauses, then loops bounded backend calls; obliterate-2.lua 35–53 rejects active records unless forced, while force deletes locks and job data without an abort signal to the processor. Reviewed test assertions in obliterate.test.ts 51–103 cover active guard timing and empty logs after a completed job, not stopping in-flight effects. This corroborates the existing cleanup source walkthrough; no new runtime receipt or destructive operation. Individual removal explicitly differs by language (TypeScript/Python error versus Rust false for locked work); those API differences must survive a fleet-control wrapper rather than be flattened into a universal success boolean.

## Scheduler eligibility is not execution or revocation

Read the four BullMQ guide/job-schedulers files completely: README 1–59, management 1–45, repeat options 1–110, strategies 1–169. README 20 says the first every job is delayed one interval, whereas repeat-options 108 says every new scheduler always starts immediately. Neither is a complete statement for this pin. job-scheduler.ts 134–204 validates combinations and checks generation count/end date; addJobScheduler-11.lua 77–93 and getJobSchedulerEveryNextMillis.lua 3–47 choose now without a positive offset/future start date, a future start date when supplied, or the next offset-aligned slot. Changing every clears previous millis and may reset eligibility to now (add script 81–88). The test source asserts waiting/delay=0 after an interval change (job_scheduler.test.ts 503–589) and future offset alignment (1591–1621); these are read assertions, not locally passing tests. every is milliseconds despite repeat-strategies 3 saying seconds. Eligibility still requires an available, unpaused, permitted worker.

Worker.ts 977–1042 produces the next iteration while acquiring the current job, before its active event and processor. updateJobScheduler-12.lua 47–114 checks scheduler existence, producer job id against the stored current slot, and next-job existence before atomically updating the slot and enqueueing. This is queue deduplication and progress coordination, not account authorization or exactly-once external effects. The helper skips missed intervals rather than replaying every overdue slot. A generation limit/end date is not an active-work deadline or downstream request budget. Queue and Worker must share the custom strategy, as the docs explicitly warn; parser/timezone/DST behavior still depends on the selected dependency and is not measured here.

removeJobScheduler-3.lua 27–43 removes the next job only when found in the delayed set, then removes scheduler metadata. It has no active-worker abort, external side-effect rollback, or blanket waiting/priority cleanup. The update script refuses further generation when metadata is absent, but callers still need a separate cancellation/revocation and cleanup policy. Source-only follow-up; no Redis or other integration executed.

## Trace continuity and telemetry labels are not authenticated attribution

Closed BullMQ telemetry README14/start47/metrics258/traces268. ## Step checkpoints leave crash windows and examples contain control-flow traps

Closed remaining7 BullMQ pattern docs: bulk33/fail-fast29/named31/cluster26/throttle40/timeout54/step585. Doing an external step then updateData leaves an effect→checkpoint gap; retry can repeat the effect, contrary to unqualified correct-step prose171. Dynamic child/flow creation followed by separate checkpoint can duplicate child work on crash unless application IDs/outbox/idempotency supply a boundary. ## Observable cancellation and batch affinity need implementation evidence

Setting group.concurrency=batch.size is described as serializing batches, but same page permits partial batches: partial occupancy alone would not mathematically prevent another partial batch without an additional implementation guard. ## Retention, lease tokens and sandbox TTL have narrower guarantees

Closed BullMQ job-IDs40, queue-retention66, sandbox-processors66, manual-fetch120 and sandbox-timeout67. Job IDs are queue-scoped storage identifiers, not worker/account principals; removal permits reusing the same ID, so ID dedup is retention-dependent rather than permanent idempotency. Age/count auto-removal is lazy on later terminal jobs, not an expiration timer or guaranteed deletion deadline. Retention can span job names and removes some evidence needed for retries/audit.

Manual fetch requires caller-managed completion/failure, lease renewal and stalled checks; missing processor is not a fully managed worker loop. Illustrative loop reuses constant token and lacks stop/backoff/error/cleanup handling, so it is not an enrollment/ownership-fencing design. Previously reviewed tests show deferred-failure manual jobs can be returned for caller terminal handling. Token is queue lease ownership, not scoped workload identity or fence accepted by external effects.

Sandbox docs describe process/thread isolation for scheduling pressure; pooled children are reused (also acknowledged by timeout finally comment44). It is not a fresh identity/secret environment for every job. Timeout pattern runs both timers inside the same event loop it admits may hang, so it is not a hard CPU deadline. Soft cleanup timer fires5seconds after start, not5seconds before30second hard timer; it does not stop work and can race it. process.exit skips normal finally cleanup. Claims of no better robust method are author prose, not established impossibility. No child process, thread, lease, deletion or timeout example was run.

## Deduplication is suppression policy, not exactly-once side effects

Closed BullMQ job-dedup197, retry-failing213, manual job-retry290, manual-retrying27 and stop-retrying66. Simple suppression ends at terminal state/removal; TTL suppression can expire while work remains active, and TTL itself is not a job execution delay unless delay is separately configured. Debounce replaces stored work/data; keepLastIfActive keeps only latest pending data, so intermediate updates are intentionally lost rather than all executed. Its no-parallel-job guarantee concerns backend job state, not stale processors continuing external effects after lease loss. Retried active work postpones pending-next creation until terminal resolution. Queue-level removal and conditional job-level removal are different ownership semantics. Hashing job data supplies equality, not account authorization; tenant namespace must be deliberate.

Automatic retry defaults no backoff; attempts3 means initial plus2 retries, despite exponential example77 listing3delayvalues. Attempt counters are mutable operational state, not immutable audit or lifetime action budget. Manual retry may reset counters/results/timestamps and restore parent dependency, without undoing completed external effects. Job-retry warning217 says exhausted attempts immediately fail when processed, but targeted worker.ts1157–1169 reread checks deferredFailure and maxStartedAttempts, not attemptsMade before processing; prior complete failure-caller review places automatic retry decision after failure. Thus that warning must not be treated as a universal pre-processor guard. Manual moveToWait requires lock token and WaitingError to avoid second terminal handling; special rate/delay/wait cases differ from ordinary failures and may need a separate started-attempt budget. UnrecoverableError prevents automatic retries, not authorized manual requeue forever. No job or retry implementation was executed.

Closed BullMQ parallelism40, worker-concurrency41, global-concurrency34, global-rate42, rate-limiting136 and deduplication-pattern70. A job may issue many downstream calls, so job-start limits are not automatically HTTP request quotas. Lowering concurrency does not cancel already acquired work, consistent with prior tests. Queue APIs can remove/reset limits, so authorization for those APIs/backend credentials is independent of limiter arithmetic. ## Abort notification is not proof that side effects have stopped

Closed BullMQ cancelling-jobs1–405. cancelJob/cancelAllJobs signal locally tracked active processors; their return/notification is not cluster-wide revocation or proof cleanup finished. Processors that ignore signal continue. Event listeners do not preempt blocked JS and must account for already-aborted signals before registration. Rejecting an outer promise alone does not cancel awaited download/process/upload functions; the multiphase example does not pass the signal into those operations. Several recommended examples use new Promise(async executor). Cleanup example181–210 similarly throws from async executor catch instead of rejecting outer promise, omits normal-success close, and allows abort cleanup to race ongoing process/cache writes. Async abort listener return is not automatically awaited by AbortController. These are source-reading defects in illustrative patterns, not executed reproductions; no guarantee of no wasted work/no data loss follows from the prose.

Regular cancellation Error can retry, while UnrecoverableError changes retry handling only when ownership permits terminal transition. Lost-lock section correctly says failure transition cannot succeed without ownership and stalled recovery may requeue; claims about no-data-loss/attempts must still respect maxStalledCount, persistence, external-effect idempotency and stale owners. Cancellation reason is diagnostic caller data, not authenticated requester attribution. Prior worker/lock/backend source and synthetic receipts remain distinct evidence. ## Queue connection resilience is distinct from delivery and authorization

Closed BullMQ connections1–318, production1–141, stalled-job40, graceful-shutdown18, worker-stalled17 and idempotent-job12. Custom clientFactory examples map only selected options; the Bun example constructs host/port URL without forwarding auth/TLS/database, so it is not secure-option-preserving production configuration by itself. Redis persistence must be independently configured; AOF interval is not zero-loss acknowledgement or atomic external effects. noeviction prevents arbitrary key removal but cannot by itself guarantee correct queue behavior under all failures. Production prose says invalid worker maxRetriesPerRequest produces warning, while connection guide says exception; prior actual RedisConnection source establishes enforcement rather than choosing reassuring prose. Retry indefinitely is a liveness strategy, not a promise of eventual success, bounded shutdown or per-request deadline. Graceful close explicitly has no own timeout and recovery may reprocess jobs; applications need idempotent effects and external fencing. Closing before restart reduces stalls but does not guarantee none.

Worker-stalled doc incorrectly conflates notification period with stalledInterval; source already distinguishes lock renewal timing from stalled scanning. Lock text saying protected from any other client is bounded by token-checked engine operations and trusted backend access; it does not lock external services against stale effects. Sandboxed processors reduce parent event-loop pressure but are not an untrusted-code security sandbox. Job data is clear text in datastore; sensitive accounts/secrets should not be put into job payloads. Idempotence document is an application obligation, not implemented deduplication of arbitrary database/API effects. Error logging/uncaughtException handlers do not establish safe recovery or unchanged process invariants. No Redis connection, process signal, production setting or upstream test was used.

## Effective settings require precedence, lifecycle and implementation checks

Closed Scrapy settings1–2692 in contiguous160-line ranges through2560 then2561–2692. CLI settings outrank spider/project/addon/default values; generated-project defaults differ from bare framework fallbacks (domain concurrency1vs8, delay1vs0, robots obeytruevsfalse). Thus an isolated default constant is insufficient to describe an actual crawler. Pre-crawler settings cannot generally be assigned per spider; first-reactor/logging settings are process-wide, and later incompatible settings raise or warn according to lifecycle. AsyncCrawlerProcess installs its specific reactor ignoring an alternative requested reactor setting. Python import/config is executable trusted code, not an authorization schema.

Settings must be consumed by enabled components/handlers to take effect. Response-exception handling defaultsfalse; enabling it can turn intentional drops into new requests if a broad exception middleware retries everything. DOWNLOAD_DELAY prose calls it a minimum, but default jitter is±50%, so it is not a strict lower interval or fixed-window quota. Global concurrency0 means unlimited. CONCURRENT_ITEMS is per response, not whole-process cap; AWS connection-pool size is reuse capacity, explicitly not a bound on concurrent calls. DNS timeout includes time queued for the shared thread pool and only applies to one resolver. Alternate clients may ignore DNS/TLS/frame/size settings. HTTP2 size checks occur per received frame, allowing overshoot before rejection; warning sizes only log.

MEMUSAGE extension enablement does not imply a cap: limit defaults0 and periodic60s checks are not immediate process hard limits or preemption of blocking code. Scraper active-size limit is explicitly soft, not resident memory enforcement. JOBDIR_SYNC_EVERY defaults0; setting1 covers scheduler active-state/DiskDupeFilter changes but requires SQLite queues for killed-process resume and still does not atomically encompass effects/spider state. Start requests have separateFIFOqueues from defaultLIFOwork; disabling them changes scheduling order after concurrency is filled. Sticky metadata preserves selected keys without overwriting explicit child values; it is propagation, not validation of tenant authority.

Logging is process-wide, may overwrite prior logs when configured, and can redirect stdout/stderr; logging enablement does not provide immutable audit retention or redaction. Upload timeoutNone delegates to client-specific defaults, including indefinite FTP wait in this documentation; cancellation must cover storage as well as downloads. No config, storage, thread pool, connection or upstream test was changed or executed.

## Middleware warnings and response filtering are not isolation

Closed Scrapy spider-middleware1–499. Spider-input exceptions may reach request errbacks, while callback/output-iteration exceptions route through spider-exception middleware; recovered iterables resume at later output middleware. Generator body execution is lazy, so code before iteration is not evidence the callback body already ran. A middleware can drop, expand or replace output, and exception recovery can introduce new work; cleanup/cancellation reasoning needs the complete chain already traced in core source. MetaCopyDetection emits one warning per crawl and permits skipped keys: it is observability, not a mechanism preventing copied credentials/routing state. HTTP status filtering is crawler response handling, not rejection of a hostile client at a protected endpoint.

Default referrer policy explicitly sends nonempty cross-domain referrers to HTTPS destinations, with separate per-request and response-header policy overrides. Legacy claims about browser defaults are not verified current browser behavior here. Referrer policy is a URL-disclosure control and is independent of cookie jars, HTTP auth scope and network identities. Depth/URL-length/start metadata are crawl scheduling conventions, not workload authorization. No middleware or upstream test was executed.

## Handler replacement changes the enforcement contract

Closed Scrapy download-handlers1–586. Scheme maps and named per-request handler selection are separate; configured HTTP/HTTPS entries must both be considered, and named handlers persist across redirects/retries and are inherited by robots fetches. Documentation explicitly warns alternatives may not honor byte limits or stop-download signals, so a setting on the crawler is not proof a plugin enforces it. Built-in handlers differ in TLS implementation, certificate representation, proxy support, DNS configuration, per-request bind address, file-backed body support and header parsing. They are not interchangeable transport security profiles. In particular, HTTPx ignores Twisted resolver/cache/timeout settings, and HTTP2 supports only HTTPS with no HTTP1 fallback/proxy/body_file in this implementation. A local bind address is interface selection, not guaranteed public egress identity across NAT/gateways.

Header-case preservation applies only to HTTP1; HTTP2 requires lowercase. Parser differences mean observation changes do not prove a different device/operator. HTTPx per-proxy connection pools can multiply local resource use; no proxy rotation was configured or exercised. Lazy instantiation defers dependency availability errors until first use rather than certifying readiness at crawler construction. Experimental handler warnings and passive-mode fallback logged as error demonstrate that accepted config keys do not imply requested behavior. ## Request provenance, callback failure and partial-body semantics

Closed Scrapy request-response1–1557 in contiguous140-line ranges through1400 then1401–1557. Request IDs/parent IDs and fingerprints are application bookkeeping, not authenticated principal/device identity. Fingerprinting customization can include selected headers but arbitrary caller-provided fingerprint metadata is not an integrity check. Default dedup and cache share identity policy, so account-sensitive resources must define equality deliberately. URL normalization/dedup examples assume backend equivalence; no universal equivalence follows for repeated queries or fragments.

copy/replace shallow-copy meta/cb_kwargs, leaving nested mutable data shared in memory; disk persistence instead serializes copies. Blindly propagating meta can carry credentials, cookiejar choice, retry counters, routing and opt-out flags. Callback exceptions do not invoke the request errback; they use spider middleware/error signals, so one errback is not a universal failure/cleanup hook. NO_CALLBACK is a routing convention, not execution isolation. A response status or callback completion does not establish full-body receipt: StopDownload(fail=False) intentionally yields partial successful responses. Explicit body_file targets can retain partial failed downloads, retries/redirects rewrite from the start and successful temporary-file cleanup belongs to the caller. File-backed downloads skip HTTP cache and need handler support; it is not transparent bounded parsing of any response.

Per-request auth without explicit domain is origin-scoped, while explicit domain settings use their separate rules. Response.request may reflect redirect-created requests and differ from response URL; meta/cb_kwargs propagate, so audit attribution needs actual redirect provenance rather than assuming original URL equals final target. Response IP is server address and certificate is handler-dependent metadata, not proof of verification or operator identity. HTML base elements can affect URL joining; host policy still must validate final destinations. Body decoding/lazy selectors are distinct from browser execution. External form helper and illustrative login/HTTP examples were read, not installed or contacted; no account action occurred.

## Cache namespaces and credential middleware need explicit account boundaries

Closed Scrapy downloader-middleware1–1362, in contiguous150-line ranges through1200 then1201–1362. Middleware order matters: request hooks ascend, response hooks descend, returned responses/requests short-circuit particular paths; an installed hook is not proof it ran before every sensitive operation. Direct engine downloads re-enter middleware, so token helpers can deadlock or stampede; sample caches a result but not the in-flight task and documents concurrent duplicate token fetches. Sample OAuth middleware uses one client from crawler settings and signs URL/method; this does not model scoped per-account OAuth assignment or body-signature completeness. Basic-auth domain scope includes subdomains; None explicitly broadens credential recipients.

Cache is disabled by default; when enabled, default DummyPolicy ignores HTTP cache-control and defaults to no expiry. RFC2616 policy explicitly lacks Vary and update/delete invalidation. Reinspection utils/request.py36–119/127–151 and extensions/httpcache.py380–412 confirms default fingerprint excludes Cookie/Authorization and cookiejar/account metadata, using method/canonical URL/body plus selected handler metadata. Cache scope is spider name/domain/global, not account. Therefore separate cookie jars within one spider do not separate authenticated cache entries for otherwise identical requests; owned-account isolation needs explicit account/tenant namespacing or disabling cache for sensitive requests. This is a source-derived risk, not a reproduced leak. Raw request headers/body and pickled response data are stored locally; directory ACLs and secret handling matter. Cache timestamp proves storage time only, not fresh authorization. Retries can repeatedly consume a cached error unless statuses are excluded; retry count/priority is not a backoff duration or endpoint idempotency guarantee.

Proxy metadata precedence/handler support is implementation-dependent, not assurance of network identity or permission. Robots middleware is voluntary client behavior with per-request opt-outs and parser-dependent semantics; it is not receiver access control. Stats observe only paths that pass their middleware and do not independently prove complete traffic attribution. Redirect TTL caps a chain but does not authorize target hosts; a sample path regex is not a host allowlist. No proxies, accounts, requests or upstream tests were exercised.

## Cookie jars are application routing keys, not identities

Closed Scrapy agents1–54, cookies1–136 and developer-tools1–315. Cookie jar selection defaults to one jar per crawler and cookiejar metadata must be propagated explicitly; omission falls back to the default jar. This is a cross-account leakage risk in owned synthetic-account assignment, not proof of server-side account identity, separate device or permission. Debug-cookie mode records cookie headers, potentially session credentials.

Documentation says disabling cookies means no cookies are sent, but actual cookies.py47–49 disables the middleware; it does not install a scrubber. Reinspection77–87 shows dont_merge_cookies returns before the manually supplied Cookie header is removed. Thus these options stop managed jar behavior, not universally prevent explicit Cookie headers or arbitrary middleware from sending cookies. The Cookie-header caution also only describes the normal managed path. Existing full source credit remains unchanged; this is a targeted enforcement reread, no new runtime claim.

Browser live DOM may differ from original HTTP HTML because of JS and browser repairs; API requests and browser automation are distinct execution surfaces. Developer-tools examples include copied request headers (including proxy authorization); captured requests must be treated as credential-bearing data and not reusable public fixtures. No example credentials were used or tested. Coding-agent/MCP page explicitly permits modifying runtime state/code and calls it fragile; tool descriptions and prompts are not isolation or per-account policy enforcement. External plugin/MCP repositories are not reviewed by reading these links. No browser/site was contacted or plugin installed.

## Crawler defaults expose separate transport, local-control and persistence risks

Closed Scrapy security1–350. Pinned documentation says certificate verification defaults false, plaintext HTTP/FTP and local file/data handlers are enabled, and selector huge-tree parsing can expand memory beyond response bytes. Download byte limit is not an aggregate resident-memory cap; the prose claiming memory cannot exceed the limit should be read against its own several-times-body tree warning and concurrent-request multiplication. Scheme/host validation is separate from cookie/domain policy. Parser limits may silently return incomplete data instead of errors.

Remote-control HTTP is enabled by default, loopback/random-port/token protected, with token in a job file; document only claims owner-only file permissions on Linux/macOS, not Windows ACL enforcement. Neither loopback nor possession of the token creates a restricted evaluator: code runs with crawler process authority. HTTPAuth domain scoping and referrer policy address different leakage paths. Trusted cache/JOBDIR writes are crucial because pickle loading executes code. SQLite queues improve per-request write atomicity but do not automatically make the whole crawl, dedup file, spider state, in-flight request or output effect one atomic transaction; no exactly-once guarantee follows. Prior full source/test review and local synthetic receipts remain the actual narrower evidence, not these documentation promises. No settings, ACLs, listeners or network traffic were changed.

## Resume state and live control require separate trust boundaries

Closed Scrapy deploy1–60, scheduler1–47, jobs1–207 and Telnet1–244. JOBDIR is one job only, trusted-write storage and same-version resume; clean shutdown is required, unclean shutdown may corrupt it. Unserializable requests still execute from memory but are lost on pause, so enabling persistence does not make every scheduled item durable. Serialized mutable request metadata becomes copies, not shared state; persisted requests/cookies do not guarantee account sessions remain valid. Feed append/overwrite/create choices independently affect resumed output. Scheduler reference delegates most semantics to docstrings, which were already read with actual implementation/tests. Deployment page names external services without establishing their enrollment/authentication/availability controls.

Telnet is enabled by default with loopback host, generated password logged and full Python process authority. Password authentication does not supply transport encryption; process logs containing that password belong to the credential exposure boundary. Extending the console namespace changes convenience names, not capabilities: unrestricted Python can still import modules. Live per-slot edits are transient, future slots reuse settings, and AutoThrottle can recompute delays; a local edit is not a durable policy update or fleet kill switch. Documentation engine pause/stop examples do not establish cancellation of already running external side effects; prior engine implementation tracing remains the enforcement evidence. No console connection, deployment, crawl or network test was attempted.

## Several crawlers multiply local budgets; they do not create account isolation

Closed Scrapy practices1–606. Process helpers own reactor/event-loop/logging/shutdown setup while runners reuse an existing loop and surface crawl errors to awaiting callers. Reactor-free mode is explicitly experimental. Separate crawl calls create separate middleware/settings instances, but this is object separation, not OS/process/security isolation: logging/reactor/pre-crawler settings remain shared or constrained. Per-crawler concurrency, delay and AutoThrottle budgets multiply when crawlers run together (470–480), so fleet coordination needs a separate aggregate limiter. Distributed crawling is explicitly not built in (491–500); static URL partitioning shown in docs supplies no lease recovery, global deduplication or authenticated enrollment by itself.

The later anti-blocking section mixes transparent crawler identification with identity-disguise/egress-rotation advice. It was read for defensive architecture only; no recipe is reproduced, executed or endorsed here. Shared/dynamic network addresses and mutable client identifiers cannot establish unique devices or operators. Owned automation should identify itself, use approved workload credentials and negotiated quotas rather than infer permission from client-side changes. Live Telnet/MCP control described594–598 permits Python execution inside the crawler and therefore requires actual access control; tool availability alone is not a security boundary. No crawler or live-control endpoint was started.

## Crawler pacing is local cooperation, not receiver-side authorization

Closed Scrapy architecture1–166, AutoThrottle1–192 and contributor AGENTS1–100. Architecture describes an engine/scheduler/downloader/spider/pipeline loop within the crawler, not distributed membership, account enrollment or workload authorization. AutoThrottle is disabled by default, targets average per-slot concurrency and explicitly is not a hard concurrency limit. Domains are default slot keys, which can be customized; separate crawlers do not gain a shared fleet-wide budget from this extension. Latency ends at response headers and can include cooperative-event-loop delay, so it is not a precise server load measure. Non200 latencies cannot decrease delay; explicit request metadata can suppress adjustments, but initial slot delay still applies. These source/client pacing controls do not enforce an API quota against an uncooperative caller. Standard concurrency limits remain distinct. Contributor test-policy assertions and mock-server recommendations are expectations, not runtime receipts; pinned implementation/tests were previously read but not executed.

## Advanced cancellation test source does not prove preemption

Closed job_cancellation_advanced.test.ts1225. Tests check cooperative regular-error retry versus UnrecoverableError terminal failure, stored reason/attempts, QueueEvents transitions, one selected job among three and partial progress. 'Before any data' test sets processingStarted but never asserts it, and only asserts wasCancelled flag set by test itself; it accepts either completion or failure. Near-completion accepts either outcome with no timing assertion. High-concurrency cancels five but only asserts cancelledJobs.size>0, not all selected or exact partition. Shutdown accepts failed/active/completed, explicitly not a terminal-state guarantee. Last-attempt cancellation relies on100ms sleep rather than gated third entry.

Async cleanup examples use simulated boolean resources, not real DB/cache/files. Promise async executors retain their5000ms work branch after abort listener rejects outer promise; no assertion waits for that branch to stop. Cleanup 'correct order' asserts contains, not ordering; timeout races leave losing cleanup timer alive. External-API cancellation test is a local cooperative loop called simulateApiCall, no HTTP request or rollback. Progress assertion last<100 cannot prove cessation because loop only publishes0..90 even on normal completion. These are valuable cooperative examples but insufficient evidence of force, remote revocation, leak-free cleanup or effect fencing. No tests/services executed.

## Utilities close telemetry and IPC enforcement dependencies

Closed utils/index.ts503 and child-command12/parent-command18/metrics-time11/telemetry-attributes62. trace awaits callback inside context manager but obtains active/fromMetadata/startSpan before its try/finally; adapter errors there bypass span cleanup, while recordException/end failures can replace original callback error/result. Metadata extraction depends on callback.length exactly2, not declared security policy. withDetachedContext falls back to unchanged context when root missing, as tests distinguish. errorToJSON copies every own error property then replaces repeated object references, with no secret/redaction allowlist; observability data can include processor-supplied error details.

asyncSend resolves successfully if target exposes neither send nor postMessage; completion means send callback/return, not recipient processed/committed command. delay registers an abort listener without initial aborted check (pre-aborted behavior depends on controller event implementation), and resolves on cancellation rather than rejecting. removeAllQueueData interpolates prefix/queueName into a SCAN glob, rejects cluster use, collects unbounded pipeline promises and only quits after successful scan/operations; supplied names require trust to avoid broader matching, and cleanup failure can leave the client open. It was never called. MetricsTime.ONE_MONTH is eight weeks by arithmetic (two weeks times4), so enum label is not a calendar month. These helpers are not enrollment/account controls.

## TypeScript connection/bootstrap differs from Python migrations

Closed postgres-connection.ts502, migrator.ts374, migrations/index.ts54 and sql-loader.ts53. TypeScript defaults to read-only schema compatibility check and requires migrate:true (or inverse skipMigrations) for DDL; Python automatically migrates on readiness. Supplied pools are not closed and retain caller search_path, but connection.schema is hardcoded bullmq, so compatibility check and actual unqualified query target can diverge unless caller config aligns. Owned pools pin validated search_path and preserve other pool options. Naming errors are best-effort; keepalive is enabled/requested with a10s initial delay, not a measured failure-detection deadline. LISTEN establishment is shared, failed attempts reset memo, fatal errors invalidate subscriptions, and close awaits in-flight establishment. Returning a pooled listener uses release without explicit UNLISTEN; exact pooled-session reuse tests remain relevant.

TypeScript migration ledger adds NOT NULL min_client_version and inserts6 for each of three bundled migrations. Python's previously read migration loop inserts only version/name and performs no min-client compatibility check. On a TypeScript-created ledger, Python applying a later pending migration can violate that NOT NULL column; Python creating the schema first permits TypeScript's compatibility query to fall back to its own major when no min-client field exists. These are source cross-port compatibility boundaries, not executed failures. assertSchemaCompatibility checks minimum client major but does not require migration version equal latest, so an old initialized schema can pass then lack newer behavior. Invalid server version parse yields NaN comparisons rather than explicit malformed-value rejection, although real server reports are trusted here. TypeScript SQL is generated into sql-scripts then cached, requiring generator/distribution bytes review separately. No migration or package import executed.

## Exact SQL cursor, discovery and renewal commands

Closed supporting read_events.sql7/read_events_max.sql4/get_client_list.sql12/extend_locks.sql26/update_job_scheduler.sql6. Cursor query is strictly id>cursor ordered ascending, with no late-commit reconciliation; '$' is MAX visible ID. The out-of-order-commit cursor concern therefore survives this dependency read and needs a gated two-transaction test, not a field-loss claim. Discovery filters database plus nonempty application_name, not schema: TypeScript's schema-free client names can conflate same queue names across schemas; Python's schema-bearing labels differ. Neither label is authenticated workload identity. Bulk renewal joins ID/token arrays by ordinality and checks active/token, with no expiry check; unequal arrays silently omit unmatched IDs from both attempted updates and returned failures. Update scheduler command directly calls the previously reviewed function with no additional guards.

## TypeScript Postgres backend full closure and port differences

Closed postgres-queue-backend.ts2285. Unlike Python, promote/retryFinishedJob/changeDelay/changePriority explicitly convert negative return codes; addFlow converts negative numeric strings to numeric codes while addJobs simply returns strings. forQueue shares connection, clientName omits schema (Python includes it), and discovery labels therefore cannot distinguish same-name queues in different schemas without additional query filtering. parseNodeKey splits at last colon, so colon-bearing custom IDs require upstream validation before claiming invertible addressing.

query deliberately returns a NEVER-settling promise once connection.isClosing or when pool-end races: suppressing shutdown rejection can hang an awaited caller; it is not cancellation acknowledgement. collectMetrics comment says best-effort, but configured metric query is awaited without catch after terminal transition; a metric error can surface as finish failure after the job already committed. Python catches these errors, so port-equivalence claims are rejected. trimEvents/removeDeprecatedPriorityKey throw notImplemented rather than Python's silent no-op; publishEvent ignores maxEvents and relies on metadata SQL retention. removeOrphanedJobs returns0 specifically for absent Redis-style hash/state split, not proof that relational dependency/dedup orphans never exist.

TypeScript waitForJob resolves immediately on matching notification, unlike Python's additional claimable-only probe; it also probes waiting jobs and next-delay after listener registration and shortens its timer. This avoids the specific Python earlier-future deadline branch concern, without proving native delivery/keepalive latency. readEvents resolves '$' to current max, fetches100 rows, waits with a timeout on no rows then fetches again; missing bulk event notification can defer read until timeout. waitForEvent lacks a post-LISTEN re-fetch and post-await closing check, so first-subscription/close races remain bounded by timeout unless connection invalidation wakes them. Listener invalidation resets subscriptions/cancels waits. Cursor ordering requires exact read_events SQL below; no adapter imported/executed.

## Full SQL function migration closure

Finished0002_functions.sql1951-3474, closing all3474 lines. Scheduler upsert stores end/limit fields but these function bodies do not compare next occurrence against them; caller checks are distinct and direct SQL callers can omit them. Advancement compares producer_id to the deterministic current iteration ID but reads scheduler without FOR UPDATE and increments its count before ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING insertion; simultaneous stale callers need concurrency tests. remove_scheduler preserves waiting/active jobs, so removing a schedule is not cancellation of acquired work. Stalled recovery is mark/sweep with configured throttle, skips locked rows, and turns excessive stalls into deferred failure for later worker processing rather than directly executing rollback.

Rate functions confirm a fixed-expiry counter window, lazily treating expired rows as zero, with manual override counter and caller-supplied wall time. Dedupe finalization is conditional on winning ID for persistent keys, but requeue_dedup_next inserts ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING then still changes winner, deletes pending payload and publishes a runnable event; a colliding custom ID can therefore consume the pending payload without inserting its data. dedup_on_removal's comment promises pending cleanup while body deletes only dedup, leaving dedup_next until another path; migration0003 later clears it specifically for stale-key/delayed replacement cases. The obsolete deduplicate_job body in0002 is superseded by0003 and must not be presented as final behavior.

Failure propagation uses per-parent advisory lock and option-specific pending/ignored/failed changes; continue-on-failure can release before other children finish. remove_child_dependency deletes any dependency category then decrements pending count unconditionally, so processed/ignored removal merits a focused counter oracle. remove_unprocessed_children decrements parent counts/deletes job rows but does not explicitly delete reverse child dependency rows; schema's FK is parent-side only, making its 'cascades clean up dependency rows' comment insufficient for the removed child's own parent link.

Fused finish+fetch functions perform both operations in one statement and propagate SQL exceptions, but logical returned negative statuses elsewhere still do not roll back. Independent bulk inserts use temp tables, preserve returned input IDs including duplicates, and emit events only for newly inserted jobs. That path directly INSERTs event rows and notifies only bullmq_jobs: it does not invoke publish_event, trim events or notify bullmq_events. Event-consumer timeout/fetch behavior must be traced before claiming eventual latency or memory bounds. These observations identify specific enforcement/test paths; none is an executed vulnerability or measured production result.

## SQL flow logical errors and administrative bounds

Functions951-1950 read continuously. add_flow explicitly RETURN NEXT '-5' or '-7' and CONTINUE rather than raising, so successful siblings can commit in the same SQL transaction; this confirms the contract's no-partial-tree promise is too broad for both adapters. Dedup mutation occurs before missing-parent check, so a skipped entry may still change dedup state. Python maps result strings directly to Job.id; missing-parent code is not automatically a rollback or exception in bulk flow. Single add_job instead raises BM001 for missing parent, a meaningful distinction.

remove checks unexpired locks through pending dependency descendants, then deletes a subtree traversed via parent coordinates; these are different graphs, and row/advisory locking around that precheck must not be assumed from atomic-function wording. Missing job returns0, which Job.remove misdescribes as 'locked by another worker'. drain/clean explicitly remove reverse dependencies/recount parents, unlike retention bulk deletion. clean active selection uses age only, with no unexpired-lock exclusion in this function; it can delete still-active state and cannot stop its processor. Parent release may make other work runnable. Scheduler pending jobs are filtered, completed/failed scheduler jobs are cleanable. promote/change_delay/change_priority read state without FOR UPDATE then update by ID only: concurrency rechecks are not established. Several functions return negative statuses rather than BM001; Python promote/reprocess return them without converting to exceptions, so local caller fields can be updated despite logical failure. change_delay explicitly notifies to recompute timing, strengthening the relevance of Python's notification deadline concern. No SQL was run.

## SQL terminal ownership and event retention source concerns

Functions351-950 read continuously. Terminal completion/failure lock rows and require active state plus matching token unless token0, but do not compare locked_until_ms. extend_lock likewise matches active/token without checking prior expiry, so an expired matching token can be renewed or finalized before stalled recovery changes the row; Redis key expiry equivalence must not be assumed. This is code-path evidence, not a concurrent runtime reproduction. Completion rejects pending/failed dependencies, updates parent dependencies, applies retention, then acquires dedup lock/finalizes/requeues. Failure emits retries-exhausted whenever taking terminal path, including explicit unrecoverable failure before numeric attempt budget. Remaining helper lock ordering still needs review.

apply_retention immediate removal detaches pending dependency/releases parent, but age/count bulk DELETE does not run that same explicit detach loop. Foreign keys cascade only parent-owned dependency/log rows, so reverse child-dependency cleanup merits tests. Event publication allocates from global event_seq, coalesces notifications via queue try-lock, and trims only when global ID modulo256==0. Because the sequence is global across queues, a particular queue can keep receiving non-multiple IDs indefinitely while another gets all multiples; the comment's per-queue max+256 bound is not established by this condition. Global ID minus max can also trim a sparse queue to fewer than max retained events. IDs allocated before transaction commit can become visible out of order; whether consumer cursor skips a late commit must be traced in TypeScript event backend. All are source concerns requiring exact consumer/test oracles, not measured event loss.

## SQL dependency receipts: schema, stale dedup and wake hints

Supporting reads closed 0001_schema.sql349, 0003_dedup_stale_key.sql148, has_waiting_job.sql12, next_delay.sql3, add_job.sql6, copy_scripts.sh12 and pyproject.toml55; 0002_functions.sql remains partial1-350/3474. Schema establishes one physical state column, queue-scoped IDs, parent-owned dependency rows with cascade and job-log foreign keys. It defines no per-account role provisioning/RLS policy; namespace independence is not account authorization. Events use sequence IDs and cursor reads, not committed-transaction ordering proof from the schema alone. keepLast stores at most one overwritten pending payload.

Functions1-350 show add_job acquires dedup advisory lock, allocates ID, deduplicates BEFORE parent validation/insert, raises missing-parent BM001, and notifies on both waiting and delayed inserts. move_to_active promotes due jobs before pause check, serializes configured concurrency and limiter checks with queue-hash advisory locks, claims FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED and installs caller token/expiry. Maximum prefers metadata; duration prefers worker option. has_waiting_job checks waiting state plus pause only; it does not promote future delayed rows, proving a matching notification for a future delayed insert need not wake the Python wait immediately. next_delay reads MIN delayed process_at; Python's already-traced notification branch doesn't re-arm its existing deadline. This is still a source timing hypothesis, not a runtime outage report.

Migration0003 replaces earlier deduplicate_job: missing persistent winner clears dedup+pending, expiring missing winner remains a throttle until expiry, delayed replacement clears pending, active keepLast overwrites latest. Add/finish helper lock discipline still needs remaining function reads. Packaging shell copies generated rawScripts (copy failure only echoes), removes/replaces bundled SQL then copies shared sources; wheel metadata includes generated Lua/SQL but does not itself run shell. Version3.4.0, Python>=3.10, redis range6.2..<9 and fixed croniter2.0.7/psycopg3.3.6 declarations are pinned metadata, not installed runtime receipts.

## Python Postgres adapter source closure, SQL still separate

Closed postgres_backend.py969. All operation enforcement delegates fixed SQL commands/functions; wrapper review alone cannot credit row locks, token checks, flow rollback or global quotas. forQueue shares schema/credentials/connection, clientName includes schema but qualifiedName only queue name, and close ignores force and has no closed guard (resource can reopen). Metrics failures are swallowed after terminal transition, making metrics incomplete independently of job state. moveToDelayed with fetchNext uses separate transition then activation calls, unlike an atomic combined function. Logs count/trim and scheduler IDs/details likewise span separate statements. Return-value compatibility parses JSON-looking strings into values, so a processor returning literal string '123' can become numeric123 in this adapter, unlike literal-string preservation expectations; source behavior, not run.

getDeduplicationJobId indexes result.rows[0] with 'job_id' although PgResult rows are tuples; first_map/maps are used elsewhere. This is a source result-shape concern requiring an oracle. trimEvents/removeDeprecatedPriorityKey are silent no-ops. moveToWaitingChildren returns code==1 without passing op for BM001 translation; reprocessJob returns raw code and ignores job.opts.lifo in favor of retry opts. No assumption of Redis-equivalent exceptions or state precedence is justified from names.

LISTEN subscribes before probing work, checks shared-channel payload equals queue name, and re-probes after reconnect. Notifications are wake hints, not claims or authenticated work identities. Once waiting, matching notification only probes immediately claimable work, without recomputing the deadline for a newly inserted earlier future delayed job; delay re-arm behavior depends on has_waiting_job/next_delay SQL and notification tests still to read.
