## Python Job and Queue API boundaries

Closed job.py392 and queue.py540. Job.updateData/updateProgress change local fields before returning a backend coroutine, so unawaited/failed operations can leave local state ahead of persistence. Completion likewise sets returnvalue before awaiting token-checked backend transition. Retry is suppressed by actual UnrecoverableError instance, discard or attempts; this differs from TypeScript's name-based compatibility. Stacktrace retention uses slice[-(limit-1):limit], not a conventional last-N slice; limit1 becomes [0:1], a source-level retention concern without a dedicated Python test found by name search. fromJSON strictly decodes most stored JSON but returnvalue parsing tolerates invalid data; datastore contents are trusted input, not signed job identity.

Queue uses shallow merged options, backend delegation for most operations, but rateLimit/global concurrency/global rate setters directly call self.client Redis commands. These methods are not datastore-neutral merely because Queue accepts a Postgres backend. The rate-limit doc calls its window rolling; actual already-reviewed Redis counter/TTL enforcement is a window beginning at counter initialization, not a sliding log of each request. Worker discovery takes the largest per-node CLIENT LIST match set rather than a union, and unsupported CLIENT LIST returns a diagnostic pseudo-worker that count includes. Names remain labels, not principals. Redis getJobs consumes atomic Lua ID/hash results with deduplication; the non-Redis path separately obtains IDs, waits a set of fetch tasks (losing order), and calls asyncio.wait even for an empty task list, a source concern needing a focused oracle. Queue pause does not stop active processors; forced obliteration deletes state, not their external effects. No datastore calls or upstream tests were run.

## Python cancellation, renewal and retry primitives

Closed abort_controller.py91, timer.py29, backoffs.py49 and lock_manager.py243. AbortSignal uses an asyncio.Event; first abort reason wins and processors must cooperate. The 'read-only' view is convention, not an authorization boundary, and cancellation doesn't reverse side effects. Failure emits diagnostics but does not automatically abort the processor. cancel_job returnsTrue whenever a controller exists, even if already aborted, unlike a fresh-cancellation count. is_running checks task presence, not task.done; a failed renewal task can still appear running.

The renewal helper catches backend errors and emits error; an error listener that itself raises can escape that handler and terminate the loop, whose outer catch covers cancellation only. Close cancels/awaits the renewal task, guards diagnostic emission and clears tracking; this is renewal-manager cleanup, not verified processor-task termination. Timer is separate: its try wraps the whole repeat loop, so one callback exception emits once then ends repetition; stop cancels without awaiting. Backoffs implement fixed/exponential synchronously without jitter/cap and call custom strategy without await despite calculate being async. Language-port behavior must be traced independently from TypeScript. Source only; no Python package imports or worker execution.

## Glide adapter serialization and close boundaries

Closed valkey-glide-client.ts continuously1–1335. Adapter serializes every operation per instance, including blocking reads, and tracks active blocking commands so close can interrupt rather than wait behind them. Transaction pipeline aliases MULTI and preloads before native atomic Batch or serialized fallback MULTI/EXEC; mixed errors do not imply rollback.

Two lifecycle source concerns require specific oracles: runSerialized replaces operationChain, waits previous, then calls ensureOpen outside the try/finally that releases its new promise. Closing while a queued operation waits can make that check throw without releasing the chain, potentially preventing later close/reconnect progress. quit fires closeRaw without awaiting it and returnsOK, so it is not a socket/write completion receipt. A compatibility native exec failure is caught then may fall back to raw MULTI; actual native failure/commit semantics must be known before assuming replay is harmless. scanStream keeps cursor/running state but can return on push(false) at final cursor0 before signaling EOF, making the next read restart from0; source backpressure hypothesis, not reproduced. ## Bun adapter full lifecycle and transaction review

Unlike node-redis, duplicate lazily uses native async duplication to preserve server/options; URL reconstruction is only a fallback for exotic raw clients. ## Getter snapshots and orphan cleanup boundaries

Closed getJobs152, removeOrphanedJobs78 and getState/getStateV2 66/59; rechecked Redis backend1310–1375/2300–2420. getJobs reads IDs plus hashes in one Lua call, skips absent hashes, backfills bounded pages under an iteration cap and does not promise a full page when exhausted. Separate pages/calls are not one snapshot. getState checks state memberships in precedence order without hash/lock verification; paused maps to waiting, and contradictory memberships return the first matching state. Old variant materializes whole lists; V2 uses LPOS.

Orphan cleanup SCANs outside the script, then atomically checks each batch against currently supplied queue state keys by type. It deletes hash plus a fixed subkey list only when none references the candidate; it does not independently test lock ownership or arbitrary cross-queue parent dependencies. Returned count increments for every unreferenced candidate even if DEL removes nothing. SCAN may revisit candidates across batches, and the limit check happens after an entire batch, so count is not necessarily unique existing jobs deleted or a strict deletion maximum. This is source analysis; no cleanup was run. Public rescheduling note correction: Job updates its local delay/priority properties after await, not its opts object.

## Public rescheduling callers and dependency removal

Closed promote62 and removeChildDependency34; rechecked Redis backend1510–1605 and Job970–1010. Public Job delegates to backend, then updates its local opts after await; Redis backend translates only negative return values to errors. Priority0 success is not a report that a particular wait/active/delayed state changed. Promotion checks delayed membership, not job-hash existence, then may write delay=0 to that key; corrupted orphan state is not rejected by that script itself. Removing a dependency checks both hashes, delegates previously traced parent-release logic, then clears child parent metadata only if relationship removal succeeds. Releasing the last dependency can make the parent runnable; relationship cleanup is an orchestration change, not cancellation of child effects. No caller was executed.

## Admission variants and rescheduling commands

Closed addDelayedJob109, addParentJob111, addPrioritizedJob119, changeDelay55 and changePriority72. Delayed/priority variants share the same parent-exists, explicit-ID duplicate and dedup gates as standard admission. Parent admission deliberately invokes deduplicateJobWithoutReplace rather than delayed replacement and stores waiting-children state; identical option names do not guarantee identical state transitions across admission types. Delay modification requires delayed-set membership and writes the new delay/score/event only after successful removal. Priority modification returns0 and changes the job hash for any existing job, even if it was absent from both prioritized and selected wait list; that result is metadata update, not proof immediate rescheduling. Priority push-back uses counter0 ahead of peers, so ordering is deliberately not FIFO in that branch. Commands trust supplied key prefix/IDs and Redis access, not account/session/device identity.

## Retry/delay/activation mutation order

Closed moveToDelayed97, retryJob88, moveJobFromActiveToWait68, moveToActive98, pushBackJobWithPriority10 and isQueueMaxed16; rechecked getRateLimitTTL18 and prepareJobForProcessing53. Delaying/retrying removes the lock and updates optional fields before checking active-list membership; a subsequent-3 can leave those earlier changes. Retry additionally promotes other due jobs before verifying the requested job exists or its token, so an unsuccessful target operation can still affect queue scheduling. Active-to-wait removes its lock but returns limiter TTL/0 even when active LREM found nothing; return0 is not proof requeue occurred. All are source semantics, not newly run failure injections.

Activation promotes due jobs before pause/max/rate early returns; queue rate/concurrency budgets concern stored queue keys/list counts, not authenticated users/devices/IPs. Global max metadata takes precedence over worker limiter max, while duration expression prefers worker duration when supplied; configuration consistency matters. prepareJobForProcessing increments rate counter except deferred failure, sets expiry only on counter1, and skips lock creation when token is literal0. Worker name is optional pb metadata. Therefore header comments promising ownership need the actual token/renewal and trusted caller assumptions. Rate TTL helper only blocks positive expiry, not a persistent over-limit key; no-expiry corruption handling requires broader caller/state analysis. No queue or limiter runtime was exercised.

## Conditional removal and waiting-children transition

Closed removeDeduplicationKey23, releaseLock20 and moveToWaitingChildren96. Dedup removal compares stored job ID, lock release compares a token; these prevent stale-owner deletion but are not account authorization. releaseLock's header incorrectly describes duration/extension/OK while implementation only conditionally DELs and returns an integer. Waiting-children checks job existence, failed-child set and requested/all pending dependencies before removing lock and active-list membership, then records waiting-children state/event. If active membership is missing, its-3 return occurs after successful lock removal: failure returns do not imply no mutation. No Redis command was run.

Finalization-order clarification for the dedup review: moveToFinished checks the completing job's pending/unsuccessful dependencies before lock removal; it updates the job fields and removes active membership before requeuing pending dedup work. Its original-parent result/dependency processing follows that requeue. 'Parent conditions before requeue' in the preceding broad description should be read only as completion dependency checks, not the later original-parent update.

## Complete deduplication test file and finalization recheck

Finished tests/deduplication.test.ts continuously481–2151 (full file1–2151 now reviewed). Tests contrast fixed TTL versus extend, delayed replacement versus active suppression, owner-conditional key removal, preserving a newer winner when removing an older completed job, pending-latest coalescing, pause/resume, custom IDs, permanent failure and retries-exhausted. The retry case counts three first-job attempts and one later payload; the 'no parallel' case measures one worker with concurrency5, not a distributed outage/fencing proof. A TTL-expiry-named case actually verifies keepLastIfActive prevents key expiry and uses concurrency1 without an independent overlap counter. Delay-requeue test checks eventual data order, not a minimum elapsed delay. Gates/listeners improve ordering, but many cases rely on sleeps and event callbacks; they do not validate arbitrary crashes, Redis replication durability or side-effect exactly-once execution. No tests ran here.

Rechecked setDeduplicationKey1–13, requeueDeduplicatedJob1–66 and moveToFinished75–150. Finalization validates job/lock state and processes parent conditions before pending-next requeue; pending data is only materialized on terminal completion/failure paths, not an intermediate retry. Requeue preserves stored custom ID, advances the queue counter regardless, sets persistent dedup ownership for keepLastIfActive, stores/enqueues, links parent then deletes pending hash. Retaining pending hash until the end is not Redis rollback: a Lua command error can leave earlier writes while retaining the proto-job. The helper itself has no fresh identity/permission check or explicit custom-ID collision guard; callers and queue access remain the trust boundary. Do not promote happy-path tests to crash-safe delivery proof.

## BullMQ Redis deduplication admission flow

Closed recoverStaleDeduplicationKey29, deduplicateJobWithoutReplace73, deduplicateJob79, storeDeduplicatedNextJob56, handleDuplicatedJob27, updateExistingJobsParent28, checkItemInList13 and addStandardJob126. Stale recovery requires a persistent dedup key (PTTL=-1) whose winning job hash is absent; a still-expiring throttle window is intentionally not cleared just because that hash vanished. Recovery/replacement deletes pending-next payload to avoid resurrecting old work. keepLastIfActive scans the entire active list, overwrites one pending proto-job per dedup ID and persists the dedup key; it is coalescing, not preservation of every accepted input. Replacing delayed work removes its stored keys; active work is not revoked by that replacement path. All decisions are within Redis-script execution, not worker-account authorization or end-to-end side-effect idempotency.

Explicit duplicate IDs are handled before dedup options; duplicate handling can update parent dependencies/results and returns-7 for a conflicting existing live parent. Standard admission increments the counter before duplicate return, requires parent existence, stores a new job only after dedup allows it, then queues and links its dependency. A returned existing job ID or deduplicated event is not evidence the new payload will run. Began tests/deduplication.test.ts1–480 (file remains partial): cases inspect original/new hashes, stale-key recovery, retained TTL after missing hash, clearing pending-next payload and job-owner-conditional removal. Outage simulation manually deletes Redis job hashes and force-closes a gated worker; it is not a real Redis durability/restart experiment. Timing-based25/100/105/150ms delays are test scheduling assumptions, not runtime receipts here. No Redis package or worker was run.

## Multipart profile controls and ambiguity handling

Closed multipart.go continuously1–687. Profiles distinguish normal form/header collections, malformed boundaries/parameters, filename versus filename* disagreement, percent-decoded names, repeated parameters, quoted extended filenames and charset policy. Both filename interpretations are inspected when they differ; that covers alternative backend readings rather than proving which backend will execute. Most filename/error cases run DetectionOnly and assert logged rule IDs, not rejection. Charset controls include a useful plain-upload negative control, unlisted charset positive and empty-charset case: negated substring @within misses the empty value while anchored regex catches it. This is policy semantic evidence, not a recommendation to evade controls.

The150-byte ProcessPartial profile intentionally keeps truncated file/form content and suppresses strict-error/body-error for a cut attributed to reaching the configured limit, including a client-precut body exactly at that limit. It contrasts a client-cut closing boundary below the limit, which expects strict/body flags and a400 interruption. INBOUND_DATA_ERROR itself is pass/log. Thus a deployment must choose its partial-body policy rather than assuming all truncation is denied. Two early invalid-character examples use raw Go strings containing literal backslash-x sequences; the previously reviewed fixture-conversion path determines whether actual control bytes reach the parser. None of the embedded PHP content or fixture strings was executed.

## Transaction interface comments require implementation checks

Closed types/transaction.go 1–204; rechecked transaction.go 780–950 and 1020–1140. The interface explicitly says transactions are not thread-safe, interventions must be checked by callers, and body accessibility may change via later ctl rules. Its ProcessURI comment says GET arguments are not added, but implementation calls ExtractGetArguments for both normal RawQuery and malformed-URI best-effort fallback. A consumer following the comment and adding them again can duplicate inspected values and consume the argument budget; this is a source composition concern, not an executed integration result. Parse-error flag and raw URI preserve evidence rather than automatically reject malformed input. AddGet/Post/Path enforce individual collection TotalValues, not one combined global count; dropping excess arguments sets a flag and warning rather than itself returning an interruption. Previously traced configured rules must turn such flags into denial. Rechecking body reader confirms disabled engine/body access returns without reading, and reaching the configured limit uses >=/equality, not only strictly greater sizes. API comments and configured policies cannot substitute for complete connector enforcement.

## Audit-part parsing and modification are different contracts

Closed types/waf.go (287) and waf_test.go (205). Status parsers lowercase explicit recognized strings; BodyLimitAction and Interruption are configuration/result types, not proof a connector obeys them. Absolute audit-part parsing requires leading A/trailing Z and permits any valid middle ordering/repeats; it does not canonicalize. Relative ApplyAuditLogParts canonicalizes and deduplicates middle parts BCDEFGHIJK, treating A/Z as implicit (tests explicitly expect their absence). Thus a relative modification changes ordering as well as membership, while an absolute configuration can retain K-before-H ordering noted earlier. Do not infer a malformed emitted boundary from the absent A/Z alone: native serialization supplies framing separately. Tests exercise helper return values, not all serializers/connector effects. Repeated argument/body limits, audit configuration and enum labels remain separate from account identity or authentication.

## Collection semantics beneath quota and audit claims

Closed map/named/concat/sized implementations and tests (273/343/200/126/109/120/82/37), no-op/test and single test (22/18/41). Map.Len counts keys, TotalValues counts every repeated value with an incremental counter; regression tests explicitly distinguish1000 repeated values from1 key. Map.Add itself imposes no quota, so enforcement requires previously traced transaction/parser callers. SizeCollection sums original key+value byte lengths for each value, not wire bytes, number of users or retained memory. Its selectors ignore supplied string/regex and return the aggregate. Default Map lowercases lookup keys but preserves original spelling in matches; case-sensitive constructors differ. NamedCollectionNames.FindString directly indexes the supplied key unlike inherited case-insensitive Map lookup; current tests use lowercase keys. ConcatKeyed.Get lowercases before delegating even to case-sensitive backing collections, whereas FindString delegates unchanged: caller access method therefore matters (source semantic distinction; no live rejection experiment).

Map.Get and Find* allocate result slices/records; NamedCollection.Data also copies map/slices, correcting the earlier provisional audit-header alias inference. Name views and concatenated argument collections remain live views over underlying collections, with nondeterministic map order and repeated-name entries. Getters on the serialized audit model later expose its own maps/slices; these are different ownership layers. The 'bulk alloc independence' test comments claim mutation but only read/check distinct values; helper assertValuesMatch joins with commas, making some distinct lists ambiguous. No-op test similarly compares no results to one empty string through that weak helper. These are test-oracle limitations, not runtime failure claims. No Go test/benchmark was run.

## Remaining action metadata and process-environment mutation

Follow-up env closure: read the36-line engine ENV profile (one transaction sets test=123 then checks ENV) and rechecked complete Close1709–1765. Close clears transaction collections/buffers and returns pooled transaction but does not restore the host environment. Repository-wide Go search finds no production Unsetenv/Clearenv/restoration caller; only example-test cleanup unsets its own overrides. Thus process-environment persistence is established in the library source, while cross-request runtime observations remain untested. Transaction ENV reset alone does not undo os.Setenv, and arbitrary embedding application restoration is outside the pin.

Closed log/nolog/logdata/msg/rev/tag/ver and their remaining tests (47/28/47/36/55/33/53/32/48/50/48/32), setenv (88), transformation action (64), and pass init test (25). Log/nolog initialize both error/audit flags; macros supply message/data text later in rule evaluation. Rev/version/tag are arbitrary configured strings, not signed source/version provenance; tag.Init appends raw text despite its prose mentioning macro support, so expansion cannot be inferred from that comment. Built-in t configures/clears transformations at compile time and has no runtime Evaluate effect; previously reviewed registry/engine paths provide actual transformation enforcement. The pass unexpected-arguments subtest accidentally calls nolog(), so it does not test that pass branch.

setenv is materially different from TX setvar: it expands request-derived macro content and calls os.Setenv on a rule-configured key, then writes transaction ENV even when the process Setenv call fails. This is process-global mutation, not request-local/worker-local secret isolation. No rollback is present in the action; transaction close/reset needs separate caller scrutiny for a restoration claim. Because nondisruptive actions can precede full chain success, config authors must not treat setenv as an atomic chain-scoped update. No environment variable was set or read during this review; no rule code was executed.

## Chain side effects and multiphase evidence

Closed chain/capture/multiMatch actions and initialization tests (71/33/54/32/46/32), normal chain profiles (243), multiphase chain profiles (438); rechecked rule300–490 and510–550. Initialization tests only set booleans. Actual matching executes nondisruptive actions when individual rules match before the complete chain succeeds (normal path), while parent disruptive/flow actions wait for the full chain; a later failed condition does not roll back earlier setvar. MultiMatch uses repeated transformed candidates without transformation cache, rather than representing extra independent requests or accounts. Capture flag is consumed by rule/operator paths, not the empty action Evaluate method alone.

Multiphase profiles are guarded by their build tag and deliberately expect some rule evaluation in phases earlier than the configured phase, partial chain reconsideration, and controlled counter deduplication. One profile explicitly expects a parent variable evaluated twice when a TX dependency changes; another expects identical chain-variable matches not to increment again. These are rule-evaluation bookkeeping oracles within one transaction, not effect-fencing/idempotency guarantees for external side effects or general detector accuracy. Simplified CRS-like profiles shorten datasets and use synthetic strings; their names do not establish full upstream ruleset performance. No payload was sent anywhere and no Go profile executed.

## Phase and skip enforcement path

Closed phase/status/severity action/tests and phase/severity parsers (64/26/55/66/50/53/111), skip/skipAfter/init tests (66/76/47), and skip engine profiles (215); rechecked RuleGroup125–313. Phase parser accepts1–5 or exact lowercase aliases and rejects invalid values; status action merely parses an integer with a TODO for valid HTTP range, so accepted rule metadata is not proof a connector can emit it. Severity is configured metadata. The phase test's unknown-case error-text condition is inverted (fails if it contains 'unknown phase'), so only actual source establishes its 'invalid phase' behavior. Skip init accepts1 despite an error message saying greater than1.

Actual RuleGroup filters phase and transaction exclusions before skip handling, resets Skip at phase end, and interruption stops later nonlogging rules. SkipAfter consumes through the marker rather than evaluating it. Unlike Skip, it has no unconditional phase-end reset: the only assignments found clear it at transaction initialization or matching marker. A missing/excluded marker may therefore carry SkipAfter into later phases (source hypothesis; existing profiles contain matching markers). The profiles assert selected triggered/nontriggered IDs and interruptions, and are build-excluded under multiphase evaluation. They therefore do not establish identical multiphase semantics or authenticate the metadata used by upstream allow/skip rules. Setvar engine profile counts repeated parameter names within one request; macroexpansion profile validates transaction thresholds and missing-variable nonmatch, not persistent counters. No profile or Go runtime was executed.

## Rule metadata and absent persistent counters

Closed accuracy/maturity/id actions and tests (55/43/56/43/59/41), initcol/test (79/25), expirevar (49), setvar/test (198/185); registration search confirms initcol/expirevar are enabled action names. Accuracy/maturity only parse and range-check1–9 into rule metadata, then Evaluate does nothing: even9 is an author's configured label, not measured false-positive/negative performance. ID action requires a positive integer; duplicate/mandatory-ID enforcement remains in RuleGroup/build configuration, not authentication.

Despite persistent-collection prose/examples, initcol's Evaluate body is entirely commented out and produces no warning; expirevar accepts arbitrary init input and only warns unsupported when evaluated. Its expiry is not enforced. initcol tests check syntax only. setvar explicitly accepts only TX collection, expands macros, and mutates the transaction map; it does not implement shared IP/account/session rate counters or persistent TTL. Arithmetic reads the first current value, returns after logging conversion errors, and has no cross-transaction atomicity or saturating overflow check. Missing macro tests inspect error logs rather than rejection; ordinary string values beginning+/- remain strings when not numerical. No distributed counter/protection claim can follow from successful config parsing or these examples. Macro implementation/tests were already fully read; remaining engine chain/setvar/macroexpansion cases remain separately queued, and no Go runtime test was run.

## Debug logging does not rescue durable audit failure

Completed debug logger/default/no-op/level implementations and all inventoried tests (193/101/29/51/184/22/58). Default printer ignores log.Printf's error and Event.Msg returns void, so the audit caller's fallback error log has no delivery acknowledgement either. Level gates can return no-op events; an empty message is suppressed even with fields. Str quotes its value, but Err appends err.Error raw between quotes and Msg passes message raw; audit native formatter's CR/LF escaping does not apply to this separate debug serialization. Whether hostile error text reaches a given path needs caller-specific validation, not a universal injection claim. Stringer documents nil handling but calls val.String unconditionally in the default implementation; current tests only use a nonnil buffer. Logger.With/event field slices use append/shared backing buffers, and tests check an empty original logger and linear accumulation, not sibling concurrent mutation under retained capacity. No race or injection reproduction is claimed. Tests largely inspect event types/output prefixes and normal values; none exercises failed output writers. Default level is Info, and a no-op logger's factory can later be reused with changed output/level, so named logging modes are configuration choices, not immutable evidence policy.

## Audit flag flow and formatter composition risks

Closed auditlog/noauditlog actions and noauditlog test (46/50/32), plus full audit integration tests (560). Rechecked MatchRule565–620 and AuditLog1540–1725. Actions set the compiled rule Audit boolean; runtime matching ORs it into tx.audit. RelevantOnly still filters a matching audited rule by configured status regex; an unaudited rule can still yield a transaction log when status matches. Tests distinguish nolog from nolog,auditlog and K metadata from H error text. Negative log assertions accept any JSON decode error rather than asserting exact empty-file length; positive tests verify selected fields. They do not inject a disk-full/sink failure or prove crash durability.

AuditLog returns a fresh outer model, copies request/response header data through NamedCollection.Data, and directly references arguments and component names. The subsequent collection dependency read corrected the earlier header-alias inference: Data allocates a new map and slices, so that alias concern does not apply to headers. It reads the entire request buffer into a string for part C without returning read errors, and missing/overflowed request length becomes0 after debug logging. Upload MIME is inferred from filename extension, not sniffed content. Part-H-only messages contain ErrorMessage_ with nil Data_, whereas OCSF getMatchDetails unconditionally calls match.Data().Msg()/Data(); this is a reachable composition concern for H-without-K configuration using OCSF (source hypothesis, no Go reproduction, OCSF fixtures all provide Data_). Part ordering also matters: K entries get ErrorMessage_ only if H has already been seen in the parts iteration. This needs a specific configured runtime oracle before a verified bug claim. Existing test comments are not substituted for the actual caller construction path. No upstream code was executed.

## Syslog and OCSF labels preserve policy interpretation limits

Completed syslog writer/test (82/285) and OCSF formatter/test (260/541). Syslog tests replace the dialer globally and inject errors, so they verify routing/info-vs-error and propagated errors without testing any daemon, network authentication, delivery acknowledgement or timeout. Error priority follows IsInterrupted, not rule severity or measured harm. Those returned errors still meet ProcessLogging's log-only caller path. This implementation is build-excluded on Windows/Plan9/TinyGo as already recorded.

OCSF hardcodes activity/type Read even when HTTP method is POST, maps Allowed/Denied solely from the transaction interruption flag, uses first message as primary text, copies X-Forwarded-For as an unverified header field, and stores arguments in comma-separated unescaped k=v text with a trailing comma. Those fields are telemetry conventions, not independent proof of actual network enforcement, trusted original IP or reversible argument serialization. Source copies transaction timestamp unchanged but emits LoggedTime using UnixMicro; fixtures use Unix-second-like values. Schema-unit correctness is not established: schema validation is explicitly commented out in tests, and the external OCSF implementation/schema is unreviewed. Tests unmarshal into the same generated types and compare selected fields; all three main fixtures have IsInterrupted=true despite a conditional Allowed assertion branch. Header tests compare each emitted value to only the first input value and use single-valued fixtures, not repeated-header semantics. Formatter ignores JSON marshal errors. No runtime/schema validation was performed.

## Audit serialization, framing and test strength

Closed native/JSON/legacy formatter implementations (189/105/72), native tests (929), JSON tests (161), registry tests (110), and audit-model tests (332). Native format deliberately escapes backslash before CR/LF in selected transaction/request/response/header/body/message/rule fields; tests distinguish real controls from literal backslash sequences and compare benign/hostile line counts. An older hasLineContaining helper alone can miss forged lines sharing escaped text, explicitly acknowledged by newer tests. These tests exercise formatter output, not the concurrent writer's separately formatted index, which does not use logEscaper; native escaping cannot be assumed to cover every sink. Random record boundaries are framing, not authenticity or signed receipts. JSON uses encoding/json serialization; legacy JSON joins repeated headers with comma-space and omits modern body/files structure, so lossy compatibility output is not byte-identical evidence. No format performs generic secret redaction here; fixture Cookie/URI/body values remain represented. Caller-controlled log data is evidence to interpret, not proof of operator/account attribution.

The invalid-JSON model test checks an error's text only if an error exists and has no else failure if nil: its name overstates its assertion. Empty/full model tests each allocate a fresh Log; neither covers the reused-instance stale-Messages_ hypothesis noted above. Registry tests establish case-insensitive lookup and same formatter pointer, not concurrent mutation safety or access control. Native test fixtures containing SQLi/CRS messages are preconstructed records, not actual detector evaluations. All source/test-oracle review only; no serialization program or network/file sink was executed.

## Audit delivery failure and build-specific no-op paths

Closed plugin registration/examples/interface (20/52/80/73/143), audit model/logger registry (456/58), HTTPS/serial/concurrent writers and tests (71/124/67/118/103/153), platform registrations/no-op implementations (29/29/29/16/19/11). Serial logger ignores Println's returned error and returns nil; concurrent writer reports per-file WriteFile errors but ignores index logger errors, writes outside its index mutex, and lacks atomic rename/fsync/exclusive creation. Timestamp+transaction-ID file naming is not collision-safe against reused caller IDs; IDs also enter path.Join without local sanitization (conditional concern for untrusted custom IDs, not reproduced filesystem traversal). Tests cover normal writes/format errors, not failed sink writes/crash recovery or concurrent ID collision.

The HTTPS writer accepts HTTP targets, uses a one-second client timeout, checks2xx and deliberately ignores response-body drain errors. It provides neither a durable remote acknowledgement contract nor replay/idempotency keys or application authentication. The custom plugin example differs: DefaultClient with no explicit timeout, closes before draining and ignores HTTP status; example output is not the built-in writer's guarantee. No network writer was run.

Registries are process-global lowercase-name maps and registration replaces values without locking or permission checks; plugin code is trusted code. Windows/Plan9 registers syslog as a successful no-op; TinyGo registers concurrent/https/syslog as no-op and OCSF formatter as no-op. TinyGo serial registration points to serialWriter, so an older example comment calling it no-op is not accepted as enforcement evidence. Empty target/absent formatter also produces successful silent serial/concurrent writes. Thus accepted configuration and nil error can mean no receipt, depending on build and sink. Audit model getters expose underlying maps/slices and Accuracy is rule metadata, not measured detector accuracy. JSON UnmarshalJSON returns early on empty messages without clearing previous Messages_ on a reused Log (source hypothesis; reuse tests pending). All remain source review; formatter/integration paths are still queued.

## Full crawl tests: partial outcomes and observation limits

Completed full crawl tests1–1090 through their already reviewed spiders/engine/mockserver dependencies. Explicit StopDownload fail=True tests verify the errback Failure still carries a Response with partial bytes/headers; fail=False routes that partial response to the callback. CrawlSpider without an errback silently drops the404 failure and finishes normally; callback exceptions still permit rule links to be followed. An async generator test retains seven already yielded items after ValueError. Errbacks may suppress failure logging, emit an item counted as scraped, or schedule a new request. Successful crawl return and scraped counts therefore need an application-specific success oracle rather than representing every fetch/effect succeeding.

Response certificate tests inspect subject/issuer, not trusted-chain/hostname validation; response ip_address is compared to the destination hostname's resolved IPv4, not public source egress. Delay tests use average callback intervals with tolerance and a no-delay control, not per-request minimum spacing or a cross-process budget. Timeout tests establish expected callback-versus-errback timing order but do not assert a strict total wall-time bound. Unsupported start outputs are intentionally treated as items and the test expects strings/objects to reach item_scraped without an ItemAdapter consumer: that signal alone is not schema validation. Body-file cleanup is explicitly performed by this spider's callback, not proved automatic on error. The test file includes a DNS.invalid lookup and an assumed-unused localhost port outside fixture allocation; none were executed. All findings here remain source/test-oracle review, not local test pass claims.

## Crash recovery is not a worker lease protocol

Closed crash tests (98), resource-tail tests (62), dependency-version test (31). The crash fixture configures SQLite FIFO disk queues and JOBDIR_SYNC_EVERY=1, then calls os._exit during scheduling in a subprocess. Its resume oracle checks persisted pending requests are parsed and active priority metadata empties. The dedup crash test explicitly states the currently downloading request is lost and permits at least19 of20 unique parsed requests. Durable queue plus seen-set persistence is therefore not an acknowledged worker lease with automatic redelivery of in-flight work or exactly-once effects. This test was read only: no crash process was launched.

Resource-tail checks cover selected root logging handlers, current-loop asyncio tasks and expected reactor choice, not all sockets/files/threads or other event loops. The test itself notes per-function loops reduce pending-task usefulness. Dependency testing checks an exact Twisted version only when _SCRAPY_MIN is set; otherwise it skips. Neither presence of that test nor source pinning proves the local runtime's complete dependency versions or vulnerability status. No environment changes were made.

Closed loader tests (570), decorator tests (172) and deprecation tests (107). Loader tests explicitly assert load_item returns the supplied item object, nested loaders share that same item, nested replace changes parent output, and output preserves repeated values/order. Existing item values bypass input reprocessing; an uppercase input processor leaves initially supplied lowercase data unchanged. External itemloaders/processors implementation remains outside this pin's enforcement boundary. Decorator tests assert deprecated functions/classes still work after warnings; the thread test only checks a successful arithmetic result in a Deferred, with no cancellation/time-limit test. No Python imports or test execution occurred.

## Middleware rejection and recovery depend on enabled exception flow

Completed downloader-manager tests (599) and rechecked manager source1–231. The default response-exceptions setting remains False: process_response exceptions reach the request errback rather than downstream process_exception, with compatibility warnings for nonbuilt-in handlers. When enabled, only not-yet-processed outer middleware handles the exception; recovery may return a replacement Request or Response and processing resumes past the recovering component. Tests explicitly assert decompression failures retry only on the enabled path. CloseSpider is specially reraised before recovery under either mode. A process_request component can return a Response and avoid the actual downloader entirely; type checks enforce Response/Request/None shapes, not authenticity, account ownership or inspection provenance. These tests inject their download function and synthetic response bytes, including malformed gzip; no target is fetched. Therefore a rejection exception name is insufficient evidence of final rejection without tracing outer recovery and active settings. No new runtime result is claimed.

## Redirect header stripping does not confine request bodies

Closed redirect tests (501), meta-refresh tests (173), shared base (449), and scheme cases (41; already complete reread); rechecked redirect source1–268. Cross-origin tests explicitly preserve POST body and content headers on307/308 to a different synthetic hostname. Dropping Authorization and Cookie therefore does not establish absence of secret transfer: secret-bearing bodies and custom headers need separate destination policy. Shared header tests expect Cookie to survive same-host port changes and HTTP=>HTTPS upgrades while Authorization is removed for any scheme/host/port change; HTTPS=>HTTP removes both. All of these are constructed middleware calls, not contacted hosts. The HTTP-only redirect scheme allowlist blocks redirect transitions to file/data/FTP/etc, independently of the broader link-extractor scheme acceptance documented earlier.

Meta-refresh high interval returns the original response rather than sleeping to follow it; it is not retry backoff. Meta-refresh allowed-target tests only assert Request type and omit target URL equality, while HTTP redirect scheme tests assert exact target. Proxy checks step through middleware state with synthetic dummy credentials and no sockets: explicit request proxy persists across scheme changes; environment-derived scheme proxy/auth is removed then recomputed. This demonstrates state propagation rules, not distinct egress addresses, anonymity or device identity. Custom headers are not universally scrubbed: a test explicitly preserves X-Custom when POST becomes GET. Full redirect source confirms shallow replacement with cookies=None and selective named-header cleanup, followed by method/status-specific body retention. No network requests, proxy use or credential provisioning occurred.

## Retry and deduplication compatibility oracle

Closed retry tests (834). Retry count/log/priority assertions are mostly direct middleware/helper calls with constructed requests/responses/errors, without a clock or live transport: they do not establish timed exponential backoff, Retry-After handling, global budgets or exactly-once effects. Request max_retry_times metadata can override the middleware setting in either direction; helper arguments take precedence over metadata. Retry statistics count generated retries, not successful deliveries or remote deduplicated effects. A retry copy normally carries skip_dupefilter_once rather than permanently disabling filtering. The end-to-end local fixture then redirects retries to an already-seen target: modern scheduler expects one parsed target and two filtered redirects, while legacy/overriding scheduler compatibility expects three parses plus one deprecation warning. Hence scheduler implementation changes materially affect duplicate target work even though retry count configuration is identical. This is source/test-oracle evidence only; no test was run and no target contacted.

Without a crawler, the first supplied instance is retained and later mismatches raise. This is in-process object consistency, not account/workload authentication or a tenant boundary. No tests were executed or temporary stores created during review.

## Stop-download test oracles and engine harness limits

Engine-loop tests (515) close the scheduling oracle: a scheduler may return no next request while reporting pending work; periodic engine heartbeats eventually resume it. That heartbeat is scheduling liveness, not authenticated worker lease renewal. A start generator that yields one request then raises is expected to crawl the yielded request and finish with start_error; failure does not retract prior output. Queue-order tests deliberately set CONCURRENT_REQUESTS=1 and inject priorities/start queues, so their strict ordering is not an across-worker fairness guarantee. Caller-provided download_slot metadata partitions scheduling, not accounts or devices. Lazy start uses needs_backout plus scheduler_empty notification, not a distributed quota/lease. Shared pipeline fixture (17) only raises arithmetic errors for error-path assertions; no production sink or rollback is involved.

Also completed core scraper tests (140), start tests (135), middleware tests (111), signals tests (57), and engine tests (642). Engine stop(force) explicitly raises ValueError; reentrant fast stop's cancellation assertion patches the downloader with AsyncMock, and the cancelled-failure suppression test injects a Failure rather than cancelling a live transport. The pipeline-stop test explicitly expects its current item to finish and be counted before shutdown. Idle checks cover pending pre-downloader work and a direct downloader fetch through synthetic handlers; pause tests only toggle the flag. Request-scheduled rejection demonstrates IgnoreRequest preventing scheduler enqueue, separate from later transport behavior. Scraper tests expect an ignored request's failing errback to increment spider_exceptions without an Error downloading log. Start/pipeline tests accept unordered completion; scheduler_empty is asserted at least once, not exactly once. Middleware NotConfigured silently excludes the component in the expected list, so configured presence alone does not establish an enabled guard. The short-timeout engine test targets an external public URL and only rejects AttributeError/AssertionError output within a harness timeout; it was read, not executed, and is not a local containment oracle.

Completed tests/spiders.py (699), stop-download bytes/headers tests (66/65), shared engine assertion/run helpers (163/185), and direct engine-download tests (140). Source review only; no upstream test execution. The headers signal test raises StopDownload(fail=False), expects zero bytes, and still expects downloaded responses and selected visited URLs. The bytes signal test expects exactly one chunk per request and only a strictly smaller-than-complete large response: it deliberately avoids a fixed byte ceiling because buffering varies. Thus stopping transport is distinct from rejecting the callback response, and these tests do not establish a universal byte budget or undo an already issued request. Shared spiders separately provide fail=True errback variants, but their presence alone is not a passing runtime result.

The shared harness records mutable in-process objects and last signal arguments, starts crawl without awaiting its returned Deferred, waits on engine_stopped, and disconnects all receivers for each signal during stop. Its normal close oracle expects reason=finished,error=False; this is not durable audit attribution or isolation between concurrent harness users. The direct download suite substitutes a Mock downloader/slot and tests success, failure cleanup and an iterative replacement chain longer than Python's recursion limit. That proves intended bookkeeping/iteration assertions, not live redirect authorization, network timeout enforcement or real cancellation. No fixture servers were launched.

## Remaining Scrapy package adapters and exports

Closed remaining inventoried scrapy/ Python files: ItemLoader adapter (107), decorators (172), deprecation helpers (165), signal definitions (65), package exports/interfaces/templates (4/1/33/24/5/1/1/42/6), deprecated display/FTP/job/version wrappers (17/17/16/16), deprecated SSL utility (47), log-root formatter (71). This closes the inventory's Scrapy package Python source slice, not its remaining tests, template/config/docs/data files or external library enforcement.

ItemLoader delegates processors/parent context to external itemloaders; if selector construction raises AttributeError it continues with selector=None. Context is extraction state with live response, not authenticated account state. inthread delegates to the already reviewed thread helper and does not impose a time budget. Deprecated decorators warn then invoke the original callable; deprecation is not rejection. Method-override detection compares __code__ identity, not component trust/signatures. Signal constants are process object identities and their declared argument-name sets describe callbacks, not signed messages or remote capabilities. Root formatter delegates to Python Formatter with configurable format; each crawler may replace process-level formatting as already traced. Top-level package import loads many components and suppresses Twisted DeprecationWarning, so silent import does not establish no side effects or current safe dependencies. Deprecated get_temp_key_info is explicitly a no-op; X509 name formatting uses a fixed512-byte display buffer and is not certificate validation. No imports or external native calls were executed; remaining adapter tests are still queued honestly.

## Local generation is not atomic worker provisioning

Completed genspider/startproject commands (246/142), template renderer/wrapper (40/22), generator tests (293 each) and renderer tests (29). These generate trusted Python files; no account creation, workload enrollment, permissions/scopes or identity revocation is present. genspider sanitizes only dots/dashes and first character, then copies/renders directly; existence checks use the original name while output uses sanitized module. Colliding sanitized names or path-like names therefore need stronger caller validation/owned-root checks if exposed beyond a trusted local operator (source interaction, not a reproduced overwrite). Template selection accepts caller filesystem paths and interpolation is string.Template, not Python-string escaping. Generated files require review before execution.

startproject guards an existing scrapy.cfg, but accepts an existing directory and recursively copies ordinary files, follows is_dir behavior and adjusts owner-write permission. No staging transaction/rollback or concurrent provisioning lease appears here. Renderer computes substitutions, renames .tmpl first, then writes output directly, so interruption can leave partial generation. Tests cover regular overwrite refusal/--force, invalid project names, file existence/content and permission preservation, but not sanitize collisions, concurrent generation, symlink confinement or crash rollback. Unknown template explicitly returns exit zero with a message and no file. No generator, chmod, move, file removal, editor or template execution was performed; only research notes/ledger changed.

## Discovery commands retain local extension privileges

Completed completion/settings/edit/list/version commands (168/109/70/24/37), completion tests (226) and version tests (26); settings/edit/list behavior tests were closed in test_commands.py. Completion rebuilds command parsers and invokes configured completion hooks/imported spider loaders, rather than being a static unprivileged catalog. Tests verify returned candidates and script strings, not actual bash/fish/zsh execution or hostile candidate escaping. A broken spider returns an empty candidate list, not a validated empty project. Settings command loads pre-crawler and normal add-ons before printing values; it does not call crawler._apply_settings or instantiate engine/extensions. Its configured value output can contain secrets and is not a credential-safe diagnostics format. Add-on code itself retains arbitrary trusted Python effects.

Editor helper uses shlex-split argv and a separate file argument, without shell=True; no shell interpolation recipe is inferred. It still executes the configured local editor and waits without timeout. List imports configured spiders and returns sorted routing names. Version tests compare heading names and current package version, not security patch provenance or deployment identity. No completion script, editor, add-on, diagnostic command or runtime import was executed.

## Benchmark worker is a workload fixture, not an admission model

Completed bench command (79), server (57)/wrapper (21); its tests were closed with test_commands.py. Benchmark child listens without an explicit interface even though the parent client URL is localhost, inherits the test environment, and startup stdout/wait cleanup lack timeouts. CloseSpider default ten seconds is cooperative and can be overridden by higher-priority configuration. Server query values govern generated link count, with no own authenticated quota; random link generation is synthetic workload, not distributed scheduling or controller enrollment. Tests run very short configured timeout and look for crawled logs/no error count, not throughput accuracy, false-positive rates, strict cleanup deadline or all-interface containment. No benchmark, listener or traffic was run here.

## Parse-command output and pipeline completion

Completed parse command (429)/tests (761). Its --rules caller actually uses LinkExtractor.matches, closing the previously identified narrower-filter dependency: callback selection ignores extractor scheme/extension/text filters and chooses the first matching rule. This is callback discovery for a URL already requested, not a promised access-control path. Command items/requests are class-level dictionaries accumulated without reset; repeated command instances in one process can share prior output (source observation, tests use subprocesses and do not reproduce reuse).

--pipelines schedules async processing with _schedule_coro and keeps no completion handles; legacy path also ignores returned Deferreds. Items are recorded/printed before those processing outcomes and pipeline-returned replacements are not used here. Tests assert a synchronous log marker, not delayed pipeline completion, failure aggregation or durable side effects. With output also enabled, returned items enter the scraper's normal output path as well; possible duplicate pipeline invocation is a source interaction requiring reproduction, not a measured count. Async generator error test explicitly retains seven yielded items while logging ValueError, and discards exit status. Tests do explicitly assert zero exit for unknown spider/no matched spider URL, so zero is not a successful parse oracle. JSON meta/cbkwargs parsing validates syntax only; mapping behavior is deferred to update. Depth zero/negative avoids parsing without an explicit error. No command, pipeline or request was executed here.

## Notification completion is not authenticated delivery

Completed deprecated mail helper (242)/tests (165), StatsMailer (54)/tests (98), extension loader adapter (26) and exception definitions (201). MailSender stores configured username/password bytes in-process, requires neither SMTP authentication nor transport security by default, passes requireTransportSecurity from its TLS flag, and delegates SMTP/TLS behavior to Twisted. SSL branch uses ClientContextFactory; exact external Twisted verification is not reviewed here, so no measured certificate-validation claim is made. Debug mode builds the MIME message and calls its optional callback, then logs “Debug mail sent OK” without transmitting; tests mostly use that mode. The single sender-factory test checks ClientTLSOptions type, not successful authenticated delivery or server certificate rejection. No mail was sent, mailbox accessed or real credentials read.

Attachments are read in full, MIME encoded and the whole message copied into bytes/BytesIO; no size budget here. Recipients/subject/error text are logged, and StatsMailer serializes all stats without redaction into a close-triggered message. It currently labels the same get_stats mapping as both global and spider stats, rather than independently scoped ledgers; MagicMock/DummyStats tests assert content and method calls, not real transport. Adding its Deferred as a before-shutdown trigger waits on transport outcome, without a helper-level global close deadline or proof of recipient reading. Exception classes are control signals only: CloseSpider.error defaults False and StopDownload.fail=False explicitly routes a truncated response to the normal callback. Actual rejection still depends on the already traced catch sites, not exception names or documentation alone.

## Contracts measure selected outputs rather than enforce capability policy

Completed ContractsManager/base hooks (354), built-in contracts (243), check command (197), contract tests (1010) and check-command tests (355). Ordinary pre-hook failure/error is recorded and callback still runs; post-hooks inspect already-collected output. _clean_req consumes/discards returned items/requests but cannot undo callback effects. Built-in returns counts only matching object types, scrapes checks key presence rather than value/type/secret policy, and missing required request constructor arguments silently skip generating that candidate. Unknown @lines are ignored. Passing checks therefore covers configured sample oracles only, not all methods/inputs, runtime admission or absence of side effects.

ContractsManager.contracts is a class-level mutable dictionary, appended/overwritten by each manager without a fresh per-instance reset. Sequential managers can retain earlier registered names (source state observation, not locally reproduced), so this registry is not policy isolation between tests/tenants. Generated sample requests deliberately set dont_filter=True; callback output is materialized, without a contract-level memory/deadline cap. Hooks catch AssertionError/Exception, while KeyboardInterrupt escapes and is explicitly tested. Cancellation of async crawl tasks is skipped by check's task-result reporting callback; this is not evidence that cancellation counts as a test error. Test counts are hook executions, not authenticated worker/task totals.

Check disables item pipelines and FEEDS at priority35, above spider but below cmdline. Tests assert no marker/output under defaults, then both files exist when command line re-enables them. This is deliberate side-effect reduction, not immutable containment. Contract URLs can point to configured live systems and method/body/header/cookie contracts can create mutating requests; do not execute arbitrary upstream contracts as isolated validation. Reviewed tests use synthetic Response mocks, data URLs or their mock server, but none were run here. TextTestResult retains exception tracebacks and sample metadata without a redaction policy in these files. Existing synthetic runtime receipts are unchanged.

## Interactive shell is a trusted operator capability

Completed shell command (158), Shell (303), embedding utility (145)/wrapper (26), command tests (484) and embedding tests (209). -c calls Python eval with Scrapy globals and live crawler/settings/response objects; interactive shells load configured local console packages. This is intentionally trusted local code execution, not a restricted expression language or remote bot admission interface. Shell creates an engine without starting the normal scheduler-processing engine loop; fetch calls engine.download_async directly, ignores callbacks/errbacks (explicit tests), and waits synchronously across a reactor/thread-safe Future without a helper-level timeout. Removing fetch from displayed vars when in the same loop is a usability guard, not access control on the Shell object.

Shell.start installs process-wide SIGINT ignore. inspect_response restores the prior handler only after start returns normally, with no try/finally; an exception can skip that restoration (source path, not reproduced). CLI starts a daemon crawler thread without retaining/joining it in this file, so process exit is not a demonstrated graceful close/flush receipt. Command disables telnet/remote control at command-setting priority, which higher configuration may override; do not present this as immutable hardening. Existing request objects do not acquire redirect=False metadata through fetch's URL-only branch. Tests distinguish shell outcomes and mock inspect_response console normal return; they do not cover exception restoration or arbitrary thread hang.

Some tests query an intentionally nonexistent external hostname, others use mock HTTP/data/local files; none were run. Embedding tests isolate IPython profile/history with IPYTHONDIR, while command interactive tests isolate Scrapy config but inherit other environment state. POSIX TTY tests skip Windows and use 60-second expectations; absence of traceback and a 42 expression result show console compatibility, not isolation. No shell, debugger, signal change, network request, clipboard or user profile access was invoked during review.

## Fetch and request generation controls

Completed fetch/genrequest/view commands (118/74/29) and fetch/genrequest tests (96/106). Fetch overwrites the selected spider class start method with its single request, sets dont_filter and broad HTTP-status handling, then marks exit failure only for bootstrap_failed. Its --headers prints request and response headers wholesale, with no authorization/cookie redaction here. Tests assert normal/redirect/curl payloads and a startup failure; they do not establish nonzero status for every fetch failure. --no-redirect specifically changes HTTP-status handling; meta-refresh/other middleware behavior must not be inferred from that label alone (test chooses a redirect without meta-refresh).

Genrequest parses curl text into Python repr output; it does not execute curl or fetch the URL. Header/cookie test confirms those values are embedded in generated source, so source output can carry secrets. Optional formatting resolves ruff from PATH and sends full source to a subprocess without timeout; clipboard fallback reads via optional pyperclip. Tests substitute both clipboard and formatting subprocess; no actual clipboard or formatter was accessed here. View delegates fetched TextResponse to previously reviewed temporary-file browser helper and treats a non-text response as logged error, not an explicit exit failure. No browser, network, clipboard or child formatter was invoked.

## Feed and sitemap parsing limits

Completed XML/CSV feed spiders (235), iterators (186)/tests (524), sitemap spider (421)/tests (907), sitemap utility (130)/tests (342)/wrapper (17), base/feed spider tests (225), crawl test helper (28) and crawler test utility (124). XML iterparse explicitly disables entity resolution; sitemap tests assert a synthetic external-entity reference does not appear in loc. This is a bounded oracle, not independent verification of every libxml2 setting/version. Feed iterator uses huge_tree=True, holds the original response body, clears matching nodes without deleting preceding siblings, serializes each match and reparses it into a Selector. Streaming the parser therefore does not mean a strict whole-process memory cap. Sitemap iterator clears nodes/preceding siblings, but spider list() materializes all selected entries before returning a Request generator. No memory profile or XML parser was run here.

Sitemap parser uses recover=True, local-name matching without namespace validation, arbitrary link href as an alternate without checking rel/hreflang, and last duplicate tag value wins. This is tolerant discovery, not schema validation or authenticated source provenance. Sitemap/robots locs are urljoined and routed by regex, without same-origin or scheme restrictions in these files; separate downloader policy remains necessary. Signal cache keys are netloc without scheme/account; _robots_parsed joins relative sitemap paths against the triggering request URL, which may differ from robots response URL. Existing tests use leading-slash paths, so a relative-no-leading-slash mismatch remains a source hypothesis.

Gzip output maximum is applied on the decompression branch, configurable through request metadata; already XML-typed/classified bodies return directly and rely on earlier downloader limits. Tests assert None on their synthetic compressed fixture and warning sizes, not XML tree or CPU caps. csviter loads decoded content into StringIO, skips wrong-width rows with a warning and dict(zip) collapses duplicate headers; no typed schema, formula policy or per-record identity check is added. Tests explicitly accept truncated/misparsed fields with a wrong quote character, underscoring configuration-dependent interpretation. Test helper get_crawler disables remote control/telnet and chooses alternate handlers in reactorless mode: those test defaults must not be mistaken for production defaults. Every upstream test in this section was read, not executed; no entity target, fixture archive, service or external endpoint was accessed.

## Link extraction and rule routing are not destination authorization

Completed CrawlSpider (358)/tests (394), link extractor (455)/tests (969), scheme/extension catalog (146), Link (62)/tests (66) and shared spider test base (132). Default extracted schemes include data, file, ftp and s3 alongside HTTP(S); schemes=() disables scheme filtering entirely (test also retains mailto). IGNORED_EXTENSIONS is a suffix convenience, not content/type validation. Extractor matches() checks domains/regex but omits schemes, extensions and text restrictions enforced by _link_allowed; a caller must not substitute matches for extraction's full policy. Cross-origin HTML base href changes extracted destinations in an explicit test. Nofollow is preserved as a flag, and neither extraction nor CrawlSpider checks it as rejection here. None of these facts establishes actual local file access/exfiltration: downloader offsite configuration and separate handler enforcement still govern requests.

CrawlSpider process_request tests deliberately change example.org to example.com despite allowed_domains; these tests stop at generated Request objects and do not establish downloader acceptance. Rule index/name are mutable Request.meta values, not signed provenance; from_rules trusts that name, and tests construct it directly. Rule callbacks merge rule cb_kwargs with request callback kwargs taking precedence; rule copies are shallow and retain nonempty cb_kwargs/link extractor references. The per-response seen set uses full Link equality (URL/text/fragment/nofollow), while extractor dedup defaults to URL; this is neither global idempotency nor account-scoped deduplication.

parse_with_rules follows links in finally even when callback processing raises; async iterator callback results are collected into a list before yielding. Error/cancellation interactions and memory extent are source observations, not a new reproduction. Tests without callbacks are actually consumed; test_parse_with_rules at 337–344 only constructs the async generator and checks no warning, so it cannot prove body execution. Link repr eval and extractor pickle round trips use self-generated synthetic values, not validation of untrusted serialized input. HTML fixture bytes and external lxml/parsel/w3lib semantics remain separate review items. No URL was fetched or parser/test executed here.

Closed generic command tests (980), cmdline tests/settings/extension (73/15/3), project test base (58), crawl tests (222), runspider tests (476). No test execution, imports, generated modules, shell/editor/browser, profiling write or connection occurred here.

Closed spiderloader tests (231), project tests (189), spider utility tests (89) and nine loader fixture files. Duplicate-name tests assert warning plus the resulting set of names; they do not assert which duplicate implementation is retained. Add-on pre-crawler hook changes SPIDER_MODULES and successfully selects its spider, establishing that trusted add-ons influence discovery before crawlers exist. Custom handles_request fixtures restrict to one exact URL, confirming extensible routing logic rather than universal domain enforcement. Project tests explicitly preserve absolute paths and manipulate temporary working/config directories; their test environment overrides do not isolate production subprocess environments. Spider selection tests assert caller defaults even when no or multiple spiders match. Requests in these tests are constructed, not downloaded. No tests were run here.

Completed cmdline (251), command base (359), crawl/runspider commands (33/65), project utility (142), spiderloader (155), base Spider (346) and spider utility (131). Command discovery recursively imports built-ins, loads installed entry points, then project command modules override matching names. Entry-point discovery receives inproject but does not filter requires_project itself, unlike module discovery; this is a source inconsistency requiring its tests, not an authentication bypass claim. Even help/completion discovery can import trusted extensions. runspider executes the supplied Python module before selecting its last discovered named spider; suffix/exists checks do not sandbox code. PID file writes just the process number without a lock/ownership/cleanup protocol.

Base run command sets nonzero exit status for bootstrap_failed or finish_reason_error, not every downloader/item/feed error. Earlier failed-feed tests with finished reason remain relevant: CLI success is not a durable export receipt. -s and -a are trusted operator inputs; Spider updates its instance dictionary with arbitrary keyword arguments and binds the actual crawler/settings afterward. No per-worker account assignment, scopes or operator authentication is implemented by these names/arguments. Duplicate spider names warn and the last loaded class wins, so names are routing labels, not stable authenticated identities. Default start URLs set dont_filter=True, bypassing duplicate filtering rather than granting authorization.

Project detection may import the configured settings module; project data paths accept absolute paths and relative traversal without an owned-root policy. Project discovery skips hidden/venv directories and stops at each scrapy.cfg, a convenience rather than validation of trusted contents. spidercls_for_request selects exactly one matching spider or returns the caller default on ambiguity/absence; domain matching is routing assistance, not enforced network containment. Material command/project/spiderloader tests remain next in the queue. Source only; no CLI, import, filesystem generation or crawl was executed.

## Test destination is not listener confinement

Closed HTTP resources (625) and async test decorators (74). Resources deliberately echo entire headers/body, select redirects from query input, create unfinished responses, and inject HTTP/2 faults; no authentication, workload quotas or tenant binding is added here. The client-IP resource reads the immediate connection address, while ConnectionId numbers transport connections (HTTP/2 through its shared connection), not users/devices/operators. Leaf delayed requests connect notifyFinish failure to Deferred cancellation; several simpler resources schedule raw callbacks directly, so not every fixture delay shares that cleanup path. UriResource explicitly says its CONNECT case fakes a timeout scenario rather than a proper HTTPS proxy; do not infer general proxy correctness from it. Books uses saved HTML bytes, with no upstream fetching in its constructor. Decorators select test execution adapters based on installed reactor state and add no timeout/isolation policy. No fault traffic or test was generated.

Completed mock DNS (89), HTTP route map (134), HTTP process/listener base (185), mockserver TLS/port utilities (55) and test utility module (77). Earlier references to local mock tests describe client destinations, not verified loopback-only binding: HTTP default --host is empty and passed to listenTCP/listenSSL, explicitly documented as every interface; self.host still reports 127.0.0.1. DNS listenUDP omits interface while its parent tells clients 127.0.0.1. Do not reuse these defaults as an isolated-lab harness. No helper/listener was executed, and the existing independently authored loopback lab receipts are unaffected.

HTTP/DNS startup waits on child stdout without a timeout; teardown kills/communicates but startup failure before context entry can leave cleanup incomplete. Child environment copies the whole parent environment and prepends tests to PYTHONPATH: this is not credential or import isolation. Mock DNS resolves any A query to loopback and fails other query types, not a realistic resolver/IPv6 authority test. HTTP route map exposes synthetic status, redirect, arbitrary payload, cookie/header and HTTP/2 fault resources with no authentication wrapper in this file; resource implementations remain the next dependency, not production attack endpoints. TLS helper reads repository test key/certificate paths when called and optionally disables TLS1.3 for cipher tests; those are fixture controls, not a recommended production TLS policy. No key material was read or printed.

## Async task outcomes and timer limits

Completed asyncio utility/tests (379/353), Deferred utility/tests (705/542). Async parallel TaskGroup/semaphore bounds concurrent callables for positive counts, logs ordinary callable exceptions and continues, while iterable errors propagate as ExceptionGroup and cancel cooperative in-flight calls. Tests assert exact successful-item membership, maximum active count, cooperative cancellation and iterable failure; they do not establish a fleet-wide rate budget, credentials or termination of blocking work. Zero count waits on a zero semaphore and negative count raises from asyncio; the Twisted helper has no positive-count validation and its finished Deferred can remain unresolved without workers. These edge cases are source observations, not new runtime receipts. Existing inert parallel/adapter receipts remain unchanged.

LoopingCall uses a monotonic schedule, skips missed boundaries, rejects coroutine/Deferred callback results and stops after ordinary callback exceptions. Fake-time tests assert early/late boundary values rather than real-world watchdog deadlines. stop requests task cancellation without awaiting task completion; run_in_thread delegates to asyncio/Twisted threading and supplies no OS process kill. Context propagation preserves an arbitrary contextvars value; it does not authenticate that value. Deferred/Future conversion delegates cancellation semantics to external Twisted/asyncio implementations, whose exact enforcement is not newly reviewed here. _schedule_coro explicitly retains no task handle, so callers cannot treat its return as a cancellation or delivery receipt.

Rechecked engine caller ranges 140–180 and 435–500 for CallLaterOnce: cancellation occurs when a closing slot has no in-progress requests, while start processing awaits nextcall and explicitly handles cancellation. Thus the helper's retained cancelled _call alone does not prove an engine shutdown hang; normal close stops its heartbeat and start-task cancellation is a separate path. A custom scheduler exception could interrupt helper waiter notification, but full liveness impact is still a source hypothesis requiring a controlled reproduction. Deferred tests exercise socketpair readiness and successful conversion, not every cancellation race. All upstream code/tests here were read only.

## Reactor compatibility and configuration are trusted-process controls

Completed reactor utility (270)/tests (127), reactorless utility (75)/tests (77), settings implementation (737)/tests (1368)/fixture (4). Reactor installation suppresses AlreadyInstalled; separate verification enforces exact reactor class while event-loop verification accepts isinstance. Import paths load trusted code. Reactorless import hook rejects one exact module name through sys.meta_path and its tests establish install/remove/specific-hook behavior; it is a compatibility guard, not a sandbox or a defense against a malicious same-process plugin. Runtime-state detection explicitly differs from early settings and is thread/loop dependent.

Settings priority accepts arbitrary integers, allows equal-priority replacement, and direct deletion ignores priority. Component setters intentionally preserve existing priority while altering entries; none authenticates the caller. freeze only sets this object's flag, setters check it, and __getitem__/get expose stored values. Nested BaseSettings/lists and public attributes therefore remain mutable by trusted in-process code (source fact, no remote input path alleged). Deep copy isolates ordinary nested data; tests mutate the original nested lists and verify the copy. Freeze tests cover setter/pop refusal, not recursive immutability. getdict/getlist make shallow copies, getdictorlist deep-copies dict/list but comma-falls-back even on valid scalar JSON (explicit test for "123"). These conversions are configuration conveniences, not schema validation or immutable security policy.

CallLaterOnce cancels its scheduled call without clearing _call or resolving waiters; __call__ clears _call before invoking the function, but a synchronous function exception skips waiter notification. Potential stranded waiters/rescheduling after cancellation require caller-specific reachability analysis; not a reproduced deadlock or general reactor claim. TCP helper tests use loopback listeners and a synthetic listen error, not authenticated admission. All upstream tests in this section were read only; no listener, reactor, import hook, global event-loop policy or signal handler was installed here.

Closed 23 remaining CrawlerProcess and 22 CrawlerRunner/AsyncCrawlerRunner fixtures (explicit full ranges in ledger). Exact reactor-class rejection includes a SelectReactor subclass fixture, not just different module names. Deferred fixtures attach errbacks; async runner examples directly await crawl tasks. Parallel-versus-sequential examples are empty spiders and parent tests assert log ordering, a scheduling oracle rather than measurable workload isolation. ip_address fixture creates local HTTP and DNS mock servers, maps not.a.real.domain to loopback, and reports response.ip_address: this is destination/peer address observation, not the crawler's outgoing public address, account or device. Its hosts/resolver chain and mockserver implementations remain separate dependencies. All fixture modules were read as text, without top-level execution, service startup or DNS changes.

Closed remaining 29 AsyncCrawlerProcess configuration fixtures (full ranges in queue/continuation-record). Together with the already reviewed four sleeping/resolver/CloseSpider fixture families, these close that directory's inventoried unread paths. Most fixtures execute process.start at module scope and are unsafe to import merely for inspection; only source text was read. They use no-request/data-URL spiders for loop compatibility, explicit uvloop type selection, the same/different installed loop, and sequential import-hook cleanup. Reactorless custom-spider settings and non-asyncio reactor fixtures attach task.result callbacks to log startup failure, reinforcing that retaining task outcomes matters.
