First aggregated output was truncated; affected final URL tail, complete URL encoder and UTF-8 ranges were reread separately before credit. No fixture or Go test was executed.

## Corpus oracles and integration profiles

Completed the engine transformation profile (117) and six supporting corpora: Base64 decode/ext/encode (45/45/38), hex decode/encode (38/24), HTML entity decode (23). The engine profile explicitly changes expected triggered/nontriggered MD5-dependent rules according to FIPS mode, corroborating the source-described enforcement difference; the multiMatch profile checks transaction match counters for changed versus unchanged stages. These are reviewed oracles, not executed results.

Rechecked the complete corpus runner (115). It looks up each case by its name, not by containing filename, and silently returns when lookup fails. Therefore the hex corpus cases named invalidCharacter and invalidLen do not exercise hexDecode under this runner. The dedicated hex_decode_test.go does call hexDecode directly and covers malformed inputs; keep those two evidence scopes separate. The HTML entity corpus contains only empty/plain/NUL passthrough cases, so it does not test any actual entity decoding. The runner generally ignores ret flags except for two path transforms and ignores file-read and string-unquote errors. Corpus presence alone is not semantic coverage, and no corpus was executed.

## Hash availability and Unicode representation

Completed MD5/SHA-1 implementations/tests (49/74 and 57/74). Both transformations remain registered, but return the prior value and an explicit error when fips140.Enabled is true. Combined with the reviewed evaluator, rule loading can succeed while evaluation logs and continues with the prior value and later transforms; this is not automatic rejection or disabling of the rule. Tests assert passthrough/error versus digest vectors according to their current process mode, not an executed matrix here. Comments about cryptographic approval are upstream commentary, not independently verified current standards guidance. These unkeyed digest transforms are not request authentication, password protection or workload identity.

Completed URL Unicode decoder/tests (149/117), URL encoder/tests (44/68), full best-fit table (394), and UTF-8-to-Unicode/tests (67/78). urlDecodeUni recognizes one pass of percent bytes, plus-to-space and four-digit IIS-style Unicode escapes; it maps selected best-fit characters, otherwise truncates to a byte, preserving malformed sequences. The table explicitly excludes general cross-script confusables; its mapping is neither Unicode identity normalization nor a device/person discriminator. The Unicode encoder emits at least four hex digits and tests a supplementary character as a five-digit escape, whereas the decoder consumes exactly four. Composition is therefore not a universal round trip, even before best-fit information loss; no runtime composition oracle was run. URL encoding works per byte with a small allow set and plus for space, not a universal URI/path/component serializer. No external URI, account identifier or endpoint was tested.

## Comment handling, case and length

Completed removeComments/source tests (67/92), removeCommentsChar (47), replaceComments/source tests (48/55), length/source tests (14/51), lower/upper case/source tests (16/70 each), and none (10). removeComments is a state machine across selected C/HTML/line delimiters without quoted-string or language context. After a block close it copies and skips the next byte directly; when the close is at end, that byte is the added NUL pad. The tests explicitly expect a NUL output for comment-only inputs. Adjacent delimiter handling therefore deserves a dedicated semantic oracle, rather than assuming a general language parser. removeCommentsChar drops delimiter tokens while retaining their former contents; replaceComments replaces only C-style comments with a space. These functions have different token-boundary effects and are not sanitizers.

Length returns decimal byte length, with Japanese fixture expecting 21, not a count of characters or graphemes; its always-true changed flag has an acknowledged equal-output corner case commented out of the tests. Case transforms delegate to Go strings.ToLower/ToUpper, unlike cmdLine ASCII-only folding; their dedicated fixtures are ASCII, not multilingual account-identifier equivalence. none itself is identity, while its special clearing of prior configured transforms belongs to the already traced parser. No source test here proves authenticated identity, rule accuracy or downstream parser equivalence, and none was executed.

## Language escape decoders

Completed CSS decoder/tests (133/107), generic escape decoder/tests (120/121), JavaScript decoder/tests (189/156), and HTML entity wrapper (14). CSS resolves up to six hex digits into a full UTF-8 code point, replaces zero/invalid code points, folds fullwidth ASCII and consumes one selected whitespace byte after an escape. Regression tests assert non-ASCII and supplementary characters and zero-value output growth; there is no browser parser equivalence test. Generic escape decoding uses byte values and wraps three-digit octal values to their low byte. JavaScript decoding instead stops overflowing octal sequences after two digits, and truncates classic and extended Unicode escapes to their low byte with a fullwidth-ASCII special case. The tests explicitly assert these distinct semantics. Thus a function named JavaScript decoder is not proof of full Unicode equivalence with a JavaScript engine. Neither decoder executes the content.

Malformed escapes commonly lose the backslash rather than raise an error; a lone trailing backslash differs between these decoders. Their effects depend on transformation order and the real application language/context. HTML entities delegate to x/net/html.UnescapeString, an exact external dependency still to review; the thin wrapper is not an HTML sanitizer. All observations are source/test-oracle review, without executing Go, browsers, scripts or endpoint traffic.

## Whitespace and command-like canonicalization

Completed cmdLine/source tests (98/83), compressWhitespace/source tests (83/152), removeWhitespace/source tests (26/43), removeNulls (15), replaceNulls (12), trim (22), trimLeft/right (12 each). cmdLine is a byte-oriented canonicalizer: it removes selected punctuation, collapses selected delimiters and lowercases ASCII only. It does not parse or execute a shell command. Tests assert changed flags for no-op candidate characters and selected transformations; its fuzz oracle checks absence of selected output characters, not semantic equivalence with an interpreter.

compressWhitespace decodes full UTF-8 runes, preserves unrelated multibyte characters and invalid bytes, treats bare Latin-1 NBSP specially, and collapses only its explicit Latin whitespace set. Dedicated regression fixtures cover accented characters, Chinese text, genuine NEL/NBSP and malformed bytes and check both value and changed flag. removeWhitespace instead delegates rune mapping to unicode.IsSpace; trim variants use only six ASCII whitespace characters. These operations have different character domains and cannot be interchanged as universal canonicalization. removeWhitespace tests cover only ASCII samples; malformed UTF-8 handling through strings.Map and possible output changes without its whitespace-only changed flag need a dedicated oracle before any runtime claim. Null removal joins surrounding bytes, whereas null replacement inserts spaces; neither proves the downstream parser sees the same token boundaries. No Go tests or application requests were run.

## Decoder normalization and error propagation

Completed Base64 decoder/source tests (125/222), forgiving decoder/source tests (11/148), Base64 encoder (15), hex decoder/tests (20/102) and hex encoder (15). The ordinary decoder returns a decoded prefix with nil error, stops at the first invalid character or padding, ignores CR/LF and permits omitted padding. The forgiving variant skips invalid bytes and ASCII whitespace and accepts URL alphabet substitutions; neither operation validates a signed token or authenticates its claimed account. Its normalization semantics must match the application parser before a rule can claim equivalent inspection.

The fuzz oracle compares against the standard decoder only when that decoder succeeds and input has no newline. That is a useful restricted equivalence oracle, not proof of equivalence on the intentionally permissive input domain. Tests include malformed and truncated inputs, but no application parser, HTTP request or Go suite was executed. Hex decoding returns an error on invalid or odd input; the previously traced rule evaluator logs transformation errors and retains the preceding value, so the helper returning an empty string on error does not establish that the effective inspected value becomes empty. Encoders provide representation changes, not confidentiality or identity separation.


## Regex filtering, captures and compiled-key boundaries

Completed URL-encoding validator/test (82/52), UTF-8 validator (48), byte-range validator/test (110/61), identifier-format validator/test (167/53), prefix/suffix/contains/equality/within operators (57/57/57/53/56), numeric ge/gt/le/lt (58/58/57/59), eq test (54) and noMatch (44). Encoding checks report a boolean violation for the selected input; percent syntax validity explicitly accepts encoded NUL, and UTF-8 validity is not Unicode normalization, semantic safety or identity. Byte-range construction validates endpoint bounds but not descending order; empty configuration returns an unconditional matcher, whereas ordinary empty inspected data returns no violation. The small byte tests cover accepted samples, not these constructor edge cases.

String operators expand macros and perform plain case-sensitive comparisons. @within uses substring containment of input inside the parameter string, not tokenized set membership despite the comment's comma-list examples. Numeric operators ignore Atoi errors; the eq test explicitly expects unrelated nonnumeric strings to compare equal as zero and distinguishes decimal syntax from integers. These are policy-language semantics, not typed validation or authentication. noMatch returns false; its comment's suggestion that associated match actions still run must be judged against the already traced evaluator, not assumed from the example.

The national-identifier operator checks a country-specific format/checksum-like function on up to ten regex matches and captures matching text; it does not verify issuance, ownership, consent, account binding or real-world identity. No personal identifier was created or queried. Chile helper checks original length before stripping characters, then slices the stripped value without a post-strip nonempty guard; a permissive trusted regex reaching a punctuation-only match is a source hypothesis for an invalid slice, not a reproduced crash or a remote endpoint test. The US helper is a local heuristic, not verified against current issuance rules, and the reviewed dedicated file has no US validation oracle. Synthetic format fixtures are not identity proof.

Completed schema validator/default/TinyGo/tests (170/18/28/620). Unlike name-only phrase datasets, schema compilation keys include a schema namespace and SHA-256 of schema bytes; tests check equal/different content keys. The wrapper reads a .json file from configured RootFS and delegates compilation/validation to kaptinlin/jsonschema (declared v0.4.6). Exact compiler/reference resolution, format semantics and concurrency remain external dependencies; do not infer a network sandbox from this wrapper. No schema compiler or remote reference was executed.

Evaluation prefers stored response JSON from phase 4 onward, otherwise stored request JSON from phase 2 onward, then falls back to the supplied string. Empty supplied data with no stored data returns false (no violation), while a nil internal schema returns true from isValidJSON before any syntax validation. The nil-schema test's comment says basic JSON validation, but only uses valid JSON and does not establish rejection of malformed JSON in that branch. Phase tests use a mocked transaction with manually populated TX maps, not the actual body processor or HTTP connector, and do not cover missing-response-data fallback to a present request body. These are selection/availability semantics to account for in response-validation policy, not proof of universal body validation. TinyGo explicitly returns unconditionalMatch and its test asserts that behavior. Schema conformity and an application's authorization of a claimed account/object remain separate.

Completed phrase matcher/source tests (114/141), dataset/source test (56/38), and file matcher (76), rechecking dataset construction at directives.go 1736–1760 and rule_parser.go 175–215. A material isolation finding is now traced through the normal parser: pmFromDataset resolves this WAF's dataset bytes but memoizes only by dataset name; parser passes WAF.Memoizer, and that memoizer is process-global by key with owners tracking lifetime only. Two coexisting WAFs using the same dataset name but different contents can therefore receive the first cached matcher, while the second wrapper computes minLen from its own different dataset. This is a source-established policy-key omission, not observed account-data leakage or a runtime reproduction. The direct dataset test passes no memoizer and covers only one dataset plus absent-name failure; it does not test this sharing path.

The file matcher key uses joined search paths plus filename, omitting RootFS identity and content/version; it also ignores Scanner.Err, loads one complete argument as one path despite the comment's multiple-file claim, and has no automatic refresh. Same-location/different-root or changed-content lifetime behavior needs an isolated compile-only oracle before any runtime claim. Plain pm lowercases/splits on literal spaces, uses ASCII-insensitive matching, and contains a TODO for Snort syntax despite the description claiming support. Its cache key is also raw data, not an operator/type namespace. Matching is substring-based, can capture up to ten matches and is not authentication of a user-agent/operator. Exact Aho-Corasick behavior remains an external dependency. No matcher package, corpus, external traffic or scanner was executed.

Completed REST path operator/tests (75/144), substring operator/tests (58/67) and macro compiler/tests (182/200). REST templates replace placeholders with named groups but do not add begin/end anchors or quote all remaining regex metacharacters. The tests explicitly accept an extra trailing path segment while capturing the preceding ID, so a successful @restpath match is not exact router equivalence or an authorization check. Captured path IDs are request-derived values; they require independent authenticated subject/object authorization. Negative incomplete/empty-segment tests establish those particular shapes, not all application route semantics.

Macros tokenize trusted strings and read transaction collections on expansion, lowercasing keyed selectors and selecting the first available value. A missing key logs and returns the token's internal text (without the original percent-brace delimiters). This differs from treating missing information as an empty or validated identity. Tests mainly assert compile/token behavior and one literal expansion; they do not cover all collection-specific missing-key, multi-value, case-sensitive-build or authorization uses. Strmatch is case-sensitive substring matching after macro expansion, with fixed-string tests; success says nothing about a claimed user-agent string being truthful. Macro expansion is neither escaping nor authentication, and no requests or templates were executed here.

Completed the remaining comparison benchmark file (151). It compares helper prefilter-plus-regex against regex-only over explicit short strings and logs whether a prefilter exists. Both paths discard match results, and it does not evaluate transaction captures, actual HTTP rules, concurrent configuration reloads, field accuracy or end-to-end throughput. Branch-comparison comments refer to historical main/branch designs, not two revisions acquired and measured in this research. No benchmark receipt exists, and no performance advantage is asserted here.

Completed `rxprefilter_crs_test.go` 1–627. It is build-tagged, parses only REQUEST-*.conf lines containing explicit @rx, skips missing/duplicate IDs and syntax failures, and extracts URI/data payloads through a line-oriented YAML subset rather than the full FTW request/response pipeline. Directory/file read failures in payload collection are silently skipped. The safety test checks only shouldFire payloads for rules with a nonnil prefilter and has no checked>0 assertion; it can log zero false negatives after checking zero payloads. This verifies the prefilter helper's necessary-condition relation to the regex on whatever subset was loaded, not application protection, actual rule-chain/transformation behavior, the rx.Evaluate exact-literal capture branch or measured bot detection accuracy.

The benchmark appends every extracted attack payload then nine copies of a ten-item benign list per rule. Its stated 9:1 ratio therefore is not enforced: the actual ratio depends on each rule's attack count (90 benign samples versus however many attacks were extracted). 'Reflects production workloads' and PL1 false-positive claims are commentary, not a measured cohort here. The top comment says per-category median, but the code aggregates nested loops without computing a median. CRS module bytes are not yet semantically reviewed; module presence and this parser's counts would not supply that review. No CRS/fuzz/benchmark execution occurred.

Completed rx.go (261), regex multiline build variants (9/9), prefilter defaults (12/13), ordinary/no-multiline regex tests (245/120), prefilter implementation (1245) and main tests (1874). Prefilter defaults Off unless selected by build tag/directive. Ordinary regex prepends dotall and normally multiline flags; binary-byte routing calls a separate binary-regexp implementation with the original arguments. Standard compiled-cache keys include the prefilter flag and wrapped pattern, protecting different WAF settings from that specific sharing collision. Binary compilation uses the unprefixed raw pattern as its key; other regexp callers also use raw keys. Their cross-type key compatibility is an outstanding source hypothesis requiring a safe compile-only oracle/exact dependency review, not an observed crash.

The optimization rejects short inputs, then tests required literals, then either uses literal equality for selected anchored patterns or invokes the regex. Full implementation review covers AST minimum-length/literal/alternative extraction, trie reconstruction, anchor adjacency, Unicode/invalid-byte fallbacks, indexed matching, and separate exact-match extraction. The header's claim that an extraction bug can never cause a false negative is an intended invariant, not a proof about arbitrary bugs. Comments claiming 99% ASCII traffic, superiority over other matchers and timing gains are upstream assertions/benchmark rationale, not our measured workload evidence.

Tests include actual regex-reference comparisons, capture contents for selected non-exact patterns, binary routing, invalid patterns, Unicode-fold inputs, synthetic unreachable AST nodes and a 100-goroutine test that does await all goroutines. Fuzzers compare against regex truth, unlike the earlier SQLi/XSS crash-only fuzzers, but explicitly cap arbitrary pattern/input lengths (256/512 bytes) and fixed-pattern inputs (128 bytes). Merely reading them establishes neither that fuzzing ran nor universal equivalence. The test named MemoizeSharesPrefilter supplies no Memoizer and checks output/metadata rather than pointer sharing. The threshold-boundary comment says 16/256, while the implementation uses anyRequiredMaxN=256 and that test exercises 16/64, so do not infer actual threshold coverage from its title.

One narrow source discrepancy remains: the exact-literal early return in rx.Evaluate precedes Capturing and CaptureField, so boolean equality can skip the normal full-match/group population. extractExactMatch unwraps outer captures. Main capture tests reviewed here do not use this exact-equality shape. This is a capture-semantics hypothesis, not a demonstrated denial bypass; remaining CRS/comparison tests and downstream capture-dependent actions still need review. Likewise the byte-minimum calculation precedes the Unicode fallback, so preservation of all folding-related byte-length cases needs more than the listed ASCII-pattern/non-ASCII-input examples. No Go runtime, fuzzing, benchmark or hostile request was run.

## Address-list matching and policy loading

Completed inspectFile/default/TinyGo/tests (64/21/85), SQLi wrapper/fuzzer (52/31), and XSS wrapper/fuzzer (47/32). Ordinary inspectFile starts a trusted configured executable directly with the inspected value as one argument, captures combined stdout/stderr, returns false on process error/deadline, and otherwise matches nonempty output whose first byte is not '1'. Its description contradicts itself about that polarity; implementation and explicit output table agree on non-'1'. It provides a ten-second context, but no local output-byte cap, aggregate process-concurrency budget or demonstrated descendant-process cleanup in this file. These are integration responsibilities, not proof of safe scanner containment. Tests use echo/tasklist rather than an antivirus, and the output table skips Windows. TinyGo returns unconditionalMatch. No executable or scanner fixture was run in this research step.

SQLi/XSS operators delegate to libinjection-go (declared v0.3.4), with SQLi storing a fingerprint on a match. The reviewed fuzzers seed inputs and discard the result: they can reveal crashes during execution, but contain no correct-classification, false-positive/negative or handler-denial oracle. No fuzzing was run here. A content-attack classifier is also not a bot/operator identity detector; detector accuracy and client authentication remain separate. Exact libinjection implementation review and labeled defensive cohorts remain outstanding; no measured field accuracy is claimed.

Completed RBL/default/TinyGo/tests (109/19/89), geoLookup (38) and unconditionalMatch (42). Ordinary RBL concatenates input and configured service into a DNS name, uses the default resolver, and returns false on lookup error or its 500ms outer timeout. It does not reverse an address or validate input in this operator; its mock-DNS tests use labels rather than real IPv4/IPv6 reputation queries. Lookup-host success leads to TXT lookup, whose error also returns false; the test distinguishes mocked TXT metadata, not real service classification or accuracy. Such a query boundary discloses the queried input to the resolver/service and requires an explicit privacy/availability policy in an application; no DNS or external service was contacted here.

The timeout cancels context on return but the goroutine sends on an unbuffered result channel without selecting cancellation. After the timeout branch has returned, a later send has no receiver; this is a source-established abandoned-send path, not a measured goroutine-leak rate. A successful late lookup can also write transaction metadata before sending; interaction with transaction reuse/Close is an unvalidated lifetime hypothesis. Existing tests induce no timeout and do not count goroutines or test post-Close writes. TinyGo's RBL factory instead returns unconditionalMatch, and geoLookup is likewise an unconditional stub that does not populate a database-backed location. A deny rule attached to such a stub differs materially from an actual reputation/geolocation decision. Build-specific availability and rule action must be checked before any deployment claim; no local or production policy was changed.

Completed Coraza IP matcher/dataset/file implementations and tests (78/55, 52/53, 68/43), file loader/test (48/86), OS filesystem adapter (57), and the exact five-line netranges.dat fixture. The .dat extension was absent from the supporting inventory; it was fully read and appended with its pin/hash, preserving the original main inventory and existing entries. Supporting queue is now 544 entries, 26 complete, 518 unread.

The constructor trims comma-separated entries, adds individual-address masks and silently skips net.ParseCIDR errors, returning nil error even if no valid subnet remains. The dataset variant rejects absent/empty datasets, but a nonempty malformed dataset can still reduce to an empty matcher through the same constructor. Evaluation compares a parsed input address against stored subnets; it supplies no operator/account/device authentication. Tests cover a small explicit IPv4 allow/deny set and an empty dataset; they do not establish IPv6 normalization, malformed-policy diagnostics, attribution accuracy, freshness or shared-network false-positive rates. An address list is policy input whose effects depend on rule action and the connector's trusted source-address extraction.

The file variant loads the complete file, scans trimmed non-comment lines and delegates to the same matcher. It does not inspect Scanner.Err after the loop, so a scanner failure can leave a successfully constructed prefix-only matcher; this is a source path, not an induced runtime result. Lists are compiled at initialization, not continuously refreshed/revoked per request. The loader tries configured search directories for a relative path and stops on the first success or non-not-found error; absolute paths delegate directly to fs.ReadFile. OSFS explicitly implements raw OS access, accepting paths outside compliant relative fs.FS semantics; RootFS is an abstraction and its confinement depends on the supplied implementation. No policy file on the host was changed or external address tested. The read fixture contains only loopback, one private CIDR and a comment, and all reviewed tests are source-only.

## Build-selected rule interpretation and evaluation

Completed normalisePath (26), normalisePathWin/tests (142/119), urlDecode (68), and string-helper tests (209), rechecking all 145 helper implementation lines. Supporting corpus files urlDecode (136), normalisePath (241) and normalisePathWin (234) are fully read. Slash normalization uses path.Clean on every host, preserves non-root trailing slash and unresolved leading relative parents; it is lexical inspection normalization, not an allow-root check, symlink resolution or safe file opener. Windows normalization additionally converts backslashes, strips the final component's stream suffix and selected trailing dots/spaces, retaining a drive prefix. Tests assert strings/changed flags, not calls to Windows/IIS filesystem APIs. Upstream platform-semantics comments are not reproduced host equivalence measurements.

URL decoding is one pass, converts plus to space, preserves invalid/incomplete percent escapes and returns nil error. Its changed flag becomes true upon seeing any percent/plus, even if an invalid sequence leaves the text unchanged; corpus ret expectations for that distinction are not asserted by the runner outside path transforms. X2c relies on callers' length/hex checks, and WrapUnsafe requires the backing byte slice not be mutated afterwards. The reviewed URL caller allocates its own byte slice and does not mutate after wrapping; this does not establish every caller's lifetime. Helper tests cover quote/regex delimiter parsing, while RandomStringConcurrency launches 5000 goroutines without waiting for them and asserts no output/uniqueness. RandomString uses math/rand/v2 and is described as pseudorandom; a transaction identifier generated here is not a secret credential or authenticated principal. No high-load or filesystem experiment was executed.

Completed experimental action/operator/transformation registration wrappers and tests (20/27, 16/27, 16/26), action/operator/transformation interfaces (34/45/12), empty directive plugin file (5), and internal transformation registry/test runner (66/115). Registration overwrites existing names in process-global registries; these APIs accept trusted executable callbacks and impose no plugin sandbox or tenant authorization. The action/operator registration tests deliberately register factories returning nil and assert only no lookup error, so lookup success alone is not evidence of a usable implementation. Transformations normalize registry names to lowercase. Their interface explicitly says errors are for logging and do not stop rule execution, consistent with the reviewed evaluator accumulating errors and retaining the prior value. The transformation corpus runner ignores file-read/unquote errors and returns silently when a transformation name is unavailable, and only validates changed flags for two path-normalization names. Corpus size and a green test title therefore cannot stand in for review of each registered transformation and its actual cases; supporting JSON cases remain unread until separately annotated.

Supporting reads complete `internal/memoize/README.md` 1–20 and `go.mod` 1–56. The selected snapshot declares Go 1.26.0, gjson 1.18.0, x/sync 0.23.0 and CRS 4.30.0; these are local dependency declarations, not a claim about latest upstream releases or installed host versions. Completed experimental WAF interface/example (36/36): explicit Close is exposed through experimental.WAFCloser and forwarded by the already reviewed public wrapper. The example sets a transaction ID and prints it; caller-selected transaction IDs are not authentication or uniqueness guarantees. No finalizer/automatic owner release was established by the inspected construction/Close paths. README recommends explicit close on reload or disabling memoization; tests named WithClose invoke memoize.Release directly and do not independently establish that every host connector calls WAF.Close.

Completed ctl tests (634) and all memoizer implementation/test variants: sync 115/340, TinyGo 91/336, no-op 22/207; rechecked WAF construction 350–372 and Close 491–505. Control tests verify phase guards, field updates, invalid-input logs, ID/range/regex parsing. Several table rows intentionally have no checkTX assertion and only establish that direct initialization/evaluation returns without a checked failure; no full request exclusion guarantee should be attributed to those rows.

Compiled-pattern memoization is a separate process-global cache from the per-transaction transformation cache discussed above. Its key determines sharing across WAF owners; numeric owner IDs track lifetime, not tenant access permission. Successful values are shared, errors are not cached; ordinary Go singleflight coalesces same-key calls, while TinyGo omits singleflight. WAF construction allocates an incrementing owner ID and explicit once-only WAF.Close releases it; in-flight references can outlive release. There is no TTL/entry cap in these implementations, so orderly ownership release and bounded trusted configuration matter. Tests cover sequential sharing, failed-call retry and explicit Release/Reset, not concurrent Do-versus-Release races. The ordinary close-scale test additionally requires CORAZA_MEMOIZE_SCALE=1, and short mode skips scale tests. These files do not establish that distinct tenant configuration automatically receives distinct cache keys or that callbacks are interchangeable merely because their keys match. No cache race/scale/benchmark runtime was executed, and x/sync singleflight remains an exact external dependency to inspect.

Completed redirect/action test (66/27), exec/action test (55/23) and block initializer test (26). Redirect likewise only constructs an interruption containing status/target; the reviewed Go HTTP wrapper neither translates this action to its redirect status nor writes a Location header on the interruption path. The initializer test stores a target but does not exercise a client-visible redirect. Exec's implementation is an empty Evaluate and rejects nonempty initializer arguments, despite a lengthy comment describing external programs and CGI environment variables. No program was executed: this is another concrete instance where documented action vocabulary exceeds enforced implementation at the selected pin. The block test is initializer-only and adds no end-to-end denial evidence beyond the separately traced parser/default-action path.

Completed allow/action initializer test (74/39), deny initializer test (23), and drop/action initializer test (62/23), tracing the existing rule-group allow switches at 225–276 and HTTP middleware 125–174. Allow phase resets at phase end; allow request skips request phases; allow all skips the rule loop. These trusted rule decisions are not client/workload authentication. The Drop action's implementation only creates an interruption with Action=drop; its comment's TCP-FIN claim is explicitly connector-dependent. In this pinned Go HTTP wrapper the status helper recognizes only Action=deny and otherwise uses the supplied default; a request interruption returns before invoking the handler but supplies default HTTP 200 for drop. No socket-close/FIN operation exists in this reviewed wrapper path. Thus request rejection of application execution and a TCP connection drop are separate claims. The Drop test only checks initializer arguments; it does not exercise transport behavior. Source-established connector behavior, not an observed TCP test.

Completed `rule_test.go` 1–983. Direct evaluator tests use dummy operators/actions to distinguish matching, target exclusions, whole-collection exclusions, flow actions in DetectionOnly, non-disruptive match actions and suppression of inner-chain disruptive actions. DetectionOnly therefore does not imply that all rule code/state changes are inert. Transformation tests exercise ordered and multimatch outputs, accumulated errors, prefix sharing, t:none identity reset, repeated-key separation by value, and clearing a transaction's cache on Close. They include counted transformation calls and 2000 repeated-key iterations, stronger than checking cache presence alone, but were not run here. TX/count/combined-size targets are explicitly expected to avoid cache reuse. This is per-transaction inspection optimization; it does not supply account separation, global duplicate-effect prevention or authorization. Macro/capture tests additionally verify propagation after the whole chain, rather than matching a title alone. Exact imported transformation/operator implementations still require their own review; dummy success does not prove them.

Completed Coraza mandatory-ID variants/test (9/9/44), case-sensitive argument variants/test (9/9/23), rule-group tests (120), multiphase variants (12/9), multiphase implementation/test (418/115). Rechecked already complete RuleGroup.Add at 24–69. Mandatory nonzero IDs are opt-in through a build tag; default still rejects duplicate nonzero IDs but permits missing IDs. SecMarker rules are exempt in the mandatory branch. Rule IDs organize rules and audit attribution, not authenticated user/workload identities. RuleGroup explicitly says mutation after compilation is not concurrency-safe. Deletion tests assert exact-case tag/message matching, preservation of absent messages and inclusive ID ranges.

Case-sensitive argument-key handling and multiphase evaluation are also build-selected, false by default. The tagged case test checks preservation of one selector's spelling, not all collection lookups. Multiphase splits ARGS/ARGS_NAMES into GET/POST variables and infers availability phases; chain processing delays earlier variables until the chain is available and tracks prior matches to suppress repeated actions. The implementation compares variable/value combinations for prior-chain membership; this is local evaluation bookkeeping, not external-effect idempotency or cross-request deduplication. Chain combinations are generated recursively without a local combination budget in this helper; resource behavior depends on callers, configured chains and input matches, and was not measured here. Tests focus on split selectors/exceptions; several two-selector checks use logical AND, allowing one incorrect selector to escape that particular assertion. This file alone does not prove exactly-once actions across all phases. Its InboundDataError 'not populated' comment is stale relative to the already reviewed transaction and body-limit tests, demonstrating why comments cannot substitute for the setter/enforcement path.

## HTTP streaming, protocol handoff and body-limit oracles

Completed `config.go` 1–226, `config_test.go` 1–113 and `internal/seclang/body_test.go` 1–54. The configuration clone copies its rules slice, but the MIME slice and supplied rule/logger/filesystem objects are shared references. WithResponseBodyMimeTypes stores the provided slice directly, so the interface's immutability comment is narrower than deep isolation from caller-owned mutable objects; no concurrency/runtime mutation experiment was run. The config test checks independent appended directives and expected rule matches/callback, not every referenced object's immutability or request denial. The seclang body-access-Off test is fully commented out; its active On case checks parsed arguments, not prevention. Setter presence and a successful configuration construction are not evidence that a hostile request was rejected.

Completed JSON helper/process/integration tests (736/370/116), rechecked all 334 lines of the previously completed JSON processor. It preserves colliding flattened/differently cased values with append operations, bounds leaf values and array-length summaries with separate counters, and budgets retained key/value bytes at max(8 times body length, 4096). This byte budget excludes Go map/slice/string headers, transient buffers, raw body retention and other transaction storage: it is not a measured total-memory cap. Best-effort flattening precedes final JSON validity checking; the iterative depth scan before gjson.Valid covers a deeply nested tail skipped by argument truncation. Partial collections remain available when a processor error is returned, while the raw TX body is stored only after successful readJSON. Argument truncation can still be a successful processor return and relies on downstream rules for rejection.

Tests explicitly cover key collisions, repeated values, separate array-summary limits, byte amplification errors, partial inspection of malformed JSON, reader errors and depth limits. Their integration label means processor-to-transaction-variable integration, not an HTTP connector/handler-denial test. The process tests named InvalidJSONReturnsError use zero recursion options and only assert a nonnil error, which can be satisfied by a recursion guard; the separate positive-depth best-effort tests establish the malformed-JSON case more specifically. The generic helper table only compares an expected error when an error actually occurs; empty expected-map/error rows can otherwise pass nil, while dedicated negative-depth and invalid-input tests provide stronger oracles. Source comments quoting amplification/timing are upstream observations, not reproduced benchmarks. No oversized fixture, benchmark or Go runtime test was executed here; imported gjson behavior remains a dependency to review rather than an assumed universal guarantee.

Completed XML processor/tests (76/123), multipart processor/internal tests/external tests (561/282/929), and filesystem environment variants/tests (29/15/45), rechecking transaction cleanup at 1709–1745. XML uses a non-strict standard-library decoder with HTML entity/autoclose settings and accepts unexpected-EOF syntax termination. It collects attribute values and nonempty text into two broad collections; its options, including argument/recursion limits, are unused in this implementation. Tests expect partial text recovery, not strict XML validation, full XPath support or a bounded-depth guarantee. The exact standard-library decoder is an outstanding dependency; no inference about external entity behavior is promoted to a runtime result.

Multipart exposes multiple filename interpretations rather than treating one normalization as authoritative for every application. It retains repeated-part filenames, flags duplicate/invalid parameter encodings, and deliberately exposes charset/language without transcoding or grammar validation. Its options.ArgumentLimit is not read in the reviewed file: unlike URLENCODED's early pair bound, ordinary fields are read and appended directly. This source finding requires outer body/resource controls and suitable rules; no host memory measurement or live endpoint validation was performed. InboundDataError suppresses unexpected-EOF strict-error tagging for WAF-truncated partial inspection; this is intentional acceptance of an incomplete inspection window, not evidence the complete downstream body is harmless.

On filesystem-enabled builds each file part is copied to a generated temporary filename, closed immediately and recorded before copy/close errors are handled. Close removes recorded files unless configured keep/relevant-only conditions retain them; removal errors are collected, so cleanup success is not guaranteed merely by calling Close. The copy-error test checks one recorded existing file and performs its own cleanup; it does not invoke transaction Close. The descriptor test samples /proc/self/fd and skips non-Linux hosts, so it supplies no Windows descriptor measurement. Several older malformed-input tests (156–269) check flags only if the parser returns an error, allowing a nil result to pass without asserting rejection. The explicit filename/duplicate tables assert exact metadata, not application acceptance or a rule-denial result.

The no_fs_access build variant sets an internal flag and panics if its writability helper is called; the ordinary variant probes by creating/closing/removing a temporary file. This is a build/runtime branch, not an operating-system filesystem sandbox. The helper's deferred close/removal errors are ignored, and the test mutates TMPDIR without restoring it. No environment or filesystem permission was changed during this review.

Body-processor closure completes plugin registration/interface (17/42 lines), the internal registry (32), RAW and test (44/35), URLENCODED and test (55/161), and internal URL parser and test (145/150), with `transaction.go` 1135–1187 rechecked. ArgumentLimit bounds total parsed pairs, including repeated keys, before allocating additional map/list entries; trailing empty separators do not falsely mark truncation. URLENCODED first copies the supplied reader into a string, retains the full request body, and marks ARGUMENTS_LIMIT_REACHED when pair parsing truncates. Thus this is a pair-materialization bound, not an independent body-byte bound or automatic request rejection. RAW likewise copies its reader and implements no internal byte cap; the enclosing transaction/buffer controls remain necessary. Zero/nonpositive parser limits mean unlimited. Tests explicitly check distinct/repeated-key truncation and metadata, not memory use or application/WAF interpretation equivalence. Non-strict unescaping preserves malformed percent sequences and maps plus to space, so connector and application normalization agreement matters; no external requests were generated.

The processor interface explicitly permits implementations to ignore options. Registry names are overwritten on registration; lookup lowercases names, while registration stores the supplied spelling. This is process-level plugin trust/configuration, not permission isolation. Missing/failed processors generate request-body error variables and evaluate phase-2 rules; they do not inherently return a Go error/deny to the connector in those branches. Successful URLENCODED truncation also reaches normal phase-2 evaluation. Enforcement therefore requires applicable rules and engine mode as well as configuration. No automatic-deny claim follows merely from a limit/error flag being present. Registry concurrency and external Go library behavior were not runtime tested.

Completed Coraza `http/streaming_test.go` 1–424, `http/websocket_test.go` 1–195 and `http/interceptor_test.go` 1–1267 at the existing pin. Rechecked the already complete interceptor 19–185 and 227–268 against these tests. No Go code, listener or upstream tests were executed here.

Streaming cases inspect both the underlying Flush spy and receipt of the first chunk before a deliberate server delay, covering engine Off, body access Off and MIME exclusion. A phase-3 response-header deny case expects 403, an empty response and no delegated flush. These are response-delivery oracles: the wrapped application handler has already begun and continues running, so they do not establish prevention or rollback of its side effects. The raw HTTP/1.0 case (328–422) has a weaker final oracle: it accepts n==0 on the second read, because the world-string comparison is conditional on n>0. The readFirstN timeout returns without cancelling the blocked reader goroutine itself; response-body closure supplies eventual transport cleanup. Comments calling behavior buggy are historical commentary, not measured failures at this pin.

WebSocket tests cover both WriteHeader(101)-then-Hijack and direct-Hijack upgrade patterns, a phase-1 request-header rejection, and subsequent benign/denied ordinary HTTP requests using the same WAF. The subsequent requests are separate HTTP requests/connections, not malicious frames on the upgraded stream. Successful Hijack marks the interceptor and skips its final HTTP response processing; a failed Hijack leaves response processing active, verified with sentinel status values. The handoff/echo fixtures establish compatibility and per-request state separation, not WAF inspection of ongoing WebSocket messages. Applications need protocol-aware controls for that separate traffic boundary. SHA-1 handshake tests explicitly skip under the checked FIPS mode; do not equate an unrun/skipped case with protection.

The body-limit table (860–995) covers one-shot Content-Length and three flushed chunks. Its exact-equal Reject case explicitly expects 500 despite the documented desired 200; this is an upstream regression expectation, not our runtime observation. ProcessPartial can return the entire original response when the partial inspection window does not contain the complete match (920–924, 982–989); it is neither full-body inspection nor response truncation. OUTBOUND_DATA_ERROR tests distinguish a partial-mode phase-4 rule's 413 from immediate Reject's 500. INBOUND_DATA_ERROR tests similarly distinguish partial-mode phase-2 rule's 400 from immediate Reject's 413. They assert status codes, not a counted handler-entry or durable-effect oracle. A body-limit interruption occurring before the rule phase is distinct from that rule enforcing a deny; configured access, selected MIME, engine mode, limit action and connector behavior all matter.

## Pause/resume and observation closure

Metrics collection closure additionally completes `tests/metrics.test.ts` 1–365 and `collectMetrics.lua` 1–47, with the already reviewed terminal caller rechecked at `moveToFinished-14.lua` 190–218. Collection is conditional on the terminal command's maxMetricsSize argument being nonempty. Each enabled terminal transition increments a cumulative stored count; minute buckets are generated only when a later transition observes a minute boundary, with intervening zeros and bounded retained history. They are event-driven stored counts, not an independent timer sampling queue health or authenticated unique-worker counts. The tests advance fake Date/timers through successful and failed processing, verify explicit fixtures, retention and read pagination after workers close. They do not test hostile clock shifts, mixed worker metric settings, concurrent deletion, detector accuracy or authorization. Source review only; Redis/fake-timer integration was not run here.

Completed pinned BullMQ `tests/pause.test.ts` 1–544, `tests/getters.test.ts` 1–1253, `pause-7.lua` 1–65 and `getWaitPlusPrioritizedCount.lua` 1–11. Global pause sets meta.paused and deletes the wake-up marker; it does not rename the normal waiting list. Resume clears that metadata, migrates a legacy paused list if present, and restores an immediate or delayed marker. The Redis backend's already reviewed 795–815 loops over remaining legacy entries, emitting the resume event only on the first invocation. Thus the Lua comment asking users to call resume repeatedly does not describe this TypeScript caller. The 7005-entry migration test (385–422) checks the combined waiting count, absent legacy key and one resume event.

Worker pause tests distinguish graceful local pause, which awaits current processing, from pause(true): the latter test (445–493) holds a long processor behind a latch, resumes, and finishes a short job before releasing the long processor. This is a materially stronger oracle for continued active work than an elapsed delay alone. Global pause's delayed-job test checks no processing over a bounded observation window, not indefinite exclusion or processor cancellation. These upstream tests were read, not executed here.

Getter tests cover missing job hashes and bounded backfill (276–334), positive/negative/overflow ranges (624–718), mixed and duplicate states (720–829), priority counts with paused metadata (896–943), and dependency items/jobs (945–1067). The completed-job test's expected length is commented out at 397; array-instance success alone is weaker coverage. Prometheus tests (1070–1250) stub getJobCounts/getMetrics: they establish formatting expectations, escaping and the zero-valued disabled-metrics representation, not collection correctness, endpoint authorization or runtime workload attribution. A stale comment calls the shared-connection worker test skipped, but its declaration is an active it.

Completed `getRanges-1.lua` 1–71, `getMetrics-2.lua` 1–20, `getCountsPerPriority-2.lua` 1–26, `paginate-1.lua` 1–50 and `findPage.lua` 1–71. Range reads can RPOP a deprecated trailing marker when other list entries exist, so even this observation path can mutate legacy state. Metrics return stored metadata/list length; priority zero counts the waiting list while nonzero priorities use score bands. Pagination accepts only sets/hashes and explicitly documents unstable pages under mutation. COUNT 100 is a scan hint, while pageSize bounds appended items; maxIterations bounds scan iterations, and an empty scan chunk returns immediately even with a nonzero cursor. Optional job fetches HGETALL dependency keys inside that script. None of these paths authenticate an operator or establish a consistent multi-call audit snapshot. No Redis process or upstream integration suite was run.

Owner Lark; task ac92ad9c-b890-4b76-9d41-43c5dc993f35 accepted 2026-10-17. The earlier dossier and receipts are preserved checkpoints. Research remains active; absence of Redis/Go runtime integration does not block source review. No broad completion claim follows from this document.

## Worker cancellation, drain and operation accounting

Completed previously partial `bullmq/src/classes/worker.ts` 1–1659 at the existing immutable pin. New ranges 1–329 show defaults, identity and worker lifecycle fields; 366–529 select a process/thread processor and sandbox, ready/backend/lock-manager wiring and event interfaces. 591–800 validates concurrency, starts stalled/lock managers, and runs an AsyncFifoQueue main loop. 801–1019 handles blocked acquisition, backend delay/limit responses, token attachment and repeat scheduling. Prior ranges already covered processing, failure/cancellation, pause/resume/close and retry/stalled loops. Queue tokens are generated from worker UUID and local attempt counter (681–706), then attached to fetched jobs (965–978). They are ownership tokens, not authenticated operator identities.

The main loop (683–748) continues while pending or resolved-unfetched operations remain even after pause/close, stops new fetches when closing/paused, and tracks processJob promises as well as acquisition. whenCurrentJobsFinished (1563–1576) disconnects the blocking acquisition channel and awaits mainLoopRunning. This establishes the intended connection from graceful close to tracked work; it does not yet prove all errors, child-process work and external effects quiesce. Forced close deliberately skips that wait. Child cleanup and connection/helper paths remain under review.

Completed `tests/job_cancellation.test.ts` 1–1093. Cases include nonexistent/completed jobs (217–269), cancel-all (272–337), cooperative mock-fetch/polling (339–469), active list maintenance (527–644), repeated cancellation/unrecoverable errors/multiple workers (646–832), signal support/reason (834–1033), and mocked moveToFailed failure (1035–1089). **Coverage distinction:** the test titled Lock Renewal Failure (472–524) installs a renewal listener, then directly calls cancelJob; it does not induce renewal failure or assert that its renewal event fired. It therefore supports manual cancellation handling, not the complete failed-renewal-to-cancellation flow. No upstream broker tests were run.

Completed `src/classes/async-fifo-queue.ts` 1–197: LinkedList tracks resolved results (7–50); add (90–109) counts pending promises, removes them on resolution/rejection, and optionally queues undefined on rejection. waitAll (117–119) uses Promise.all, not allSettled. Counts (128–149) distinguish pending and resolved-unfetched. fetch (180–195) checks empty state once before waiting; after a rejection it catches/reloops while the result queue is empty, without rechecking whether pending became empty.

Predeclared [helper experiment](fifo-lab-plan.md), [executed script](fifo-lab.mjs), and [receipt](fifo-receipt-1792267623919.json): 15 comparisons pass using the actual pinned class transformed by Node, with no dependency substitutions. A fetch started before the final pending operation rejected did not resolve within a 30ms watchdog although counts became zero; a fresh fetch returned undefined. Adding a fulfilled sentinel recovered the original waiting fetch. ignoreErrors=true queued undefined and allowed progress. waitAll rejected while another promise remained pending. This is a bounded helper-class result, not proof normal Worker code reaches the problematic input. Worker paths commonly convert failures into resolved undefined through retryIfFailed/onlyEmitError; trace unexpected rejection/trace callback paths before claiming worker deadlock.

The initial fixture failed due to an unobserved internal nextPromise rejection when testing waitAll without an active fetch consumer. [Failure receipt](fifo-initial-failure.json) preserves the prior script hash and error; corrected fixture creates a consumer before rejection. No earlier successful receipts were overwritten. Watchdogs were cleared, controlled work settled/fetched, and no broker/socket/process was started.

Completed `src/classes/sandbox.ts` 1–184 and `child-pool.ts` 1–133. Sandbox bridges child messages to progress/log/data/state methods (35–125), sends Start with job/token (128–134), and forwards abort as a Cancel message (136–152); finally removes listeners and returns a still-live child to the pool (163–179). Sending Cancel is again not a process-kill guarantee. ChildPool retains/initializes processors, avoids returning failed initialization children to reuse, removes children before kills, and clean() sends SIGTERM with a 30-second timeout via Child.kill (106–120). Child.kill implementation and child-side cancel behavior are the next dependency reads. These source files were not executed.

Next: complete child lifecycle/cancel dependencies and selected tests, then finish original partial job/backend files and their terminal conditional helpers. Coraza transaction/body/error and memberlist state/transport remain accessible queued work. The original nine partials are tracked through queue.json; new dependencies do not receive reading credit until inspected.

Child-flow continuation: complete `child.ts` 1–260, `child-processor.ts` 1–366, `main-base.ts` 1–51 and both 9-line process/thread entrypoints. Child.init forks a process or creates a Worker thread (78–129); Child.kill (157–178) registers an exit promise, sends termination, escalates to SIGKILL after the configured grace interval, and still awaits exit. Escalation time is not a hard bound on the returned promise if exit is never observed. Child-side Cancel (130–134) only aborts the controller; start (92–124) awaits processor work before reporting completion/failure and resetting state. SIGTERM/SIGINT handlers in main-base wait for currentJobPromise before exit; forced escalation is implemented by the parent. stop() is empty and ChildCommand.Stop is a no-op in this selected dispatch. No subprocess or worker thread was started by this research.

Child wrapJob exposes selected parent operations through IPC (156–341), including data/progress changes and queue transitions. waitResponse (344–365) installs a response listener and timeout; success removes the listener but does not clear its scheduled timeout in this file. This is a cleanup/timing observation, not a demonstrated production leak. Process/thread separation is execution isolation; these files do not establish a hostile-code security sandbox or per-account secret boundary. The child imports the selected processor in its ordinary runtime.

Completed upstream `tests/async_fifo_queue.test.ts` 1–152: default rejection case (16–33) includes another eventually fulfilled promise; all-rejection case (131–149) explicitly uses ignoreErrors=true. Neither directly tests a default-mode waiter when its only pending promise rejects, the branch covered by our helper receipt. Completed `tests/child-pool.test.ts` 1–142 covers reuse, killed-child replacement, execArgv and pre-fork init error; not executed here.

Completed original partial `job.ts` 1–1455. Constructor/serialization (188–535) normalize attempts/backoff, parent flags and deduplication, select the queue backend and serialize data/options/state; there is no authenticated tenant/account binding implemented by these selected methods. The producer/resource must supply that boundary. Updates/logging/removal/lock extension (538–640) delegate to backend; completion (652–690) serializes return values and records state after backend transition. Existing retry/failure code is now read in context. State/dependency getters and waitUntilFinished (896–1172) expose backend state/event observation; a local wait timeout is not job cancellation. Delayed/children/promote/retry and flow-entry methods (1181–1323) mutate requested state through the backend. Option validation (1325–1410) checks payload size, mutually exclusive parent policies, IDs, priorities, deduplication and jitter; it does not substitute for producer authorization. Stack traces and diagnostic return parsing (1412–1455) need sensitive-data handling by the embedding system. Backend implementation and conditional scripts remain next.

## Redis backend now read in full

Completed original partial `redis-queue-backend.ts` 1–3100. Connection status/reset and constructor/key context (77–202) connect to queue namespacing and sibling backends (204–274). Event forwarding/readiness/owned shutdown (276–423) distinguish connection ownership: a sibling backend's close is a no-op. execCommand (425–444) invokes a version-suffixed registered command; it does not authenticate an operator. State checks (446–490), insert argument builders/dispatch (492–701), bulk pipeline (703–734), flow multi (736–770), pause/dedup/scheduler methods (772–971), removal/renewal/data/progress/logging (973–1127) delegate concrete keys and payloads to client/Lua operations. Bulk submission's pipeline and flow submission's multi are distinct calls; the source must not be flattened into one unqualified all-or-nothing effects claim.

Terminal argument construction/keep policy/result handling (1129–1218) now read together with parent dependency/range/job/count queries (1243–1419), completion/failure argument wrappers (1421–1478), state/delay/priority/children/limit helpers (1480–1763), cleanup/scheduler/retry APIs (1765–2008), acquisition/stalled/rate-limited requeue (2025–2174), destructive maintenance/pagination/orphan cleanup (2176–2415), and higher-level transitions/dependency reads (2421–2712). These maintenance methods were read only; none was executed. An embedding API must authorize access before exposing them. Default token '0' in selected privileged requeue/delay helpers is a deliberate lock-check bypass convention, not evidence that arbitrary remote users have access to those methods.

Metadata/rate-limit/stream methods (2719–2926) and compact serialization (2929–3100) complete the file. `waitForJob` and `readEvents` race commands against watchdogs, but their finally blocks may await reset/reconnect. [Predeclared method-order experiment](watchdog-lab-plan.md) and [11-comparison receipt](watchdog-receipt-1792267783154.json) execute unchanged extracted isClientLive/resetBlockedConnection/waitForJob bodies through a typed-wrapper adaptation and synthetic timers/connection. The watchdog fired, yet the outer wait remained pending while reset's reconnect promise was unresolved; releasing reconnect completed the timeout return. Closing skipped reset. No real Redis/ioredis behavior was exercised. Thus the watchdog firing and a bounded public return are different claims; shutdown/reconnect policies need an explicit deadline if that stronger property is required.

## Complete literal terminal-script dependency closure

`terminal-flow-graph.json` records the three root Lua commands (finish, stalled recovery and batch lock renewal) and all 42 literal-include nodes, totaling 1479 lines. Every node has now been read fully at its existing pin; closure inventory itself originally assigned no credit. This closes the selected raw-Lua dependency graph, not all producer scripts, generated build artifacts or Redis internals. Node hashes/statuses are reproducibly checked against queue.json.

Acquisition-after-finalization: `fetchNextJob.lua` 1–95 reads global metadata, promotes due delayed jobs, checks rate TTL and paused/global-concurrency gates, selects waiting/prioritized work and calls the already reviewed prepareJobForProcessing. Complete `getQueueMetadata` 1–22, `getRateLimitTTL` 1–18 and `moveJobFromPrioritizedToActive` 1–14 show those decisions; `promoteDelayedJobs` 1–45 caps one promotion batch at 1000 and publishes waiting/delay reset before wake-marker insertion. Priority helpers (`addJobWithPriority` 1–15, `getPriorityScore` 1–9) combine priority and a counter. `getNextDelayedTimestamp` 1–13 and `addDelayMarkerIfNeeded` 1–16 track the earliest delayed score; a marker is notification state, not an authorization grant.

Parent dependencies: `moveChildFromDependenciesIfNeeded` 1–71 distinguishes fail-parent (deferred failure plus unsuccessful set), continue-parent (failed record plus readiness transition), and ignore/remove dependency behavior. Complete `moveParentToWaitIfNoPendingDependencies` 1–14 checks SCARD==0; `moveParentToWaitIfNeeded` 1–15 requires an existing waiting-children parent; `moveParentToWait` 1–45 routes delayed/prioritized/waiting work and wake markers. `updateParentDepsIfNeeded` 1–14 stores the child result before readiness evaluation. Thus fail, ignore, continue and remove are different control policies, not one generic propagation behavior.

Retention/removal: complete `removeJobKeys` 1–9, `removeJob` 1–19, `removeJobsByMaxAge` 1–25, `removeJobsByMaxCount` 1–16 and `removeParentDependencyKey` 1–91 delete job/log/dependency/result keys and may recursively remove or awaken parents according to hard-removal/same-queue conditions. `destructureJobKey` 1–13 derives IDs/prefixes. These are queue-state cleanup operations; no external application resource compensation is implemented here. Age cleanup is batch-limited; count cleanup's range can include many retained entries. Retention/deletion can erase evidence, so an assessment must preserve needed audit receipts before authorized maintenance.

Deduplication: complete `removeDeduplicationKeyIfNeededOnFinalization` 1–24 and `...OnRemoval` 1–19 condition deletion on expiry or current job ownership. Complete `requeueDeduplicatedJob` 1–66 reads the latest deferred payload, allocates an ID, sets a new dedup key, stores/enqueues the replacement, attaches parent dependency and only then deletes deferred data. `setDeduplicationKey` 1–13 selects TTL/no TTL; `storeAndEnqueueJob` 1–43 routes delayed/prioritized/normal; `storeJob` 1–37 persists payload/options/parent metadata; `addDelayedJob` 1–24 and `getDelayedScore` 1–26 schedule delayed work. Preserving deferred input until enqueue completion is not automatic rollback of earlier writes after every possible script error, nor does queue dedup provide idempotency of external effects.

Observability helpers: complete `collectMetrics` 1–47 uses minute-level count deltas and bounded list retention; `batches` 1–19 partitions argument batches; `getOrSetMaxEvents` 1–12 chooses stream trim length. Event/metric existence does not certify effect success; retention and asynchronous consumers change what later observers can reconstruct. No Lua was executed in this continuation.

Completed `redis-connection.ts` 1–890. Client-driver detection/default construction (74–291) accepts structural adapters or selected optional drivers, owns initialization and enforces blocking retry options. Readiness (312–377) observes statuses/events and cleans its listeners; command registration (383–399) supplies generated Lua content with the version suffix; init (401–481) wires events, patches blocking cluster access, waits and checks server version/capabilities. Blocking-cluster patch/refcounts/reconnect (483–695) **does contain a separate default 30-second cap on one cluster.connect attempt** and shares a reconnect promise; it is narrower than an end-to-end watchdog/cleanup bound. disconnect (697–726) awaits end/error when requested; reconnect (728–754) can wait/retry readiness; close (756–797) distinguishes shared/owned connections, force/initialization and quit, then removes listeners. ### Coraza transaction continuation: lines 1–1630

Read contiguous ranges through 1630, including the previously recorded header/interruption ranges. Lines 383–440 select a request parser from the first Content-Type header; parser agreement with the application is an integration requirement. Lines 789–909 preserve ordered query arguments and count individual values against the argument limit; URI parse failures set an error variable and attempt best-effort query extraction. These are source observations, not executed security tests.

Lines 960–1095 enforce request body limits in both byte-slice and reader paths. Reaching the limit (including equality) sets INBOUND_DATA_ERROR; Reject directly creates a 413 interruption, while ProcessPartial buffers the remaining budget and invokes body processing. Off or inaccessible body returns without reading. Lines 1104–1187 require the header phase; disabled/empty body still evaluates phase-two rules. Missing parser skips parsing but still evaluates rules. Invalid parser or parser failure sets body-error variables and evaluates rules, returning the resulting interruption with nil error. A parsing error therefore is not by itself proof of rejection: inspect error-variable rules and connector handling.

Lines 1196–1425 implement response header/body phases, MIME eligibility, response limit rejection at 500, and partial processing. Response processor failures set error variables before rule evaluation; buffer-reader failures can return actual errors. Lines 1428–1630 separate logging from enforcement: relevant-only audit selection can use the hypothetical DetectionOnly status, whereas the audit transaction interruption flag uses actual interruption. Configured audit parts can include headers, body and arguments; logging policy needs privacy review and is not a bot-accuracy measurement. No Go runtime validation was performed. Remaining transaction lines 1631–2656 and body buffer/parser/rule enforcement dependencies remain unread.

### Parser-to-rule enforcement and pooled-state follow-up

Full additional files read: sync pool interface/std/TinyGo implementations (12/29/32 lines); body processor registry (32); JSON processor (334); JSON process tests (370); Rule (802); deny action (64); ctl action (526); eq operator (58); public WAF construction (186). Directive handlers 359–395 and 580–675 were read as partial ranges.

JSON request/response processing reads the bounded buffered body, flattens values with append semantics for collisions, separately limits leaf and array-length entries, bounds flattened bytes, checks depth iteratively before validity checking, and preserves best-effort values before returning errors. Its tests cover both directions, raw TX storage, collisions, reader errors, invalid input and recursion errors using fresh variable collections; they do not test transaction reuse. These tests were read, not executed; gjson dependency internals and JSON integration tests remain material unread paths.

The response error-state hypothesis remains plausible at the pinned source: the standard pool delegates Get/Put to sync.Pool; TinyGo uses a LIFO slice, so reuse is an intended path. ctl can select a response processor before or during response headers; JSON ProcessResponse can return an error; transaction generates the four omitted error fields. Neither generator nor successful JSON processing clears those fields. Runtime effect still depends on reuse, loaded response error rules and integration behavior. No claimed exploit, live account leak or validated Go result.

Rule 171–418 obtains selected collection values, applies transforms/operators, requires matching chain children and runs parent flow/disruptive actions. Deny 35–49 invokes tx.Interrupt with configured status (default403); Transaction.Interrupt distinguishes On from DetectionOnly. Non-disruptive ctl actions run during matching and can select parsers or change engine mode. The public NewWAF 113–123 explicitly forces both body limit actions to ProcessPartial when the final configured mode is DetectionOnly, after parsing and overrides. The directive handlers only store values: reading the public constructor resolves where this enforcement really happens. Per-transaction ctl:ruleEngine only changes tx.RuleEngine; it does not rewrite the WAF-level limit actions, so do not generalize constructor normalization to every dynamic mode transition. Tests for these mode/limit transitions remain pending.


### Memberlist net.go: full 1–1416 review

The stream path (225–361) accepts transport connections, applies deadlines and label checks, then readStream before dispatch. Push/pull concurrency increments before checking >=128; state is read, local state sent, and only then mergeRemoteState runs. Thus join Merge.NotifyMerge rejection prevents merge but occurs after the response was sent; it is not by itself a confidentiality gate for local membership state. Custom transport/cryptographic access controls must be traced separately. This is ordering evidence, not a network test.

Packet ingestion (363–544) validates labels, conditionally decrypts when a keyed config enables encryption, and permits plaintext fallback only when GossipVerifyIncoming is false. CRC checking is separate from authentication. Ping/ack processing is immediate; alive/dead/suspect/user messages enter bounded priority queues, with alive prioritized and LIFO dequeue. Overflow drops messages; this is a load/reliability control, not principal authentication. Handlers (546–786) decode messages and invoke state changes. handleAlive applies configured source and advertised-address CIDR checks; this does not prove all message types are subject to identical address admission.

Outbound paths (788–1122) piggyback broadcasts, optionally compress and encrypt under the primary group key, pass Address.Name to transport and enforce RequireNodeNames as presence checks. Incoming stream path (1124–1234) rejects encrypted state without configured keys and plaintext when encryption plus incoming verification are enabled. Stream encryption authenticates type, length and label; group-key possession still does not establish a distinct operator or per-node credential.

State readers (1236–1363) bound declared node count, user-state length and user-message length, reject negatives, and dispatch merge/delegate callbacks. Encrypted-state byte length has a separate20MiB bound; decompression and codec allocation enforcement remain dependency questions, so do not call every plaintext stream globally20MiB bounded from these checks. TCP ping (1369–1414) verifies ack sequence and returns failures at deadline/error paths. Next: state.go complete transitions, memberlist initialization, transport/label/crypto/decompression implementations and relevant tests. No transport was opened or Go code executed.


### Memberlist state.go: full 1–1338 review

Scheduling 119–229 staggers probes/gossip and dynamically scales push/pull intervals; deschedule closes stopTick when tickers exist. Probe flow 231–521 distinguishes local transport errors from likely remote failures, tries direct UDP, indirect checks and optional TCP fallback, and adjusts local awareness when confirmations are missing. Failure detection therefore models uncertainty and network faults; it is not malicious-bot classification. ACK registry 829–905 matches sequence numbers and times out handlers; names/sequence IDs are correlation, not separate cryptographic identities.

Alive admission 940–1153 invokes the optional Alive delegate before insert/update, filters advertised addresses and handles name/address conflict with dead-node reclamation constraints. It rejects stale incarnation updates, refutes accusations about self, updates metadata and emits join/update notifications. This closes passive-merge admission through aliveNode, whereas Merge.NotifyMerge only applies when join=true. Callback existence alone does not establish callback authentication strength.

Suspicion/death 1157–1307 ignores unknown/stale nodes, refutes self, starts a timer tied to StateChange, and only marks dead if still in that same suspect state when the timer fires. Reporter names confirm suspicion, shortening timeout through the separately reviewed suspicion implementation; identity independence is not proven. A dead message whose Node equals From is classified Left; this equality is semantic convention, not per-node signature verification. Anti-entropy 1311–1337 routes alive through admission, left through deadNode, and remote dead/suspect through suspicion rather than immediately accepting remote death. Reaping, shuffle and gossip 563–683 separate membership retention and convergence from task cancellation/account revocation. No Go/network runtime test was performed.


### Memberlist initialization and concrete transport

Full transport.go 164, net_transport.go 379, label.go 182 and memberlist.go 800 lines read. Construction 100–240 initializes a keyring from SecretKey if provided, otherwise uses configured transport or plain NetTransport, applies node-aware shim and label wrapper, and launches listeners/handlers. NetTransport uses UDP and ordinary TCP; its WriteToAddress/DialAddressTimeout only use Address.Addr, ignoring Name. The shim likewise discards Name. RequireNodeNames therefore is not concrete peer-name authentication on this default transport. A custom NodeAwareTransport may implement stronger binding, which must be established from that implementation.

Labels are bounded framing/routing markers. Incoming mismatch is rejected before message parsing; cryptographic binding is conditional on keyed encryption, not supplied by the label bytes themselves. NetTransport startup creates listeners immediately; Create does not wait for Join to accept peers. setAlive only warns about public addressing without encryption; the warning is not a reject gate. Public metadata size is checked on locally generated NodeMeta; incoming decoding constraints need separate review.

Join resolves supplied addresses and calls pushPullNode(join=true); one successful address clears aggregate errors, so its success count is contacted hosts, not a consensus admission certificate. Leave broadcasts intentional-left but keeps listeners/gossip active. Shutdown closes transport first, then shutdown channel and schedules, and does not broadcast leave. Neither operation revokes a shared group key or cancels application jobs. Wrapper label-write errors return without closing the newly dialed connection at those functions; caller/transport behavior would need tests before a resource-leak runtime claim. Full transport tests and relevant net/state tests are pending; no listeners or DNS lookups were executed.


### Network tests and decompression closure

Full net_test.go 1163, transport_test.go235, util.go346 and MergeDelegate18 lines read. Network tests exercise direct/compound/indirect pings; wrong target name; TCP sequence/type mismatches and connection failure deadline; state exchange/piggyback; encryption roundtrip and mismatched keys; CRC corruption; bounded declared state/user sizes; missing message type; and original-connection closure after label-reader failure. Transport tests use MockNetwork for join/user delivery and a local TCP fixture for accept-error exponential backoff. These are upstream test intentions/assertions, not local passing runs. Some tests use real loopback sockets, which we did not open.

Util 287–310 bounds each decompression operation to maxDecompressedBytes+1 copied bytes and rejects excess (40MiB cap from net constants). This closes the previously open per-decompression budget question; it does not prove a global cap across nested command handling or codec preallocation. Compound parsing bounds declared-length table and returns complete parts plus truncation count. Group encryption tests prove expected key mismatch handling only if executed in their upstream environment; test presence is not measured deployment security. MergeDelegate explicitly excludes anti-entropy; admission enforcement must include Alive or transport policy.

Full cookies middleware tests1191, redirect test base449 and HTTP cookie wrapper208 lines read. The cookie test suite explicitly marks expected failures for host-only cookies being sent to subdomains, empty Domain dropping, dotless explicit domains and IPv6 hosts. These are documented pinned-suite limitations, not successful isolation assertions and not locally reproduced. The wrapper uses Python DefaultCookiePolicy and delegates acceptance/return decisions to http.cookiejar; it removes locking with a dummy lock, so do not infer thread-safe shared-session behavior. Runtime-specific cookiejar behavior remains a dependency.

Cookie tests586–639 demonstrate separate caller-supplied jar keys and that cookies can cross ports on the same host. Requests without the explicit jar key use the default jar; account assignment must keep jar selection attached to every new request. A jar key is a session partition, not a vault, per-account authorization check or distinct device identity.
