Cookie middleware can subsequently repopulate eligible jar cookies: inspecting only the redirected Request before middleware runs is an incomplete enforcement trace. These inspect synthetic Request/Response objects; they do not rotate public addresses, contact gateways or establish network anonymity. None of these upstream Python tests were run. ### Redis quota path and bounded adapter lab

Full Redis adapter211, StoreAbstract372, InsuredAbstract114, compatibility helper7/interface50, BlockedKeys68/index4 and ioredis tests1099 read. Default Lua first initializes a fixed-window counter with SET NX/EX, increments and reads TTL, repairing missing expiry. Quota comparison occurs in StoreAbstract._afterConsume after the returned count: counts exceeding points reject. Caller must gate protected work on promise resolution; the library does not identify an account or send HTTP429 itself. Key prefix/identity are caller inputs. Atomic script semantics are source evidence; no Redis execution or failover consistency test.

Readiness checking is opt-in and recognizes particular client shapes; unknown shapes pass through. Ordinary storage Errors invoke configured insurance, while RateLimiterRes quota rejection does not. Insurance can have different points/duration, and independent fallback instances do not create a global budget. BlockedKeys is a local time-expiry map with opportunistic expired-entry collection above999 entries; this is not a hard999-key maximum. Redis tests cover normal limits, expiry, values, pacing, fallback, prefixes, storage failures and custom Lua. Recognized closed client rejects without a command when enabled; default-off and unknown shapes reach the supplied synthetic command. Over-budget rejects without fallback; storage Error uses fallback. Two independent synthetic insurance budgets allow four total, illustrating scope rather than a Redis outage measurement. Prefix and default script registration are verified; decimals reject before command. No sockets, timers, child processes, Redis/Lua code or services were created.


### Broadcast completion is not delivery acknowledgement

Full label_test376, peeked_conn49, queue412 and broadcast109 lines read. Label tests cover framing, truncated/empty labels and size255 boundary, both packets and net.Pipe streams; they do not authenticate an operator. peekedConn preserves buffered payload before reading underlying connection.

TransmitLimitedQueue prioritizes low transmit counts, larger fitting messages and recent IDs. Named broadcasts replace previous same-name entries and call Finished on invalidation; selection increments transmission counts and calls Finished when the limit is reached, before the caller performs the actual network write. Reset/prune also call Finished. memberlistBroadcast.Finished sends the notification used by Leave/UpdateNode. Therefore notification means queue lifecycle completion, not confirmed peer receipt, handler execution or cluster consensus. Existing source comments saying successfully broadcasted should not be promoted to an acknowledgement guarantee. Checking queue depth in memberlist.go only emits a metric; it does not invoke Prune. A configured monitoring interval therefore is not itself an enforced queue-size bound. Queue tests, custom delegate budgeting and btree dependency are still pending. No Go runtime test.

### Account assignment: OAuth identity is not worker identity

Deliberately read archived RFC9700 lines354–476 and1960–2094; hashes/retrieval remain in sources.json, selected ranges in primary-reading.json. [RFC9700 sections2.2–2.5](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9700.html#section-2.2) separate sender-constrained tokens from scope/resource/action restrictions. A token naming a resource server is effective only when that resource server checks its audience on every request; the same applies to action/resource permission. Issuing narrower credentials is not proof that callers enforce them. Sender constraint ties token use to a key holder; it does not establish one human, physical device or worker per key.

[Section4.14](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9700.html#section-4.14) requires issued refresh tokens to stay within consented scope/resources and describes retained rotation lineage: replay of an invalidated token can trigger revocation of the active token without identifying which presenter was malicious. Owned workers sharing one refresh-token family therefore need coordinated renewal; isolated worker processes do not make a shared renewal credential independent. This is an architecture implication, not an observed provider race here.

[Section4.15](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9700.html#section-4.15) directly addresses confusion between client identities and resource-owner identities in a common subject namespace. An owned-system assignment should preserve issuer, principal kind, subject, tenant, grant/audience, assigned workload and credential lineage as separate fields. This schema is our design inference from the specification, not a prescribed universal OAuth data model. No third-party account was created and no actual credential was issued or used.

### Service-account assignment and revocation freshness

Read archived Kubernetes service-account article886–1033 in three bounded outputs. [The project documentation](https://kubernetes.io/docs/concepts/security/service-accounts/) identifies ServiceAccounts as namespaced non-human principals, separately grants RBAC permissions, and assigns them to Pods. A Pod with no explicit assignment uses its namespace's default account. Several Pods can use the same ServiceAccount: process replication does not create distinct authorization principals. A per-workload account can preserve permission separation, while per-instance token/object binding can add instance attribution; these are distinct design choices.

The article distinguishes automatically rotating projected tokens from legacy long-lived Secret tokens, and describes disabling automatic token mounting for workloads that do not need credentials. Its current node-audience restriction is explicitly conditional on its feature gate/admission plugin and applies to kubelets, not every TokenRequest caller. Do not generalize the protection to arbitrary clients.

Documentation1002–1029 distinguishes signature/time/audience validation from current bound-object validation. It recommends TokenReview for recognizing deletion of the bound object; independently validating through OIDC can continue accepting the token until expiry. This supports the architectural requirement that a swarm kill/revoke path specify which validators recheck current authority, and their cache/failure behavior. It is project documentation, not a code-level or local-cluster proof of immediate revocation. Kubernetes authenticators, caching, outages and exact deletion timing remain unread dependencies. No cluster was contacted or changed.

### Address changes and shared egress: evidence limits

Read RFC6269 archived855–1043 and RFC8981 archived130–238/861–904. [RFC6269 sections12–13](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc6269.html#section-12) explain why a shared IPv4 address cannot uniquely identify a subscriber, and why dynamic mappings require port/time context for provider-side disambiguation. This still does not identify a person or application account, and most endpoint defenders lack the provider's mapping records. Its retention/legal discussion is historical2011 context, not current legal guidance or a proposed retention policy here. Logs need purpose, minimization and controlled retention; collecting more identifiers does not establish an attribution oracle.

Section13.1 describes legitimate aggregate activity and collateral denial when an IP penalty box combines unrelated subscribers. The earlier synthetic shared-IP receipt demonstrates that failure mode under our tiny configured quota; the RFC supplies architectural context, not a field false-positive rate. Neither proves all IP limits useless: a shared network resource budget can still protect capacity, while authenticated per-principal budgets support fairer application policy.

[RFC8981 sections1.2–2.1 and8](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8981.html#section-8) explain that temporary IPv6 addresses limit one form of stable-address correlation, but prefixes, cookies, authentication and on-link observation remain distinct correlation channels. Legitimate address changes therefore do not prove abusive rotation, and one authenticated account using several addresses does not become several authorization principals. Conversely, a stable prefix may group unrelated household devices and users. A detector should evaluate false positives for both shared and changing addresses; no exact prefix-based attribution rule or evasion configuration follows from this evidence. No interface, route, egress gateway or address was changed.

### Memberlist delegate obligations and completion tests

Read delegate.go1–41, event_delegate.go1–68, queue_test.go1–246 and internal/retry/retry.go1–208 completely. The general Delegate contract explicitly requires concurrent safety, prompt NotifyMsg/GetBroadcasts callbacks, copying retained message slices, and application respect for the outgoing byte budget. These are integration obligations; the interface does not isolate slow/untrusted application code. LocalState/MergeRemoteState are application state hooks, not permission checks.

ChannelEventDelegate copies the Node struct then synchronously sends to its channel; it does not guarantee a nonblocking queue or deep-copy referenced slices. The documentation warns that slow consumers block the delegate. Previously reviewed state.go1142–1152/1250–1306 invokes event callbacks inline in node-state transitions; deadNode holds nodeLock until return. Therefore a blocked event consumer can delay that transition's lock release. This is source-level backpressure reachability, not a timed local stall or remote denial-of-service demonstration.

Queue tests176–213 explicitly expect prune notifications for messages that were never sent, supporting the earlier finding that Finished/notification is not delivery acknowledgement. Other tests cover byte packing, replacement and retransmit exhaustion; no remote receiver or ACK appears in these queue-only tests. The retry helper is test infrastructure: it repeats checks in a goroutine with a default7-second window/25ms wait and retains/deduplicates failed-attempt output. This is eventual-test retry, not a production work retry/backoff policy. Go tests remain unexecuted locally.

### Local identity/proof/enforcement lab

The [predefined plan](identity-lab-plan.md) and [script](identity-lab.mjs) exercise an actual ephemeral127.0.0.1 HTTP endpoint using Node built-ins, in-memory synthetic issuer/worker keys, timing-safe HMAC comparison and an effect counter after all checks. This is a custom protocol fixture, explicitly not OAuth/JWT or RFC9421 wire syntax or conformance. No real account, token, key, address change or external target was used.

The final [receipt](identity-receipt-1792269161509.json) records22 passing comparisons on Node24.21,3 accepted effects, and listenerClosed=true. Replayed signed requests return409; invalid MAC/expiry/time/unknown worker return401; incorrect tenant, assigned worker, principal kind, audience, scope or revoked authority return403; wrong method/path and unsigned body return404. Rejected calls preserve effects. A bad request MAC does not consume the nonce, so a valid proof with that nonce can subsequently succeed. Changing an untrusted address label does not reset authenticated replay identity.

The counterexample validates signature and expiry on the same token after current worker authority has been revoked: authentication alone remains true, while the protected endpoint rejects it through a separate current-authority check. This demonstrates the fixture's trust boundary, not revocation behavior of an actual OAuth provider or Kubernetes cache. Single-process synchronous nonce check/insert and in-memory state do not prove distributed replay resistance, bounded retention, outage behavior, key rotation, body canonicalization or physical-device identity.

Earlier receipts1792269139685 and1792269150514 remain immutable. The first passed the original comparisons; code inspection then added explicit rejection of body framing because bodies are outside the signed protocol. The second passed those original comparisons after the guard. A further body-rejection oracle was added to the plan before the final run, using Node HTTP because fetch forbids GET bodies. Only the final receipt tests that additional boundary. No failed security oracle was hidden and no service remains listening.

### Registered actions and test reachability

Read rule_parser_test.go1–585, actions/actions.go1–141, actions/pass.go1–52, actions/block.go1–76, operators/operators.go1–52, operators/operators_test.go1–146 and strings/strings.go1–145 completely. Rule-parser tests330–477 actively check disruptive-chain rejection, the exact error sentinel, removal of the whole pending chain and independent loading of a subsequent rule. These are stronger state assertions than the directive syntax-only cases. Quote-warning tests518–577 explicitly expect selected malformed action strings to initialize without error and emit a warning. None were executed locally.

Action registration lowercases names and permits replacement; Get constructs the registered implementation. Operator registration/Get also permits replacement but does not lowercase names in this file. Thus installed trusted plugin code can change the meaning behind a familiar name; a catalog entry alone is not enforcement evidence. pass.Evaluate is empty; block.Evaluate is also empty and documented as a placeholder, with its effective default action supplied by the already-read mergeActions path. A literal block keyword alone must not be treated as an independent rejection primitive.

This is source-established dead coverage within this specific data-driven test function, not a claim that other operator tests do not run, operators are broken, or a local Go test was executed. The JSON fixtures were not semantically reviewed or credited as executed cases.

### Centralized process-cluster rate budgets

Read RateLimiterCluster.js1–368 and RateLimiterCluster.test.js1–230 completely. Workers send operations through built-in process IPC to a primary-side RateLimiterMemory indexed by keyPrefix. This coordinates a process cluster; it does not establish a durable counter across independent hosts/primary processes. The primary creates a limiter only for the first initialization of that prefix208–210; later initializations acknowledge it without checking for conflicting points/duration. Therefore the keyPrefix is both a budget namespace and a configuration coordination obligation, not a tenant permission credential.

The operation dispatcher84–123 allows consume/penalty/reward/block/get/delete for an existing prefix without a separate per-sender authorization check in this module. That is compatible with trusted application workers; it is not a security boundary against hostile worker code. An external protected API still needs to authenticate and bind the requested principal/key before invoking it.

savePromise175–193 rejects and removes a pending client promise on timeout, but does not cancel a primary operation or recall an already sent IPC message. workerWaitInit52–60 also sends its pending payload if initialization becomes true before its next timer callback, even if the corresponding promise was removed during that interval. Thus a caller timeout is not proof that no budget state changed. This is source-level ordering evidence; no IPC race or multi-process runtime was induced locally. Request/effect idempotency remains separate from quota accounting.

The upstream tests replace process.on/send with synchronous arrays and stub cluster.on; they are not actual forked-worker integration tests. The two-limiter assertion141 checks keyPrefixes[0] twice, so does not independently assert the second prefix's presence there. Other operations do exercise constructed synthetic worker instances. No test here establishes primary restart persistence, real IPC failure, delayed initialization cancellation or PM2 transport behavior. PM2 launch/send dependencies remain unread and were not installed or run.

### Group-key rotation and transport-fixture identity

Read keyring_test.go186, ping_delegate.go18, conflict_delegate.go14, broadcast_test.go31, awareness_test.go45 and mock_transport.go201 completely. Keyring tests distinguish add, switch primary and remove: adding a secondary does not switch the primary, removing the active primary is rejected, both installed keys can decrypt, and removing a secondary makes its earlier ciphertext undecryptable with that local ring. These are group-key operations, not revocation of an individual node/operator. Fleet-wide distribution/removal and incoming verification mode remain necessary parts of an actual rotation protocol. Tests were not run locally.

MockTransport.getPeer186–199 routes by Name when supplied, unlike the reviewed default NetTransport that ignores Address.Name. Its packet/stream paths use synchronous channels/net.Pipe; DialAddressTimeout does not use the timeout argument, and Shutdown returns nil without closing channels or pipes. Therefore generic transport tests using this mock cannot establish production peer-name authentication, real deadline behavior or socket cleanup. This difference strengthens the existing warning to inspect the actual selected transport.

PingDelegate documents direct-ping RTT only, excluding indirect/TCP fallback measurements; conflict callbacks report name/address collisions rather than cryptographic identity proof. Awareness tests assert local score saturation and timeout scaling, not malicious-peer classification. Broadcast tests check same-node invalidation/message bytes, without a receiver or delivery acknowledgement. These narrowly scoped fixtures should not become broader authentication, bot-detection or reliable-delivery claims.

### Legitimate crawler controller: scheduling, identity and shutdown

Read complete Scrapy scheduler560, dupefilters245, utils/request283, throttle126, robots middleware241, downloader462, engine1049, stop-mode helper28 and throttle tests324. This expands the earlier session-only path into controller behavior; priority-queue/backing-store, scraper, handlers, async bridges and scheduler/shutdown tests remain open dependencies. No crawler was started and no URL requested.

Scheduler388–414 checks duplicate status before attempting disk enqueue; unserializable disk requests fall back to memory446–470. RFPDupeFilter140–147 records a fingerprint as seen at scheduling time, not successful download or external-effect completion. Its default fingerprinter uses method/canonical URL/body and selected handler identity while headers are omitted by default; cookiejar/account/tenant metadata is absent from that key. Separate account jars therefore do not imply separate deduplication domains. This is a key-model observation, not a server authorization leak or a recommendation to disable crawler policies. Owned multi-account jobs need an explicitly chosen task identity consistent with their intended resource semantics and permission/rate budgets.

Persistent queue state is written through a temporary active.json then replacement555–559, with periodic syncing conditional on queue support/settings. This does not establish fsync/power-failure durability or that memory-fallback requests survive restart. DiskDupeFilter uses SQLite WAL/NORMAL, commits conditionally and on close; imported SQLite/queue durability semantics were not runtime tested. Request fingerprints are not work leases, workload credentials or transaction idempotency tokens.

Downloader enforces per-slot transfer capacity in _process_queue and stamps automatically selected slots so redirects/retries can recalculate them. Total concurrency is exposed through needs_backout238–240, which the engine consults for scheduled work498–511; download_async658–681 directly uses the downloader path rather than scheduler deduplication. Those entry points must not be treated as equivalent admission paths. Custom slot metadata is trusted application configuration, not a server-side account authorization system.

AutoThrottle adjusts slot delay from latency/target concurrency, bounded by configured min/max; non200 responses cannot lower it. The complete upstream tests use mock downloaders/slots and assert numerical adjustments, skip conditions and debug output. They do not prove server quota compliance, global fleet budgets or real network behavior. Delay/concurrency regulation and authenticated server quotas serve different purposes.

Robots middleware requires enabled settings and a parser. It calls parser.allowed then IgnoreRequest on denial; download/parser errors resolve the cached parser to None and the request check then returns without denial. Status counters do not themselves enforce an access policy. This is cooperative crawler behavior, not access control at the protected endpoint. Parser implementations and tests remain material dependencies; do not claim full RFC9309 conformance from this middleware alone.

Engine stop transitions halt new scheduled dispatch, cancel start iteration, optionally invoke downloader.stop, await in-progress bookkeeping, close scraper/scheduler and emit close signals. Reentrant close calls during opening/closing can return before final shutdown; they are deliberately handled to avoid deadlock877–1048. Downloader.stop400–438 drops queued work, cancels tracked Deferreds and waits for settlement; close440–461 only disables acceptance/stops timers. Actual handler cancellation and external effects need their own evidence. Lifecycle enum validation296–307 logs invalid transitions but still assigns them; the table is diagnostic, not an exception-enforced transition firewall.

### Crawler queue composition and tests

Read pqueues510, squeues202, downloader/middleware231, middleware163 and complete scheduler484/dupefilter308/base-scheduler172 tests. Priority queues negate application priorities, keep start queues distinct, discard empty priority queues and expose optional state/change tracking. DownloaderAwarePriorityQueue chooses the least-active download slot with round-robin ties; its constructor rejects nonzero per-IP concurrency and incompatible resumed state. That is local scheduling fairness, not tenant/account authorization or a cluster-wide budget.

Queue composition serializes Request.to_dict, then pickle or marshal, then a queuelib backing store. Deserialization occurs when popped; the reviewed code provides no authenticity/encryption wrapper around persisted request dictionaries. Treat this as a trusted job-state boundary, not a vault or a format to load from untrusted sources. Request headers/cookies/body/meta can be persisted through the previously read Request serialization path. No real queue state or credential file was opened; no untrusted pickle was built or loaded. queuelib/storage/fsync dependencies remain unread.

Downloader middleware processes requests in configured order, responses/exceptions in reverse, can short-circuit with a replacement Request/Response, and only invokes the actual download function if no request middleware handles it. CloseSpider is re-raised rather than treated as a recoverable transport error. Response-exception recovery is configuration-dependent. Component loading catches NotConfigured and omits the component; an installed class is not proof that its policy is active.

Scheduler tests verify clean reopen, numerical priorities across memory/disk, periodic active.json changes and memory fallback. Most deliberately choose BaseDupeFilter, which never filters, so their queue fairness/order coverage is not duplicate-filter coverage. Dupefilter tests separately check repeat detection, clean restart, custom fingerprinter semantics, truncated final fingerprint handling and SQLite commit visibility through a second connection273–295. Neither clean reopen nor second-connection visibility is a power-loss/crash-durability oracle. Base-scheduler tests use a minimal pending-request dictionary; its deduplication semantics differ from the production seen-set lifetime. All these upstream tests were read, not run; MockServer fixtures were not started.

### Callback completion and cooperative robots policy

Read scraper587, pipelines160, robotstxt adapter157 and robots middleware tests430 completely. Scraper completion includes callback/errback output iteration and item-pipeline tracking. It increments itemproc_size before scheduling fire-and-forget start items, and close waits for queued/active/item work before invoking pipeline close. Callback exceptions and DropItem/item errors are recorded/signaled through distinct branches, not transformed into proof of durable output. Parent request IDs annotate derivation, not worker/account authorization. The scraper size threshold is backpressure bookkeeping, not a hard bound on all allocations or external effects. Robots adapters delegate matching to stdlib RobotFileParser, optional Rerp or default Protego; exposing crawl_delay does not itself enforce a downloader delay. Custom signal listeners/plugins can differ; no full Protego/Rerp semantic review or local crawler execution was done.

It separates unavailable4xx behavior from unreachable network/5xx behavior, specifies matching/cache/parser requirements, and states that robots rules do not grant authorization. This normative specification is distinct from implementation behavior. The reviewed Scrapy _robots_error path caches None, and tests198–237 explicitly expect requests not to be ignored after a DNS failure. The tests also distinguish garbage/empty policy bodies from deny rules, exercise concurrent parser requests, and use local MockServer fixtures for start-denial finish reasons. The parser itself receives response.body, not status, through these adapters; status counting is not the RFC access-result policy. Do not claim conformance or rejection merely from a robots parser class being registered. No HTTP status, DNS failure, crawler, parser package or challenge was exercised against any external service.

CONNECT response buffering waits for the complete header delimiter; its local buffer has no explicit size cap in this file, while endpoint/time/library bounds require separate tracing. ### Request credentials and cache account boundaries

Global Basic credentials require an explicit domain setting (including explicit None meaning all domains); per-request credentials without domain record/check an origin, while explicit domain allows subdomains. Existing Authorization is preserved. These are credential routing rules, not server authorization; request metadata and plugin code remain trusted. URI middleware moves credentials into metadata and replaces URL, not a vault or proof secrets never persisted/logged earlier.

When optional HTTP caching is enabled (default disabled), default DummyPolicy caches eligible schemes/statuses without identity-aware cache controls and always treats cached responses as fresh. Both storage keys call the crawler request fingerprinter, previously traced to default method/canonical URL/body without cookies/auth/jar/account/tenant. Filesystem scope is spider/domain/none, not account; therefore distinct accounts using the same default request key and scope can select the same stored response. This is a source-derived collision path pending synthetic runtime validation and complete cache tests, not observed production disclosure. Filesystem also stores request headers/body and response cookies/body in trusted local cache, and loads pickle metadata/extra response data; DBM also loads pickle. These files are sensitive trusted state, not safe untrusted imports. Storage writes multiple files sequentially without a local atomic-entry transaction; middleware treats retrieve exceptions as miss, not evidence of consistent crash recovery. RFC2616Policy conditional behavior differs from DummyPolicy and does not create an account partition by itself. No real cache/credentials opened or upstream suite run.

### Allow/deny precedence, composite quotas and local wait queues

Read limiter wrapper176/union52/queue163/queue-error14 and full wrapper818/union84/queue385 tests. Wrapper checks allow before deny; overlap is explicitly tested395–414. Allow result bypasses quota; runActionAnyway optionally starts inner operation without awaiting it and swallows its rejection, so wrapper result does not prove inner accounting success. Predicates are synchronous function calls with truthiness and no await; async risk lookup needs a different adapter, not an assumed supported enforcement path. Denied consume rejects, while block/penalty/reward/get/set can resolve synthetic denied results; resolved Promise alone is not access approval. Union concurrently consumes every limiter and only then aggregates rejections; no rollback/refund appears when one rejects, so composite quota consumption is not an atomic transaction. Result object keys use keyPrefix; duplicate prefixes can overwrite reported evidence. Constructor accepts one limiter (requires at least1); test26–36 is named requires2 but calls1 and has assertion only inside catch, so passes on unexpected success. This is a specific weak oracle, not proof union admission fails.

RateLimiterQueue is an in-memory per-key FIFO with retry timers after quota rejection; Error rejects, quota result queues. Default maxQueueSize4294967295 is not a practical memory budget; map entries have no eviction and no public close/cancel method in this file. Expiry is swept only when FIFO processor runs; immediate consume and already-started consume are not deadline-fenced, and waiting until quota retry can delay expiry notification. Queue limits are per-key, not aggregate across all keys. Tests cover expiry sweep regression using private internals, not a real distributed-store timing race. Internal queueLimiters uses plain object/property lookup without own-key guard; caller-controlled key namespace handling needs a bounded runtime oracle before an impact claim. No upstream tests, broker or timers from these new files were executed.

# Drizzle quota accounting and database test boundary

Complete contiguous review: limiter `lib/RateLimiterDrizzle.js:1–228`, `lib/RateLimiterDrizzleNonAtomic.js:1–176`, both Postgres test files `1–428`, and their database/config/schema helpers `1–7`, `1–11`, `1–7`. These seven files are source review, not a database execution receipt.

The atomic adapter encloses a select and conflict-upsert in a transaction, but its decision to replace versus increment points depends on the earlier selected record. No row lock or isolation level is selected in this implementation. Two transactions seeing a missing key could therefore select the replacement branch; actual concurrent behavior depends on the ORM, database isolation and conflict semantics, which are not runtime tested here. The non-atomic adapter additionally computes accumulated points from an earlier read. Neither label alone establishes a distributed quota guarantee.

Both implementations treat a missing or null prior expiry as a reason to replace points. This is a source-level concern for continued consumption after a forever block/set and duration-zero accumulation. The two suites check that forever records remain present and readable, but do not consume again after those operations. Their sequential quota tests do not settle the fresh-key concurrency question. Dynamic operator imports are cached; the non-atomic loader maps import/initialization failure to its package-availability error. No package was installed to exercise this path.

Test-method limitations: both suites have rejection-only catches without a corresponding assertion on unexpected resolution, and their block-expiry setup catches its own `expect.fail` in an empty catch at305–309. The minimum-delay test sleeps before the operation, so its elapsed-time lower bound does not isolate limiter delay. The non-atomic insurance test supplies a client with only a rejecting `transaction` method even though that adapter's upsert uses direct select/insert methods; fallback success therefore does not identify the intended failing method. These are narrow oracle limitations, not evidence that all assertions are ineffective.

The fixture uses a local Postgres URL and a primary-key key column with nullable timestamp expiry. Before/after hooks delete the entire limiter table, and the package's broader test commands can push schemas. No suite, database connection or migration was run. Public fixture configuration is not a real credential receipt. Tests remain unexecuted until an explicitly isolated database/toolchain is available within the authorized boundary.

# Dynamo counter transitions and persistence test oracles

Full review: Dynamo adapter `1–401`, suite `1–377`, memory-persistence suite `1–241`, abstract-options suite `1–192`, and store-abstract suite `1–123`. No AWS SDK/server or upstream suite was executed.

Dynamo's first conditional update replaces absent/expired state; any error, not just conditional failure, triggers a second update that increments an unexpired item. Therefore the recovery path does not classify transient/auth/validation failures or ambiguous commit outcomes before trying the alternate operation. Each update can be atomic while the two-step operation still has failure/interleaving conditions; the comment's broad no-race claim is not established by these tests. No idempotency token is supplied. The stored never-expire sentinel is -1, but the first update's expiry comparison includes it (`expire <= currentTime`), so later consumption selects replacement. The upstream duration-zero test explicitly expects a previously set count of two to become one after consuming one; it validates that behavior rather than cumulative forever quota semantics.

Logical reads separately reject expired items before physical TTL cleanup. Constructor initialization can create a table and enable TTL; callers need actual lifecycle/permissions, not just a constructed object. The constructor's detached `_setTTL().finally` branch calls its callback without forwarding a TTL failure; its rejected promise is not returned/handled by that branch. Table creation success is not an explicit table-active wait here. These initialization error consequences remain source-only. `_delete` uses `_client`, which is correctly supplied by the already reviewed base-class `client` setter; it is not a field-name bug.

The suite targets local Dynamo on port8000 with fake fixture keys. Some cleanup deletions are not awaited. The synchronous `it` at216–239 schedules its assertion in an async constructor callback without returning a promise or accepting `done`, so test completion does not await that assertion. The error-deletion test accepts any rejection and therefore does not identify the stubbed cause. No parallel counter/retry/ambiguous-result oracle appears in this suite.

Memory persistence tests deliberately allow restoring into a different prefix and changing the points budget. That makes dump/restore an administrative state migration contract, not an identity boundary or account-authority check. Version mismatch is ignored, invalid/expired entries are counted, live absolute expiries survive a new instance, and block state is recalculated against the new budget. Tests use synthetic objects and elapsed wall-clock expectations; they do not exercise file durability, atomic disk replacement, authentication of dumps or concurrent merge. The abstract-options tests cover finite numeric validation, negative/zero points, nonnegative duration, mutable derived pacing and key-prefix parsing; parsing a key does not authenticate its owner. Store tests inspect in-memory blocked-key deletion with a fake store, not shared-backend revocation.

README21/97 and CONTEXT32 assert broad atomicity/no-race behavior, whereas README220–221 and other passages explicitly allow non-atomic adapters. The completed adapter paths require per-backend qualification. README173 and reference523 promise points never reset at duration zero; the Prisma inert `_upsert` receipt and Dynamo's own duration-zero test contradict a universal cumulative-counter interpretation. Preserve both sides rather than upgrading a product summary over actual code.

Reference171 says best-effort snapshots suit losing up to1% of requests; no failure distribution or bound is established by the reviewed snapshot code/tests. It is application advice, not a measured maximum-loss guarantee. Reference39–49 gives latency numbers with worker/load parameters, but no current environment/version/raw sample receipt was executed here; these are upstream benchmark claims, not our measurements. Docs explicitly disclose independent insurance state and no copy-back after recovery, consistent with the reviewed implementation. The periodic-sync sample leaves synchronization as a comment and admits weaker consistency; it is not a finished distributed quota algorithm.

Identity remains caller-owned: examples choose a request IP or a user ID without a principal-kind namespace, and several simplistic middleware catches collapse infrastructure error into429 while the later error-handling section distinguishes those errors. No limiter authenticates `req.userId`, establishes trusted proxy hops, normalizes account identity or authorizes snapshot import merely by consuming a string. The trust-proxy warning is documentation, not code-level header validation.

Infrastructure boundary: general Compose uses mutable tags (including latest), bare host port mappings, fixture Postgres credentials and unauthenticated etcd. The cluster Compose requests host IPC/network and privileged mode; its shell script deletes `/data/*`, optionally changes a kernel setting, binds all interfaces with protected mode off and starts daemonized nodes. Its readiness loop can exhaust30 attempts and still announce completion. These are reasons this supplied fixture is unsuitable for automatic launch under this track's no host/network mutation scope; no host control rejected an attempted action because none was attempted. The legacy Travis matrix lists Node8–14 while README advertises Node>=20, so the mere presence of that CI file is not current-runtime evidence. ESLint disables unsupported-feature checking; a lint configuration alone cannot establish version compatibility. License is ISC with notice retention and warranty disclaimer; it supplies no security correctness guarantee.

# BullMQ flow insertion, namespace and error-result boundary

Full files read: QueueBase1–243, QueueKeys1–43, FlowProducer1–706, Redis-only flow test1–92. Revisited already-complete backend190–270/630–700/730–800 and Job1280–1405 to close the caller path. General flow suite is explicitly partial:1–150 and6418–6630 only; remaining151–6417 is active queued reading, not inferred from test names.

Queue names/prefixes build storage keys and qualified parent references, not authenticated account identities. FlowProducer binds siblings through `backend.forQueue` over the same connection; sibling close/disconnect are no-ops because they do not own it. There is no per-node account credential/permission enrollment in this flow layer. The owning producer's close does not cancel already submitted jobs or revoke workers. Base close awaits backend close; event-emission failures may be caught, forwarded to an error event and ultimately logged rather than propagated. Observer success is therefore not an enforcement receipt.

Flow validation rejects deduplication on any node with children before submission; `Job.toFlowEntry` serializes and validates each job before the backend call, including size limit only when configured and mutually exclusive parent-failure options. UUID/default IDs and custom IDs are workflow identity, not authorization. Read-flow depth defaults10 and maxChildren20 per type; these are observation bounds, not add-tree resource limits. Recursive add/validation do not specify a node count/cycle budget here. Depth0/negative inputs are not normalized before truthiness-based recursive decrement, leaving a source-only bounds question for malformed administrative input.

Redis backend adds each entry's insert command to one `MULTI` and returns all result tuples. Single `add` checks the first tuple's error/negative code; `addBulk` updates IDs only for successful string results and otherwise returns its constructed trees. It does not reject every tuple failure. General suite6564–6598 explicitly asserts that a valid root exists and an invalid missing-parent root does not after the same bulk call. This is contrary evidence to FlowProducer228–229/328–329's universal no-jobs-on-failure promise, already present in the project's own intended oracle. No Redis transaction, crash, cluster-slot or rollback experiment was run here. A batch submit abstraction and a per-entry result need separate guarantees.

The selected suite tail checks child-bearing dedup rejection, leaf-root dedup ID preservation, eight concurrent same-dedup add calls yielding one ID, partial bulk root failure and single missing-parent rejection with absent orphan state. These are source oracles, not executed results. Redis-only suite checks parent/child metadata hashes; it does not test atomic failure. Tests create workers/connections and remove queue data; no suite was launched against any existing service. Earlier selected first test attaches its completion observer after insertion, so its wait relies on scheduling; no actual missed event was measured.
# Flow suite contiguous continuation through1750

Read general flow suite151–1750 in complete bounded outputs, joining initial range1–150. It specifies retained child results in the parent's dependency map even after child auto-removal, including age-based removal; deleting child job records therefore does not by itself erase copied parent results. Parent observability/retention needs its own policy. The happy-path ordering test verifies children results and a waiting-children state, but its observation of that state depends on worker timing rather than a test barrier.

Dependency-removal tests distinguish releasing a last pending dependency from retaining waiting-children when another remains, and check reloaded child state loses its parent reference. Missing parent/child operations reject with contextual errors. Ignore-failure tests preserve three failure strings in the ignored dependency map and allow parent progress; remove-failure tests drop dependencies. These are different workflow choices, not evidence every parent completion means every child succeeded.

Step-job tests add children, save a step and move the parent to waiting-children with its lock token, using WaitingChildrenError as a control transition. The three operations are distinct awaited calls; no application side-effect transaction is introduced. Additional tests assert pending/failed-child guards prevent completion, propagated deferred failure reaches parent/grandparent, and an already thrown parent error remains its last error. Many waits are resolve-only async event listeners; assertion rejection is not explicitly wired to the enclosing promise in all of them, so failure can surface as runner-level rejection/timeout rather than the awaited assertion path. No runtime hang or test failure was measured. Current read ends within the deferred-failure/rate-limit test; its outcome is not inferred until its remaining body is read.
# Flow suite complete through6630: negative oracles and cleanup meaning

Remaining general-suite1751–6417 has now been read in full bounded contiguous outputs, joining the previously reviewed head/tail; the entire6630-line file is complete. Deferred-failure/rate-limit test expects no limiter key (`TTL=-2`) and completion of two deferred failures within5seconds despite a10second normal limit; this is an explicit source oracle for failure bookkeeping not consuming the ordinary rate budget. It is not an executed throughput measurement.

Priority tests delay or disable autorun to establish ordering among ready jobs; chain/delay tests inspect dependency results and delayed states. The backoff test described as fixed actually configures a custom strategy and checks eventual progress without a minimum elapsed retry interval. Re-adding retained custom IDs tests attaching existing active/completed children and rejects changing an existing parent. Removing the parent record and recreating its ID may reuse an existing completed child, reinforcing that IDs/dedup retention are not effect-level exactly-once contracts.

Failure propagation is asserted from waiting-children, delayed and prioritized parent states, with separate remove/ignore policies on ancestors. `continueParentOnFailure` permits parent processing with other unprocessed siblings and retained failure details. The test named “another one fails afterwards” at3859–3947 flags two children but throws only when `job.id === childToFail.id` for the first chosen child; it does not actually produce the second failure its title describes. No unsupported second-failure success is inferred. The baseline “should not process parent if child fails” test5008–5055 starts no parent worker and asserts waiting-count zero; it checks enqueue state, not a worker's refusal to execute a claim attempt.

Observation bounds need a qualification to the producer review: the `maxChildren` value is forwarded as scan count, not enforced by slicing returned children. The partial-tree test4320–4341 expects at least2, not at most2, and pagination tests4155–4166 allow at least50 in the first scan. Thus these are requested observation parameters, not hard memory/returned-child caps. Depth-positive normal cases are tested; zero/negative/cyclic cases are not covered in this suite.

Retention/cleanup oracles differ by operation: parent auto-removal also empties its processed dependency data; `removeUnprocessedChildren` preserves active/completed/failed jobs; ordinary parent removal deletes descendants by default, while `removeChildren:false` leaves them. A locked descendant makes tree removal reject with all three tested job states unchanged. Deleting an intermediate node releases a grandparent to waiting rather than canceling it. The “not grandparent” test reads the grandparent ID through the parent's queue at5812–5817, so that particular undefined lookup is not proof of grandparent deletion; its correct tree-state assertion5835 expects waiting. Retry tests reinsert failed/ignored/completed children into unprocessed dependencies, including regression context for hash-versus-sorted-set WRONGTYPE handling. These source oracles still need exact remove/retry helper closure and actual datastore execution before runtime guarantees.

One retry regression registers the failed observer before `waitUntilReady` with an explicit race explanation6103–6108, confirming the suite authors recognize event-registration ordering. Other paths register a parent completion observer only after awaiting child retry, leaving a narrower observer timing concern. Most cleanup is success-path rather than universal `finally`; one unprocessed-removal test5448–5454 does use `finally`. No test was run and no live cleanup command was issued.
# Removal/retry enforcement closure and partial-admission recovery

Full direct Lua reads: removeJob45, removeUnprocessedChildren32, reprocessJob75; helpers isJobSchedulerJob16, isLocked35, removeJobWithChildren97, moveChildBackToParentDependencies21, destructureJobKey13, removeDeduplicationKeyIfNeededOnRemoval19, removeJobFromAnyState36, removeJobKeys9, removeParentDependencyKey91, addJobInTargetList12, addJobWithPriority15, getOrSetMaxEvents12, isQueuePausedOrMaxed19, addBaseMarkerIfNeeded10 and getPriorityScore9. Already-complete helpers were revisited without treating repeat reading as extra files. Job590–635/backend970–1020 close the remove caller/error path.

Ordinary remove first rejects the current scheduled repeat job, then recursively checks outstanding dependency locks before deleting. Lua returns0 on a lock, and Job.remove turns that into an exception; negative scheduler codes are translated by the backend. This lock check is existence-based, not an operator credential check. Removal of job keys includes logs and dependency/result/failure collections; an absent job can still return the command's success code without a removed stream entry. A removed event therefore is not interchangeable with the integer caller result.

Recursive removal traverses processed, ignored-failure hash, unsuccessful set and pending dependencies; unprocessed removal skips processed collections, ignores locked individual jobs and preserves failed jobs. Its void return provides no per-child proof that all work stopped. Dedup cleanup checks current mapped job ownership before deleting its key and pending-next data, preventing this particular stale-owner cleanup. Parent dependency removal can release the final dependency's parent into waiting, which may initiate more work instead of quiescing the whole graph. No recursive resource/cycle bound appears in these helpers; input graph trust remains material.

Retry requires the job key and expected terminal sorted-set membership, clears terminal timestamps/result or failure, optionally resets attempt counters and enqueues by priority. It then moves a child's processed/failed reference back to dependencies only if the parent exists. The helper explicitly does not move a parent that already left waiting-children back into that state. Retrying a child is therefore not a guarantee the parent will wait again; application reconciliation must consider parent state and effects already performed. Event streams use approximate MAXLEN in most shown paths; removeParentDependencyKey's waiting event has no local MAXLEN argument. No universal event-retention cap follows from a single configured default.

Scope of atomicity correction requested by Rowan8266: no automatic compensation appears in FlowProducer.add/addBulk or backend.addFlow after inspecting returned command tuples. Pre-submission recursive/job validation and one MULTI submission differ from all-root success or rollback of per-command failures. Single add reports its root error; bulk can return both constructed roots while one is absent. The completed upstream contrary test is sufficient source evidence; no Redis launch just to repeat it. A permitted recovery design would record intended roots/IDs, inspect persisted states, reconcile missing/accepted work and use supported deletion only where locks/state allow; these methods alone do not cancel active tasks or compensate external effects. The exact scope and evidence now also appear in findings.md.
# Recurring-job scheduler: stored identity, collision control and removal

Complete source: JobScheduler585; add203/update115/remove44/get20 Lua commands; getJobSchedulerEveryNextMillis49/storeJobScheduler67/addJobFromScheduler20/isQueuePaused8 helpers. Previously reviewed enqueue/store/delay/removal dependencies were revisited to close the path. Complete Redis-specific scheduler suite551 and stress suite577. General scheduler suite remains unread. Queue upsert460–510/remove615–640 were inspected as caller fragments; full Queue review follows. No scheduler runtime or Redis mutation occurred.

JS validates mutually exclusive interval/pattern and immediately/startDate, iteration limit and end date, then uses the injected/default cron strategy or Lua interval computation. Cron parsing occurs before the try that swallows next-iteration exhaustion; invalid parse is not the same as exhaustion. Actual cron-parser semantics/version remain an exact external dependency to review. For intervals, Lua skips missed slots using offset-aware alignment; it does not replay every missed historical iteration. No distinct worker workload identity is enrolled by schedule ID or producer ID.

Update Lua requires scheduler membership and `producerId == repeat:<schedulerId>:<previousMillis>` before advancing; it does not check the processor lock token. That is duplicate-generation/state fencing within a trusted datastore client, not per-worker authentication. Existing next-job keys emit duplicated rather than adding again. Override upsert can replace queued/delayed/prioritized prior iterations but does not remove active/completed/failed ones. Interval collisions scan at most32 subsequent slots and return a busy code when all are occupied; pattern collision returns a separate code. Backend converts those negative codes into thrown errors. The Redis collision tests intentionally create bare existing job hashes; despite one title mentioning concurrent access, they are deterministic collision simulations, not concurrent two-client races.

Scheduler removal deletes scheduler membership/hash and deletes the recorded next job only if it was found in the delayed set. Already waiting/prioritized/active jobs are not removed by that command. Subsequent update stops when membership is absent, but removing future production is not current-job cancellation or worker revocation. Redis suite removes a scheduler while an old job is stalled, verifies deferred failure after the stall limit, and gives a new worker a processor that explicitly rejects if invoked; this is a stronger source oracle for processor non-entry in that one failure path. Legacy migration-error fixture expects the current job still returned while the next-iteration error is emitted, separating observability from stopping current processing.

Listing schedulers has a write side effect: missing nonlegacy metadata triggers removal of dangling membership via getSchedulerData. Redis tests assert the count drops from1 to0 after listing. Legacy-shaped keys instead cause migration errors; isJobScheduler uses the metadata `ic` discriminator rather than colon count alone. None of these identity strings is an account/tenant ACL. Parsed templates are trusted stored JSON; no per-listing schema/resource validation is added here.

Stress evidence qualifications: the100-upsert settings test is skipped. The rapid-upsert test attaches its duplicate-event observer only after all five upserts and a4second delay, so zero duplicates in its later1second window does not prove none occurred during admission. Update-data test permits transition jobs by skipping two observations and checks eventual stored template/next-job state; it does not guarantee an instantaneous global cutover. First-start timing starts after the upsert has returned, measuring the remaining wait rather than full caller latency. Most cleanup errors are swallowed, so successful suite completion would not independently prove cleanup. The disconnect test deliberately disconnects/reconnects its connection and checks eventual five completions and one delayed job; it was read, not run on this PC.
# Queue administrative caller completed

Full Queue1–989 reviewed after the selected scheduler fragments. Queue.remove returns the backend's numeric0 for a lock, whereas Job.remove translates0 into an exception; recovery must respect the exact caller's result shape. Scheduler remove translates Lua0-success to boolean true. Queue.close closes its backend; pause permits current processing to finish. Drain defaults to preserving delayed jobs, and does not remove active/completed/failed work. These are separate administrative actions, not one universal kill switch.

Metadata initialization catches its rejection; the comment says errors may be heard via an error listener, but this catch itself does not emit one or rethrow, so an initialized promise alone does not prove metadata write success. The scheduler getter wraps an async executor with only resolve; an exception constructing its JobScheduler could reject the executor's discarded promise and leave the getter pending. This is an exceptional source-only path, not a measured reachable normal failure. Global rate/concurrency setters directly store supplied values without local numeric-range validation in this layer; actual enforcement is in backend/Lua paths, not in method names.

Clean computes one fixed per-call batch size before looping. With a finite non-multiple limit, a final full batch can exceed the requested total limit because the remaining allowance is not passed down; this is a source hypothesis requiring a bounded backend oracle, not an observed deletion. Retry/promote/obliterate loops await successive backend calls but contain no local deadline/cancellation option. Obliterate first pauses and can fail afterwards without restoring prior pause state. These routines were read only; no deletion/production mutation was performed. Logs can be unlimited when keepLogs0; stream trimming explicitly approximate. Queue interface permits arbitrary serializable data and telemetry; TypeScript payload types do not add runtime tenant validation.
# General scheduler suite completed: what its assertions establish

Complete3687-line general scheduler suite read in bounded contiguous outputs. It covers explicit error forwarding, invalid-option rejection, endDate/count limits, concurrent same-ID upserts, active-job schedule updates, interval reset-to-immediate, offset alignment including missed slots, start dates/time zones, custom RRULE strategy and removal/re-addition. Fake wall clock and often stubbed worker delays make these scheduling-state oracles; they are not real-time service reliability measurements. Some day/month cases disable lock renewal/stall checks to avoid simulated elapsed time confusing leases, so they cannot jointly validate scheduling and production lease survival.

The fixture demonstrates next iteration is scheduled at pickup, before processing completes, and quiesces a worker before sampling delayed count1459–1470 to avoid an acknowledged transition window. This distinguishes state snapshots during an active loop from settled inventory. Same-ID concurrent upsert tests inspect one scheduler/job ID, not per-account authorization or unlimited concurrency. Offset regression comments supply historical context, while assertions cover concrete next timestamps; no history beyond these pinned comments was independently verified here.

Several negative claims are narrower than titles. The mocked scheduler-failure test2770–2864 gathers delayed events/processed IDs but asserts only error messages and update-attempt counts, not a delayed state or backoff timing. The regular-versus-repeatable-stall test2970–3086 creates no regular job or forced regular stalls, accepts delayed checks only if events exist, and computes delay values without asserting them. It establishes expected error observation in its constructed source, not regular-job failure or exponential backoff. The infinite-retry test2866–2968 waits for three stalled events and checks not-failed only if the job still exists; a finite observation cannot prove infinite liveness. Earlier Redis-specific removed-scheduler test has the stronger explicit processor-must-not-run oracle for its own path.

The named-removal test2146–2197 comments out boolean-return and delayed-empty assertions; rejecting an already resolved promise on a later extra run would not invalidate the completed wait. Other removal tests separately assert true/false on first/second removal and inspect delayed absence. Individual current scheduler jobs reject ordinary Job.remove. General drain/clean tests explicitly preserve a scheduler's current iteration, including a cron-delayed job after `drain(true)`3571–3617. Thus administrative drain's broad comment is subject to scheduler exemptions in the backend; it must not be presented as a blanket stop for recurring work. Exact drain/clean Lua closure is the next bounded review.

One paused-upsert test775–801 calls itself removal but expects waiting count still1 after changing the schedule, unlike the unpaused test expecting0. Do not infer every paused-state replacement is removed merely from the test title; the exact list-routing/observation path needs inspection. Some assertions compare timestamps from locally parsed dates without explicit timezone, others use explicit offsets; portability depends on the test environment and imported calendar libraries. None was executed here.
# Administrative cleanup closure and caller-bound validation

Complete Lua commands: drain42, cleanJobsInSet60, moveJobsToWait77, obliterate127; helpers cleanList50/cleanSet59/removeListJobs24/removeZSetJobs22/filterOutJobsToIgnore14/removeJobs14/getZSetItems8/getJobsInZset14/getTimestamp20/batches19. Loader379–405 revisited: includes interpolate at their reference positions, preserving the outer `local limit` declaration before cleanList; no free-global-limit bug is claimed. Backend1230–1265/1755–1805/1900–1930 revisited for exact arguments/results.

Drain builds a current scheduler-ID/slot map and excludes those IDs from ordinary waiting/paused/delayed/prioritized removal. Sorted-set removal removes selected IDs explicitly. List removal instead filters the selected IDs and then trims the original list by the filtered count. For an original list `[scheduled, ordinary]`, exclusion yields `[ordinary]`, deletes ordinary job data, then `LTRIM1..-1` would discard scheduled's list entry and leave ordinary's stale entry. That is a source-level mixed-list correctness concern; no Redis/Lua runtime was run, and it does not erase the separately observed intended scheduler-exemption oracle. Exact key ordering/fixtures matter. This requires a minimal Redis regression fixture if an isolated runtime becomes available; do not mark the intended exemption universally enforced.

Clean's list path checks active locks unless treating a waiting list, excludes current scheduler jobs, chooses first present finished/processed/submitted timestamp and removes entries eligible by grace. Set cleanup passes scheduler exclusions only for nonterminal states. Raw scan candidates are limited before later eligibility filters; a short result can stop the JS loop even if additional eligible records lie beyond skipped candidates. This is a distinct source-only completeness question, not evidence of a deletion count in production. Large set/list enumeration and recursive parent removal are not globally bounded merely because variadic commands split into7000-item batches.

The caller total-limit hypothesis is now validated at its exact method boundary. [Plan](queue-clean-contract-plan.md), [fixture](queue-clean-contract-lab.mjs) and immutable [four-check receipt](queue-clean-contract-receipt-1792272518208.json) extract only the hash-checked Queue.clean method and strip TypeScript with Node's built-in transformer. An inert backend returns fabricated IDs. Limit15000 sends two unchanged10000 batches and returns20000 IDs; limit10000 makes one call; waiting normalizes to wait and each batch emits cleaned. All promises settled; no timers/services/datastore/deletion. This replaces the earlier source-only qualification for caller accounting, while actual Lua/store deletion remains untested. A remaining-budget argument is the corresponding remediation/regression contract.

Bulk retry/promote use moveJobsToWait, which explicitly does not support priority routing, clears terminal result/failure fields and restores dependency references before LPUSH. It preserves attempts unless another path resets them; individual reprocess supports priority and optional counter resets. Administrative retry is not identical to retrying one job. Obliterate requires paused state and rejects active jobs unless force, but force deletes lock/data rather than terminating processor code. It works through listed state structures, and its final key list is not a universal scan of all possible prefixed extension keys. Its “leaving no trace” comment must not imply application logs, external effects or retained orphan/custom keys were erased. No destructive operation was executed.
# Queue observation is not worker enrollment or an atomic audit snapshot

Complete QueueGetters1–740. Worker discovery matches mutable Redis client names, not authenticated workload certificates or enrolled worker records. For cluster replies it selects the single node with most matching clients, not a cross-node deduplicated union. On the recognized unsupported-client-command error it returns a one-element explanatory placeholder, and getWorkersCount simply takes array length: unsupported discovery can therefore look like count1. This is a source path, not an actual GCP/service result. Do not treat this endpoint as an authoritative active-worker/account inventory.

Counts and job reads are distinct operations. `getJobs` gets IDs/data through a Redis-specialized path or backend ranges, then fetches jobs again concurrently and drops missing jobs. Snapshot/state changes between calls are not fenced in this layer; a list response need not agree exactly with a separate count. getWaitingCount delegates waiting alias handling to the backend, whose exact Lua path is next. Count missing responses map to0, while malformed metric points coerce to0; telemetry absence/invalid data can be indistinguishable from measured zero without another quality signal.

Prometheus label values escape backslashes/quotes/newlines, but caller-provided label names are inserted directly and duplicate reserved label names are not rejected here. This is trusted configuration, not an observed remote-input attack. Metrics collect counts and completion/failure summaries at separate awaits, and no endpoint authentication is supplied by an exporter that merely returns a string. Job data/log/dependency getters similarly require an application/API authorization boundary; queue prefix and ID selection alone do not authorize reads. Default end=-1 allows full historical lists in several getters, so dashboard callers need their own pagination/resource budgets.
# Redis observation/cleanup suites and contrary deletion evidence

Complete getters.redis89, clean.redis300, obliterate.redis444 and getCounts Lua37. Backend1290–1400/2005–2040 revisited. Getter reconnect test expects discovery count1→0→1 around blocking-client disconnection, reinforcing that this is connection observation rather than worker/process lifetime. Its10/20ms sleeps are scenario timing, not a proven reconnection SLA. No socket was opened here.

Orphan cleanup tests assert removal of hashes and known subkeys, including orphan lock-only entries; preserve wait/delayed/prioritized jobs, metadata/repeat/dedup keys and retained custom IDs; scan150 orphans with small scan count. The case titled “across all states” actually creates only wait/delayed/prioritized states. This source oracle does not settle active/completed/failed/waiting-children coexistence or a concurrent state transition. The missing-timestamp cleanup uses a100ms sleep before asserting both jobs failed; no deterministic failure barrier is present.

Obliterate's own cross-queue-parent case206–246 explicitly expects three queue-prefixed keys and one waiting-children job to remain after the operation. This is direct intended contrary evidence to the command's universal “leaving no trace” comment: a parent with pending children in another queue can remain. Child-queue obliteration releases a parent in another queue to waiting, confirmed by tests249–344. Queue deletion need not stop its larger flow; it can make another queue eligible to run. Tests also cover empty/past-job queues, scheduler records and a2223-job mixed-state fixture with keyspace checks. These are source oracles, not executed keyspace measurements.

The force-active test348–378 waits for a designated job to finish before forcing deletion and does not assert a processor is active at deletion time or stopped afterwards. A zero-key assertion cannot prove processor termination. General active-guard tests remain separate unread work. getCounts performs a legacy marker cleanup RPOP for some wait/paused list counts, so this apparent read can mutate deprecated marker state. An observation API name alone is insufficient to classify it as mutation-free.
# General cleanup test closure

Complete clean938 and obliterate109 suites. The general orphan suite adds the state coverage absent from the Redis-only mixed fixture: active/completed/failed/delayed/prioritized/waiting-children survivors, plus real wait job retrieval. Active survival uses an explicit processor-start promise and2second delay, but not a latch holding the job until the cleanup returns; do not promote this into proof under arbitrary backend latency. The completed/failed cases assert removed-count0 rather than all retained data. Source claims are now supported by both suites, still without execution.

Cleaner tests cover grace windows, exact small limits, wait/waiting alias and cleaned event normalization, scheduler future iteration retention while terminal iterations are cleaned, cross-queue parent release and an active parent surviving failed-child cleanup. The failed-parent lookup450–451 forgets await and therefore checks a Promise is defined, not persisted job presence; its separate `getState()==failed` assertion is real and must not be discarded with the weak lookup. No non-multiple limit above10000 occurs here, leaving the newly validated caller-bound regression uncovered by this suite.

General obliterate active guard expects an exception after a designated job has completed while a later250ms job should still be active; it uses timing rather than an explicit active latch. The job-log case checks emptied logs after completion, not cancellation. The suites do not prove a universal no-effects-after-delete guarantee, and no deletion was performed here. Queue cleanup used by all these tests is an imported dependency whose exact scope is next to inspect, not assumed safe merely from the name.
# Test-target selection, backend overrides and actual configuration reachability

Full source reads: cleanupQueue33, connectionFactory35, create-backend145, Redis/node/Glide/Bun setup28/19/16/18, Postgres setup70/global setup25, all eight Vitest configs81/27/18/21/46/28/52/81 plus adapter conformance22 and Postgres URL18. Full supporting BullMQ package.json250. Main read ledger358 complete at this checkpoint; supporting20 complete of543. No test configuration was imported/executed.

Factories select explicit options, then environment host/port, then localhost6379; Postgres URL has a local fixture default but an environment override. The researcher did not read actual environment values or use them. Merely choosing a named suite does not establish an isolated target. cleanupQueue creates a fresh client, calls forced obliterate and finally closes Queue. The Redis factory leaves caller-supplied clients open by design, while duplicating blocking clients it owns; caller/connection ownership and queue close must be assessed separately. Exact close path follows before calling this a leaked socket.

The Postgres shared-suite setup changes the process-wide backend factory and passes an inert Redis-shaped compatibility object, with Postgres pool max1 to serialize inserts. Its comments explicitly distinguish this test ordering from production pools. The global setup drops the configured schema; it is not a read-only preflight. However, the pinned vitest.postgres.config and smoke config both include only `tests/postgres/**/*.test.ts` and reference neither that setup nor global setup. Package test:postgres selects this dedicated config. Repository search found no active references to the two shared setup filenames. Thus their existence does not prove shared suites run on SQL or that the schema drop executes under the declared command; claims about configured behavior must follow imports, not comments. Any other invocation/CI wiring remains separate to inspect.

The Bun config excludes scheduler stress for a stated hang/adapter limitation and child-pool Node-specific tests; Glide excludes its own client tests from the broad suite. Coverage excludes SQL backend and some adapter/child-process entrypoints; thresholds are declared goals, not measured correctness or full-driver coverage. no-file-parallelism is present in package scripts, while per-config concurrent:false concerns test sequencing; neither is workload authorization.

Declared BullMQ runtime dependencies are exact versions cron-parser5.10.2/msgpackr2.1.0/node-abort-controller3.1.1/semver7.8.5/tslib2.8.1. Optional peer ranges allow multiple driver versions; dev declarations pin ioredis5.11.1/redis6.3.0/pg8.23.1/rrule2.8.1/Vitest4.1.11. Yarn1.22.22 and override versions are declared, but no installed resolved graph/source review is implied. pretest deletes/regenerates script sources and builds SQL artifacts; build/release scripts include mutations/publication. None was run. Package version6.3.16 is this pinned source declaration, not a claim about latest published release.
# Connection close ownership and ioredis adaptation

Complete ioredis-client440, its test234 and create-backend test96. RedisConnection750–815 revisited. Shared close skips quit/disconnect, removes this wrapper's listeners and marks the wrapper closed. Backend ownership tests explicitly expect a nonblocking supplied client to remain ready even after forced close and call connection.quit separately in finally. cleanupQueue creates a fresh supplied client and never quits that client explicitly; the source path therefore leaves its caller-owned connection open. This is a source-established lifecycle omission, not an observed socket leak/process hang on this PC. Ordinary queue close preserving a genuinely externally managed shared client is intentional; the omission is the helper losing that caller responsibility.

The ioredis proxy caches wrappers by raw object, preserves prototype method identity, translates structured commands to driver argument forms and rewraps duplicate clients. Cluster duplication merges existing redisOptions, including public fixture password, rather than creating a new credential/principal. Cache/duplicate identity here concerns JavaScript objects and connection ownership, not per-worker account separation. Structural `isIRedisClient` checks and an adapter marker are compatibility detection, not authentication. Unknown properties are forwarded; non-override writes go through to the raw client, whereas override spies remain in the override table. The no-mutation test checks initial wrapping, not a claim that all later proxy writes are isolated. Transaction augmentation intentionally adds/replaces methods on the returned transaction object.

Pure fake-driver duplicate tests verify option routing; other tests instantiate real ioredis, with lazyConnect for two identity cases and explicit quit/disconnect cleanup. The raw native-driver backend test verifies distinct blocking duplicates and caller listener counts, then runs one job; it does not establish distinct accounts or device identities. None of those upstream tests was run. External ioredis5.11.1 implementation/transport semantics remain separate exact dependency work.
